                                Preface

TC 6-0.2 Training the Command and Control Warfighting Function for Battalions and Brigades, is a
guide for commanders of units with staffs and command posts (CPs) to use during unit training
management and planning. This TC provides the framework for commanders, leaders, and Soldiers who
prioritize, plan, prepare, execute, and assess training for command and control (C2). The products
described in this publication are developed to support C2 training at each echelon. Proficiency for each
task in these tables is established in AR 350-1 and is further explained in FM 7-0. The three possible
proficiency ratings for collective tasks are Fully Trained (T), Practiced (P), and Untrained (U). This
publication is written to include four training audiences: commanders, staffs, CP personnel and the
digital crew. In addition to the training aspects of this publication, educators throughout the Army will
find significant value using this TC as a reference for C2 and training management topics.

Commanders, staffs, and subordinates ensure that their decisions and actions comply with applicable
United States, international, and in some cases host-nation laws and regulations. Commanders at all
levels ensure that their Soldiers operate in accordance with the law of war and the rules of engagement.
(Refer to FM 6-27, The Commander’s Handbook on the Law of Land Warfare, for legal compliance.)

TC 6-0.2 uses joint terms where applicable. Selected joint and Army terms and definitions appear in both
the glossary and the text. TC 6-0.2 is not the proponent for any Army doctrinal terms. When first defining
other proponent definitions in the text, the term is italicized, and the number of the proponent publication
follows the definition. Subsequent uses of the term are not italicized.

TC 6-0.2 applies to all components of the United States Army: The Active Army (component (COMPO)
1), the Army National Guard/Army National Guard of the United States (COMPO 2), and the United
States Army Reserve (COMPO 3) unless otherwise stated.

The proponent of TC 6-0.2 is the U.S. Army Mission Command Center of Excellence (MCCOE). The
preparing agency is the Directorate of Training, U.S. Army MCCOE, Combined Arms Center, Fort
Leavenworth, KS. Send comments and recommendations on DA Form 2028 (Recommended Changes
to Publications and Blank Forms), U.S. Army Mission Command Center of Excellence, ATTN: DOT
MCCOE, Bldg. 472, Room 313, 310 McPherson Ave., Ft. Leavenworth, KS 66027; or submit an
electronic DA Form 2028 to usarmy.leavenworth.tradoc.list.mission-command-coe-dot-ted@army.mil.

30 July 2025  TC 6-0.2  ix

This page intentionally left blank.

                            Introduction

This publication is updated with the C2 warfighting function (WFF), whereas the previous version was
written when doctrine still referred to the mission command WFF. The command and control warfighting
function (C2 WFF) is the related tasks and a system that enable commanders to exercise authority
and direction to accomplish missions (ADP 3-0). The Army defines mission command (MC) as the
Army’s approach to C2 that empowers subordinate decision making and decentralized execution
appropriate to the situation (ADP 6-0). To assist in commanding forces and controlling operations,
commanders, at every echelon, establish a C2 system, which is the arrangement of people, processes,
networks, and command posts (CPs) that enable commanders to conduct operations (ADP 6-0).
Effective commanders use this publication to increase the state of readiness of their C2 system to ensure
their units are prepared to conduct large-scale combat operations (LSCO).

This publication describes the training pathway for the commander and staff as they implement the
C2 system. Figure Introduction-1 illustrates the exercise of C2. This publication highlights the training
pathway for the commander, the staff, the CPs, and the digital crew.

30 July 2025  TC 6-0.2  xi


---

Introduction

              Figure Introduction-1. The Exercise of Command and Control (ADP 6-0)

     To assist commanders in building and maintaining their overall C2 and unit readiness, this TC provides
     C2 training tables (C2TTs). The C2TTs are a series of task based tables for training a unit’s commander,
     staff, all CPs, and digital crews on individual and collective task training objectives with clear inputs
     and outputs. The C2TTs are designed to function and be applied like those of typical gunnery tables
     that commanders use to certify and validate tank or fighting vehicle crews. The tables are divided into
     the four distinct categories: the commander, the staff, the CP, and the digital crew.

xii           TC 6-0.2  30 July 2025


---

                                        Command and Control Warfighting Function Training Tables – Battalions

     This publication provides information about the Army’s approach to training the C2 WFF and
     highlights several training considerations and enablers that, when implemented, will improve unit
     readiness.

     Chapter 1, Command and Control Warfighting Function Training Tables – Battalions, describes
     battalion- level command and control training and the training tables for each cohort.

     Chapter 2, Command and Control Warfighting Function Training Tables – Brigades, describes brigade-
     level C2 training and the C2TTs for each cohort for brigade combat teams and functional and
     multifunctional brigades.

     Each chapter is structured with five sections: Chapter introduction, commander training tables, staff
     training tables, command post training tables, and digital crew training tables.

     Appendix A, Command and Control Training Management, describes how the C2TTs should be
     integrated into the unit’s training management process and products.

     Appendix B, Large-Scale Combat Operations and Multidomain Considerations for Command and
     Control Training, describes how factors of the modern operational environment (OE) impact the
     command post system and how to account for them in training.

     Appendix C, Battle Rhythm Training Considerations for Large-Scale Combat Operations, describes the
     unit battle rhythm and considerations for training its implementation.

     Appendix D, Command and Control Training Resources, lists the training products – individual
     tasks, collective tasks, drills, training support packages, and more – that are referenced in the body of the
     TC.

     Appendix E, Training Tables Quick Reference, provides convenient roll-ups of the tables for each of the
     four training audiences for battalion and brigade.

     One noteworthy change since this training circular (TC) was last printed is that the title for the duty
     position for a key leader in training the digital crew has changed. The Mission Command Digital Master
     Gunner (MCDMG or DMG), formerly holder of the 5C additional skill identifier (ASI), is now known
     as the C2 Information Integrator. For simplicity, this publication sometimes uses ‘information
     integrator’ to identify Soldiers who lead the digital crew.

SUPPORTING TRAINING REFERENCES

     The C2TTs are built using individual tasks, collective tasks, drills, and training support packages (TSPs).
     A training and evaluation outline (T&EO) is a summary document that provides information on individual
     or collective task training objectives, resource requirements, and evaluation procedures (FM 7-0). The
     T&EOs for these ‘building blocks’ of the C2TTs are not included in this TC, but may be downloaded
     from the Army Training Network (ATN <https://atn.army.mil/>), or from the Central Army Registry
     (CAR <https://rdl.train.army.mil/catalog/dashboard>) using a common access card (CAC). Authorized
     CAC users access the CAR and find specific T&EOs by locating the empty “search the CAR field” and
     entering either the T&EO number, T&EO title, or both, and then clicking on the magnifying glass search
     icon. If the actual T&EO number is unknown, then a search can still be made using the title. Once the
     T&EO number or title or both have been entered in the search field, tasks with similar titles, or task
     numbers, will also populate. The user then scrolls down the listing to find the requested T&EO.

     The first set of digits in a T&EO number identifies whether the task is an individual or a collective task
     by. Two-digit numbers identify the task as collective, whereas a three-digit number indicates that the
     task is an individual task. Additionally, these initial numbers indicate which Proponent developed and
     manages the task. The echelon or task type is identified by the middle set of letters. For example, BN
     for battalion, or LDR for leader. The last set of numbers identifies the specific task. To illustrate, the
     T&EO number for “Conduct the Operations Process for Command and Control” is 71-BN-5100. The
     initial number “71” identifies this task as collective and that it was developed by the MCCOE. The middle
     set of letters “BN” identifies this task as a battalion echelon task. The last set of numbers connects the type
     of task (such as collective or individual) to the task title “Conduct the Operations Process for Command
     and Control”.

30 July 2025  TC 6-0.2  xiii


---

Introduction

     The T&EOs provide the conditions, standards, and performance measures that describe how and under
     what circumstances the tasks listed therein are performed. Refer to Appendix D for a cumulative listing
     of tasks and drills that are identified within this publication.

     The MCCOE has developed other training materials that can assist the commander in setting training
     conditions and conducting the training reflected in these tables, including:

          • Warfighter Training Support Packages (WTSPs). A WTSP is a complete, detailed,
                exportable package integrating training products, materials, and information necessary to
                support operating force training. WTSPs provide the actual details for securing the materials,
                training venues, and other necessary resources identified in each Unit CATS training event
                supporting the Standard METL for designated units. (see Appendix D for instructions on
                downloading training support packages):
                 WTSP-71-TS-BDE201 - Displace the Command Post for Brigades.
                 WTSP-71-TS-BDE201 (Europe) - Displace the Command Post for BCTs Warfighter
                     Training Support Package.

          • Individual Training Support Packages (ITSPs) that commanders can use for self-study or to
                train their key staff and subordinate unit commanders on the following:
                 ITSP 150-C2-6000/1.0 – Command Post Survivability.
                 ITSP 150-C2-5115 / 1.0 – Wargaming.
                 ITSP 150-DIV-0006/ 2.0 (China) - The Division Commander Drives the Operations
                     Process (China).
                 ITSP 150-DIV-0006 / 2.0 (Russia) - The Division Commander Drives the Operations
                     Process ITSP (Russia).
                 ITSP 150-C2-5104 / 1.0 - Conduct Plans-to-FUOPS-to-CUOPS Transition.

          • Other C2 training supporting materials are available from the MCCOE Directorate of Training
                (DOT) MS Teams page using Teams code 2wqy4u7.

     Units can contact the MCCOE DOT at the following address with questions or suggestions regarding
     the C2TTs or any of the supporting training materials: usarmy.leavenworth.tradoc.list.mission-
     command-coe-dot-ted@army.mil.

COMMAND AND CONTROL INFORMATION SYSTEMS (C2IS)

     This TC uses the term and abbreviation command and control information system (C2IS) to mean
     computer systems and software designed to assist Army units in commanding forces and controlling
     operations or specific aspects thereof (e.g. a specific WFF). As of this writing, C2IS in use by Army
     units and trained at mission training complexes (MTCs) includes:

          • AFATDS: Advanced Field Artillery Tactical Data Systems
          • AMDWS: Air and Missile Defense Workstation
          • ATAK: Android Tactical Assault Kit
          • CPCE: Command Post Computing Environment
          • CPOF: Command Post of the Future
          • GCCS: A- Global Command and Control System–Army
          • GCCS-A/JC2: Global Command and Control System-Army/Joint Command and Control
          • DCGS-A: Distributed Common Ground System-Army
          • GCCS–J: Global Command and Control System–Joint
          • JADC2: Joint All Domain Command and Control
          • JBC-P: Joint Battle Command – Platform (runs Mounted Mission Command (MMC) software.
          • TAK: Tactical Assault Kit
          • TAIS: Tactical Airspace Integration System
          • Various servers that support the C2ISto include:

                 GCCS-A server
                 Intel Fusion Server (IFS) for DCGS-A
                 Tactical Server Infrastructure (TSI)
          • WINTAK: Windows Tactical Assault Kit

xiv  TC 6-0.2  30 July 2025

                                               Chapter 1

  Command and Control Warfighting Function Training
                           Tables – Battalions

    This chapter describes training tables for Army battalions, squadrons, and similar units
    with two or more company-sized subordinate units, commanded by lieutenant colonels that
    have staffs (typically S-1 through S-4 and S-6) and one or more command posts (CPs).
    The command and control training tables (C2TTs) for battalions are designed to develop
    and test the proficiency of four training audiences–commander, staff, CPs, and digital
    crew–at the basic, intermediate, and advanced levels. The tasks in each table are intended
    to replicate training under realistic conditions against threats in all domains (air, land,
    maritime, space, and cyberspace) in the operational environment (OE). The C2TTs are
    designed to be executed in conjunction with training events listed in the unit’s Combined
    Arms Training Strategy (CATS). Progression through all tables will reinforce perishable
    skills and refine organizational processes and procedures. This chapter initially discusses
    considerations and tasks the commander uses to be an effective commander. This is
    followed by the staff training tables that reflect how the staff supports the commander. The
    chapter then covers the tasks involved with training CP personnel and how they support
    the commander. The chapter concludes with training the digital crew and how the digital
    crew supports the commander and staff.

SECTION I – INTRODUCTION TO CHAPTER 1

     1-1. Section I discusses the overview and scope for chapter 1.

OVERVIEW

     1-2. Chapter 1 assists battalion commanders in verifying proficiency in collective and individual training
     through a certification and validation process. The unit progresses through a common, standard, and
     commander-driven certification of the unit commander, staff, CPs, and digital crews. Validation of the
     unit’s command and control (C2) system occurs through an externally evaluated culminating exercise,
     usually the unit’s External Evaluation (EXEVAL). Refer to Appendix D for a cumulative listing of tasks
     and drills that are identified within this publication.

SCOPE

     1-3. The tables in this chapter apply to Army battalion headquarters. Given the broad variety of battalion-
     sized units, it is likely that some of those units will lack staff structure or equipment to execute selected
     tasks in some of these tables. For example, an Army field support battalion CP is not designed to be
     mobile, or a Forward Engineer Support Team may be designed to deploy with another unit’s
     headquarters, so it wouldn’t necessarily need to secure its command post while conducting C2. In such
     cases, battalion commanders discuss which tasks to perform during each table – and in particular prior
     to Table X Validation Exercise –with their higher headquarters commander.

     1-4. The C2TTs are closely aligned with the battalion level C2 functional CATS. The exercises
     mentioned in the C2TTs are meant to nest with collective training the unit is already performing. A
     commander does not need to schedule a training event outside the scope of the unit CATS solely for
     purpose of completing the C2TTs. However, as resources, time, and other restrictions affect training,
     commanders can extend or repeat any of the tables to enhance unit training, based on their assessment
     of the unit’s proficiency.

     1-5. Given the current Army training guidance of focusing live training at the company level and below,
     battalion commanders will have to seek opportunities to train their staff, CP personnel, and digital crew

30 July 2025  TC 6-0.2  1


---

Chapter 1

     as oversight of company level training. In some cases, battalion-level training may take the form of
     providing stimulation to and reacting to responses from companies conducting company through
     squad/crew level training. Mission training complexes (MTCs) can provide commanders with support
     to command post exercises (CPXs), staff exercises (STAFFEXs), and games for training (GFT).

     1-6. This chapter details all battalion level of C2 warfighting function (WFF) training and certifications
     in detail. Table 1-1 on page 3 summarizes how the C2TTs support C2 training and shows the progressive
     objectives for the battalion. Tables I-V guide the unit through basic proficiency of the listed tasks at the
     cell and section level. The four training audiences will typically train Tables I-V independently. Table
     VI is the certification for each of the training audiences: namely the commander, the staff, CP personnel,
     and the digital crew. Tables VII-VIII reflect advanced task mastery at the headquarters level. Tables IX
     and X provide the framework for validating the C2 WFF proficiency of the battalion headquarters. The
     four training audiences will train together to progress through Tables VI-X.

1-7. Tables I through X for each training audience present a training strategy that supports the
progressive development of C2 WFF proficiency. All subordinate steps list individual and collective task
T&EOs for each table. The tables are designed to support the commander’s training plan through
reinforcing repetitions and sets to achieve and maintain proficiency. The tables and associated individual
and collective tasks should be trained concurrent with training events already on the unit’s CATS – the
battalion should not have to schedule separate events specifically for executing the C2TT. Some CATS
will have the C2TT already integrated into the unit CATS. In other cases, units may have to manually
schedule the C2TT into training events listed on their CATS. See Appendix A for a discussion of how
C2TT are incorporated into unit CATS.

30 July 2025                                                       TC 6-0.2                                                3


---

Chapter 1

     1-8. Tables I through VI are designed to improve and enhance individual skills and to establish limited
     collective task proficiency while the unit progresses towards exercising C2 in the conduct of large-scale
     combat operations (LSCO). They are the ‘crawl’ of C2 training. The different audiences may enter
     training tables I through IV at different points based on different proficiency levels (e.g., the staff
     sections have had more turnover than the digital crew and therefore should start with Table I whereas
     the digital crew could enter at Table III). Similarly, the different audiences may proceed through these
     tables at different paces as long as they all complete Table IV before beginning to work together in Table
     V.

     1-9. Tables I-II are designed to facilitate reoccurring training throughout the year to integrate and build
     proficiency in new personnel as they are assigned to the unit. Tables III-IV are designed to establish staff
     section-level and CP cell-level proficiency prior to the cells’ integration into the staff and unit CP(s).

     1-10. Tables V-VI are structured for commanders to evaluate, assess, and certify their organizations
     while conducting standard mission essential tasks (MET) or assigned mission METs. Tables V and VI
     can be accomplished in the same CPX by designating two to three days for Table V, taking a break for
     AARs and adjusting, then completing the CPX with Table VI.

     1-11. Commanders and staff leaders can choose to run Tables III-IV in a single event or across several
     events. For example, the battalion commander may use a STAFFEX prior to a CPX to exercise the
     headquarters through the first five tables on the first two days of the STAFFEX and then retrain Table
     IV with tougher conditions on the third and fourth day.

     1-12. Table V should be conducted as a CPX under the same conditions the commander will certify the
     staff in Table VI. Leaders conduct section/cell level after action reviews (AARs) to ensure their teams
     are ready for certification.

     1-13. Once staff leaders have certified personnel in all shifts of their respective sections, cells, CPs and
     digital crews on their respective Table V requirements, the unit conducts Table VI simultaneously. The
     commander certifies that the staff, CP personnel, and digital crew are proficient on the Table VI
     collective tasks, and that the various parts of the C2 system are prepared to conduct ‘walk’ level training
     in tables VII and VIII. The brigade commander also certifies the battalion commander during Table VI.
     Table VI should be conducted as a CPX or a field training exercise (FTX) with the C2 system functioning
     during 24-hour operations.

     1-14. Tables VII through X build on unit competency and require inclusion of supporting and supported
     units, augmentees, unified action partners, or liaisons (while most battalions do not usually work with
     liaisons or augmentees, those that do will incorporate them beginning in table VII). Tables VII-IX
     incorporate the full complement of echelon appropriate associated assets, a rigorous and dynamic OE,
     and the stresses typically experienced during capstone training events or deployments.

     1-15. The figures at the beginning of each training table discussion for each training audience summarize
     the key inputs, performance elements, and outputs for each training table. The remainder of this chapter
     lays out the training tables for the four training audiences in greater detail in the following order:

          • The commander training tables.
          • The staff training tables.
          • The CP training tables.
          • The digital crew training tables.

SECTION II – BATTALION COMMANDER TRAINING TABLES

     1-16. Section II discusses the C2TT for the battalion commander.

COMMAND AND CONTROL TRAINING TABLES FOR THE
BATTALION COMMANDER

     1-17. Commanders can use tables I through V for their own professional development and as a resource
     in counseling subordinate commanders. Many of the tasks in the early tables are designed to complement

4          TC 6-0.2  30 July 2025


---

                         Command and Control Warfighting Function Training Tables – Battalions

     the Center for Army Leadership Publication, Army Handbook for Leadership Transitions. Table VI is
     the commander certification training table. Brigade commanders certify battalion commanders on Table
     VI and then, under the counseling and assessment of their evaluating commander (two echelons up),
     battalion commanders validate their proficiency in Table X. For example, the 1st Battalion, 82d Combat
     Aviation Brigade (CAB) commander performs Table X during a battalion FTX with external evaluators
     selected by the CAB commander. Based on the EXEVAL outcomes, discussions with the CAB
     commander, and the EXEVAL AAR the 1/82d CAB commander validates the battalion’s C2 system.

     1-18. Battalion commanders complete Tables I-VI at a pace consistent with the unit’s long range training
     plan, mission variables, and the evaluating commander’s guidance. Once the battalion commanders are
     certified by the brigade commander and they also certify the other audiences in Table VI, the four
     training audiences transition into Tables VII-X together.

                                           Develop Training Strategy

DEVELOP COHESIVE TEAMS THROUGH MUTUAL TRUST

1-20. Team building is an essential ingredient when assembling any organization. However, building the
team is a commander centric responsibility driven by perceptions and perspectives shaped by their
schooling, personal experiences, personality, and military maturity with consideration of the staff’s
knowledge, skills, attributes, and capacity. Within time and resource constraints, the commander
develops the entire team and encourages leadership within the command to conduct team building within
their organizations, sections, cells, working groups, and WFFs. See Individual Task 158-LDR-5001,
Develop Cohesive Teams Through Mutual Trust.

EMPLOY APPLIED CRITICAL THINKING

1-21. Applied critical thinking is the deliberate process of applying tools and methodologies to critically
review problems by asking better questions, deconstructing arguments, examining analogies,
challenging assumptions, and exploring alternatives. During the first weeks of command, commanders
teach subordinates to understand their leadership style and how they best absorb information. The forms
and methods in which they prefer information to be presented can change given the wide-ranging variety

30 July 2025                                        TC 6-0.2                                                                  5


---

Chapter 1

     of responsibilities of the commander. Examples of this include the military decision-making process
     (MDMP), decision briefings, town halls, and budgets. How information is presented to the commander
     affects how they visualize concepts, understand data, and avoid predetermined outcomes. To assist their
     staff in establishing the preferred communication methods, the commander conducts a self-awareness
     assessment and reviews the applied critical thinking performance steps to ensure their subordinates
     understand their tendencies and problem-solving expectations to provide insight into their critical
     thinking methods. Commanders challenge their staff and subordinates to always ask themselves the “so
     what” of any given piece of information received or delivered. As the commander and staff relationship
     matures, the commander conducts a re-assessment of their preferred communication methods to ensure
     the staff is meeting expectations aligned with organizational mission, goals, and growth. Critical thinking
     skills are covered in Individual Task 158-MC-8012, Employ Applied Critical Thinking.

UNDERSTAND UNIT DOCTRINE APPLICATIONS

     1-22. Battalion commanders have a thorough understanding of doctrine and policy. To successfully
     transition from Table I to Table II, the commander demonstrates an application of that understanding in
     the context of their organization. Further, the commander reviews and re-approves, amends, deletes, or
     adds policies and procedures appropriate to the organization that meet their leadership style and adhere
     to the Army Ethic. Commanders also review FM 7-0, regarding how to train their headquarters and the
     force as a whole. Refer to Individual Task 158-LDR-8000, Transition to a New Leadership Position.

ESTABLISH A LEADER PROFESSIONAL DEVELOPMENT PROGRAM

     1-23. Commanders are responsible for establishing a professional leader development program and a
     climate in which learning can take place to build stronger subordinates. Leader development is a
     continuous and purposeful process that can include assigned professional readings, a leader professional
     development program, staff rides, and so on. The battalion commander schedules leader development
     activities that incorporate the mission command approach to C2 and discussions of how they envision
     responsibilities when conducting the operations process. These activities focus on the seven principles
     of mission command as well as the tasks and systems that develop and integrate activities enabling the
     commander to balance the art of command and the science of control. The underlying theme for the
     overall program is to build competency in current and future leaders and to facilitate a deeper
     understanding and practice of the mission command approach to the C2 WFF tasks, and the C2 system
     that enables the successful execution of the C2 tasks. Commanders use Individual Tasks 158-MC-8009,
     Analyze Army Character Development; and 158-MC-8005, Conduct Self Development; and FM 6-22 as
     the basis for their programs.

ASSESS ORGANIZATIONAL EFFICIENCY AND EFFECTIVENESS

     1-24. Organizational design must address structure, processes, interrelationships, and the integration of
     the work effort. This systems approach allows leaders to comprehend the multiple factors that create the
     organizational context. See Army Handbook for Leadership Transitions, Organizational Alignment
     chapter. These activities determine how commanders organize, tailor, or adapt their individual staffs to
     accomplish the mission (FM 6-0).

     1-25. Commanders review their mission essential task list (METL), anticipated mission sets and the
     staff’s capabilities to address and meet impending mission requirements. Commanders also determine
     how the environment impacts the staff's effectiveness and efficiency across the C2 system. Assessing
     the staff's collaboration capability enables commanders to promote unity of effort. Commanders monitor
     each staff element’s function, assign associated duties and responsibilities, and make other adjustments
     as needed and within the commander’s authority to change the organization of their staff. Directing
     changes to the headquarters' physical organization to enhance collaboration and selecting personnel to
     fill key billets may be among the most effective techniques to develop a high functioning staff. For
     example, an engineer commander directs representation from all S-staff sections to occupy a single large
     office space for garrison operations to develop the team and create a learning environment. The
     commander uses Individual Task 150-LDR-5013, Organize the Staff for Operations, and FM 6-0 to
     assist in this effort.

6          TC 6-0.2  30 July 2025


---

                         Command and Control Warfighting Function Training Tables – Battalions

ASSESS CAPABILITIES AND ESTABLISH COMMAND AND CONTROL INFORMATION
SYSTEM (C2IS) REQUIREMENTS

     1-26. Upon assuming command, the new commander considers establishing their C2IS requirements
     early in the process to give the staff maximum time to adjust. Commanders at battalion level typically
     understand between the information systems. Analyze the battalion’s C2IS to identify critical system
     requirements for the mission, current problem set, or OE. Commanders must understand the electro-
     magnetic signature, power generation requirements, and information requirements of their CPs. Further,
     they must understand the relationship to higher, adjacent, supporting, supported, and subordinate CPs;
     and the bandwidth requirements for data which must be pushed or pulled between CPs and other
     headquarters. Understanding interoperability between these systems is key. Commanders consider which
     systems are appropriate for each echelon and grouping of echelons. Refer to Individual Task 150-C2-
     5251, Organize the Command and Control (C2) Network.

COMPLETE UNIT STANDARD OPERATING PROCEDURES REVIEW AND UPDATES

     1-28. The commander conducts an internal review of all standard operating procedures (SOPs) and
     policies. The commander further directs an SOP review by all staff sections to ensure the latest doctrine
     and policies, and the results of recent unit AARs are incorporated and practiced in accordance with the
     current mission set and OE. Refer to Individual Task 158-LDR-8001, Prepare to Transition to a New
     Leadership Position.

ESTABLISH A COMMON VISION

     1-29. As commanders begin to develop an understanding of the organization and the OE they start
     visualizing the operation’s end state – in this case achieving C2 proficiency – and potential solutions to
     solve problems. Commanders refine visualization as their understanding matures. Visualization is
     typically articulated to staffs and subordinate commanders using a concise description statement of one
     to three sentences. This description facilitates shared understanding of the situation, mission, and intent.

30 July 2025                       TC 6-0.2                                                                                   7


---

Chapter 1

     Commanders refer to FM 6-22 and ADP 6-0 for additional information as well as Individual Task 150-
     LDR-5018, Establish a Common Vision.

ESTABLISH COMMAND / LEADERSHIP PHILOSOPHY

     1-30. Commanders establish their command (or leadership) philosophy. This philosophy establishes the
     commander’s method for achieving personal and unit goals nested with the unit table of organization
     and equipment (TO&E) design. At the battalion level, the command philosophy focuses on the
     commander’s vision, goals, objectives, and performance measures and either reinforces or alters the
     existing organizational values. drive operations and sets the foundation for how the organization will
     create shared understanding. Commanders review assessments of the unit from peers, higher
     headquarters, and subordinate leaders when crafting their command philosophy. The command
     philosophy is informed by the unit’s standard METL. Commanders describe how they intend to achieve
     the principles of the mission command approach to C2 with their personal command (or leadership)
     philosophy to provide clarity to all subordinate commanders, leaders, and Soldiers.

     1-31. Commanders deliver their command philosophy in a place or venue of their choosing. Many
     commanders post their command philosophy in their training guidance. Other commanders deliver their
     philosophy verbally before the start of an exercise or operation. Refer to Individual Task 150-LDR-5028,
     Establish the Mission Command Approach to Command and Control.

ESTABLISH PLANNING / TRAINING GUIDANCE

     1-32. Working with the battalion S-3, the commander reviews the current planning and training
     guidance, and training objectives for each event taking place - including how those objectives nest with
     the unit mission as a whole. Refer to FM 7-0, Training, and Individual Task 150-LDR-5321, Establish
     Planning Guidance. The commander tailors that guidance to fit the mission set and the needs of their
     own command philosophy, vision, and intent. The commander must provide this guidance for the staff
     to develop appropriate products that enable the commander to achieve their desired end state. The
     commander provides the updated planning and training guidance to the staff and subordinate leaders.
     The commander’s training guidance can contain a variety of items to include the following (See FM 7-
     0, Appendix A, for a more comprehensive discussion of training guidance):

          • Commander-to-commander dialogues.
          • The command’s current training plans and strategies.
          • Changes in mission-essential task required proficiency, weapons proficiency, or collective live

                fire proficiency.
          • Publication of senior headquarters annual training guidance.
          • Major changes to training resources availability.
          • Changes in the OE, threat conditions, or unit regional alignment.

DRIVE CELL CONSTRUCT

     1-33. Battalions typically do not have staffs large enough to permanently establish Future Plans or Future
     Operations integrating cells. Furthermore, many battalion-level staff personnel are inexperienced at
     working on a staff. The commander takes these factors into consideration when reviewing the staff
     organization and structure during this portion of the tables and provides guidance for functional and
     integrating cell framework. The commander outlines the purpose for each cell using the SOP and unit-
     specific Army training publication (ATP) as a guide and directs updates or changes to processes and
     procedures. Refer to Individual Task 150-LDR-5251, Organize the Command and Control System.

ESTABLISH INPUTS AND OUTPUTS FOR THE STAFF

     1-34. The commander ensures that all staff elements, processes, and procedures have established and
     approved inputs and outputs specific to their respective functions, enabling them to achieve commander
     expectations. Staff outputs generally involve a decision by the commander. For example, the battalion
     commander refines requirements for each staff element to contribute to the mission analysis led by the
     executive officer (XO) during MDMP. These inputs provide the staff planners information so they can
     create feasible, acceptable, suitable, complete, and discrete courses of action (COAs) to consider.

8          TC 6-0.2  30 July 2025


---

                       Command and Control Warfighting Function Training Tables – Battalions

     1-35. Defining the inputs and outputs assists in product refinement, creates processes standard to the
     unit, and manages staff involvement which enables effectiveness and efficiency within the organization.
     Refer to Individual Task 150-LDR-5011, Establish Boards and Working Groups. Staff inputs and
     outputs are synchronized through an effective battle rhythm. The commander or designated
     representative for each function and/or working group must periodically review approved inputs and
     outputs to ensure alignment with changing conditions and commander requirements to create shared
     understanding within and external to the organization. Commanders and their XOs reference ATP 6-0.5,
     FM 6-0, and Individual Training Support Package (ITSP) 150-C2-5123 / 1.0, Establish the Battle Rhythm
     to develop input and output guidance. Appendix C addresses battle rhythm training considerations in
     more detail.

     1-36. Tables I-II are typically completed by the commander once in their command cycle. However, the
     commander revisits Table III-IV as the staff performs similar tables for retraining. For example, the
     commander may choose to run Table I-VI in the first three months of command. However, the
     commander notices that the staff needs retraining on staff Tables III-IV. The commander then can use
     an upcoming CPX to retrain these tables. The commander can run through Tables III-V a second time
     to better refine the products and outputs needed by the staff allowing them to build proficiency. Figure

COMPLETE COMMAND, CONTROL, COMMUNICATIONS, COMPUTERS, INTELLIGENCE
(C4I) CONSTRUCT WITHIN THE HEADQUARTERS

    1-37. As the headquarters prepares for Table IV, Prepare the Headquarters for Operations, the
    commander must determine the purpose, interaction, organization, and construct of the command,
    control, communications, computers, and intelligence (C4I) systems within the CP. This is based on
    mission requirements, the commander’s intent, and the OE. Table I began the process of determining
    what information systems to use and the interoperability of these systems. In this table, the commander
    refines those decisions and leads the staff to establish the structure to support those decisions. The
    commander must weigh the mission, current SOP, and their tailored command philosophy when refining
    guidance for the C4I within the CP. When designing the C4I, the commander must weigh the importance
    of mobility, operability, and survivability of the CP. Refer to Individual Task 150-LDR-5253, Manage
    Command and Control, Information Systems. The commander should appoint a battalion knowledge
    management officer (KMO) now to prepare for knowledge management requirements in future tables.

30 July 2025           TC 6-0.2                                                                                  9


---

Chapter 1

ASSESS STAFF WRITTEN AND VERBAL COMMUNICATIONS SKILLS

     1-38. Commanders train subordinates to write and speak effectively through formal and informal
     opportunities. Commanders will still seek opportunities to assess the written and oral communications
     skills of the staff, but formal assessments can be delegated to the XO. Commanders or designated
     representatives assess staff and subordinates’ written and verbal communications using FM 6-0, AR 25-
     50, and Individual Task 158-LDR-8009, Communicate Effectively. Once assessments are complete, the
     commander and XO should help individual staff members to develop action plans to overcome
     weaknesses in their communications skills.

DEVELOP INITIAL COMMAND POST ARCHITECTURE

     1-39. Once the commander understands the C2IS requirements, they provide guidance to develop the
     architecture of each CP. The XO, operations sergeant major (SGM), knowledge management (KM)
     team, section establish and ensure network connectivity for the main CP and any other CPs the
     headquarters employs. Establishing guidance for each CP and how it is employed is based on its specific
     functions. Common considerations include:

          • Administration operations.
          • Assessing operations.
          • Controlling operations.
          • Coordinating with internal and external organizations.
          • Building and maintaining situational understanding.

     1-40. In addition, the commander and staff must pay attention to the identified or projected threats in the
     electromagnetic spectrum (EMS). Given the maturity of the organization and the dynamic threat that is
     encountered, CP guidance will constantly evolve and is addressed when internal or external conditions
     change that will affect the purpose, function, survivability, or effectiveness of the designated CPs. The
     commander references FM 6-0 and other appropriate doctrinal references and can assess this with
     Individual Task 150-C2-5202, Develop a Command Post Layout.

DELEGATE AUTHORITIES

     1-41. The unit will most likely have in place delegated authorities for legal, administrative, and other
     pertinent items. Each commander has a unique personal decision-making style and staff interaction
     preference. For example, one commander may prefer to personally make task organization decisions,
     where another prefers to delegate decision-making authority for task organization to the XO. The
     commander reviews these delegated authorities to ensure that they are in keeping with their intent and
     philosophy, the mission, and overall unit well-being. Commanders must wisely consider the selection
     for delegation of decision-making authority for specific meetings and functions; commanders cannot
     attend every battle rhythm event and must allow their staffs to function and develop. Essential elements,
     at a minimum, in development of authority delegation include constraints, risks, and success criteria.

     1-42. When delegating authorities, the commander considers how to best employ the command sergeant
     major (CSM) during the conduct of operations. As the senior noncommissioned officer (NCO) of the
     command, the CSM carries out policies and enforces standards for the performance of individual training
     and conduct of Soldiers. They give advice and initiate recommendations to commanders and staffs in all
     matters pertaining to Soldiers. During operations, the commander employs the CSM throughout the area
     of operations to extend command influence, assess morale of the force, and assist during critical events.
     Refer to Individual Tasks 150-LDR-5320, Designate Succession of Command, and 150-LDR-5319,
     Delegate Authority.

TABLE IV: PREPARE THE HEADQUARTERS FOR OPERATIONS

     1-43. As commanders direct their staffs through the operations process, they provide guidance on the
     following topics:

          • Common operational picture requirements.
          • C2 system.
          • Battle rhythm.

10         TC 6-0.2  30 July 2025


---

                                  Command and Control Warfighting Function Training Tables – Battalions

     • CP organization.
     • Assessment of subordinates.

1-44. Before any training event, the commander establishes training objectives. This is a critical
component in comprehensive preparation and establishing expectations prior to executing operations.
Training objectives include identification of specified tasks with associated conditions and standards, as
well as the expected proficiency rating the commander intends the unit to achieve by the conclusion of
the event. For example, the commander uses a STAFFEX to train the staff on Tables III-IV over four
days in their third month of command. The training objectives for the event include: the unit will achieve
an overall Practiced (P) rating on two specific METs, all processes are validated, all retraining is
identified by day three and completed by day four, and the headquarters is prepared to be certified
through a CPX in three weeks. Assessments and training objective proficiency/deficiency is integrated
into retraining or included in SOP adjustments prior to Table V.

1-45. At this stage of training the commander should strive to achieve basic C2 system competency. As
the unit becomes more proficient, more advanced tables should be conducted under tougher conditions
that force the commander to refine their guidance to account for such things as CP dispersion, site
selection, security considerations etc. Figure 1-4 below establishes the inputs, tasks to be accomplished
during this table, and the auditable outputs for the table:

PREPARE FOR DRIVING THE OPERATIONS PROCESS

    1-46. As the staff initially begins integrating and preparing for operations, the commander uses
    Collective Task 71-BN-5100, Conduct the Operations Process for Command and Control, as a reference
    and training aid to direct and refine actions. Commanders are the most important participants in the
    operations process and use Individual Task 150-LDR-5100, Lead the Operations Process, as needed to
    guide them through their roles. While staffs perform essential functions that amplify the effectiveness of
    operations, commanders drive the operations process through understanding, visualizing, describing,
    directing, leading, and assessing operations. To effectively prepare for driving the operations process
    commanders consider and relay to the staff how they envision building and maintaining situational
    understanding, apply critical and creative thinking, and encourage collaboration and dialogue. The
    commander understands the doctrinal roles and responsibilities of each staff section and CP. The staff
    develops a primary, alternate, contingency, and emergency (PACE) plan within and between CPs that
    will sustain the operations process throughout the exigencies of LSCO.

    1-47. The operations process is always conducted in conjunction with another task, normally drawn from
    the unit METL. To gain or maintain proficiency, commanders and senior staff leaders incorporate
    supporting tasks that reinforce the major C2 activities performed during operations and allocate time for
    retraining as required. For example, the unit performs well in the majority of steps in MDMP but
    struggles in COA development. It is not required for the battalion to complete this task to the T or even
    the P proficiency level at this point in training (Table IV - step 5), but the staff must be poised to attain
    at least basic task proficiency in the next table (Table V).

DIRECT COMMON OPERATIONAL PICTURE REQUIREMENTS

    1-48. The common operational picture (COP) is a display of relevant information through a shared visual
    workspace that commanders and staff use to monitor operations, analyze data, share thoughts, and plan
    COAs. Once the operations process is initiated, the commander begins directing COP requirements to
    assist in making informed and effective decisions through a display of relevant information. These
    requirements are based on all elements of the C2 system, the capabilities of the unit, the mission, the C4I
    construct, mission and operational variables, and the commander’s personal leadership philosophy. In

12                                              TC 6-0.2                                    30 July 2025


---

                                        Command and Control Warfighting Function Training Tables – Battalions

     development of the COP, the commander must consider difficulties with C2 processes and procedures,
     digital and analog COP correlation, and network and information system integration.

     1-49. The COP is key to each step of the operations process: plan, prepare, execute, and assess.
     Commander requirements for incorporation of a relevant and comprehensive COP into a CP can include:

          • How to display intent and planning guidance.
          • Description of posted relevant information.
          • Level and type of known and templated enemy threats, hazards, activities, and locations.
          • How the organization will collaborate with higher and subordinate organizations.
          • Use of planning and brief-back tools.
          • Types of control measures, graphics, and efforts used.
          • Level of battle tracking.
          • Integration of priority intelligence requirements with friendly force information requirements

                (FFIR) and essential elements of friendly information (EEFI).
          • Display of significant activities (SIGACTs).
          • Display of key nodes and critical infrastructure.

     1-50. For specific guidance to the staff, commanders can review Individual Task 150-LDR-5315,
     Provide Guidance for the Common Operational Picture, and ATP 6-0.5.

ORGANIZE THE COMMAND AND CONTROL SYSTEM

     1-51. The C2 system enhances the commander’s ability to conduct operations. Individual Task 150-
     LDR-5251, Organize the Command and Control System, addresses the effort. This includes the
     arrangement of people, processes, networks, and CPs. A commander’s C2 system begins with people.
     Commanders base their C2 system on human characteristics (skills, knowledge, and abilities) more than
     equipment and procedures. The C2 system must be organized to:

          • Support the commander’s decision making.
          • Collect, create, and maintain relevant information.
          • Prepare knowledge products to support the commander’s and leaders’ understanding and

                visualization.
          • Prepare and communicate directives.

     1-52. The commander ensures that the headquarters establishes and rehearses all aspects of the C2
     system before the staff begins their final rehearsal for certification in Staff Table V.

PROVIDE BATTLE RHYTHM GUIDANCE

     1-53. While the battle rhythm is managed by the XO, it is commander-centric and must be directed to
     support the commander’s decision-making process. Either directly, or through the XO, the commander
     provides the criteria for development of a deliberate daily cycle of command, staff, and unit activities
     intended to synchronize current and future operations. This guidance includes the types or series of
     meetings with expected staff representation, outcomes, and delegated decision authority for particular
     processes or functions. The battle rhythm enables:

          • A commander’s decision making.
          • A routine for staff interaction and coordination.
          • Interaction between the commander and staff.
          • Staff synchronization across time, space, and purpose.
          • Planning by the staff.

     1-54. The battle rhythm is a logical arrangement for the sequencing and timing of reports, meetings, and
     briefings based on the commander’s preferences, higher headquarters’ requirements, and the type of
     operations (FM 6.0). The commander must describe or direct their required touch points to the staff (both
     internal and external to the organization) to further develop and refine the battle rhythm to save time and
     energy and prevent frustration. The battle rhythm is tailored, adjusted, and assessed throughout the C2TT
     and unit events to match the needs of the command, the mission and the OE. The commander can use
     Individual Task 150-C2-5123, Establish a Battle Rhythm, and ITSP 150-C2-5123 / 1.0, Establish the

30 July 2025  TC 6-0.2  13


---

Chapter 1

     Battle Rhythm, for reference in forming the battle rhythm guidance, as well as FM 6-0 and ATP 6-0.5
     for a list of integrating functions and inputs/outputs to consider.

REFINE COMMAND POST LAYOUT

     1-55. Throughout the event for Table IV, the commander will review, refine, and reassess the CPs’
     physical design to ensure that it meets the commander’s information requirements and provides for
     efficient CP operations. The physical layout of a CP has a significant impact on its functionality. A layout
     contributes to how efficiently information is passed from one staff element to another and how
     effectively sections communicate with one another. Commanders will analyze the mission to vet the
     layout or design of their CPs (to include positioning of personnel, information systems, displays,
     equipment, network cabling, and electricity requirements) to ensure it is appropriate for the OE. The
     following list will aid in designing the CP physical layout:

          • Staff integration and crosstalk.
          • Meeting and working group workspace.
          • Trafficability.
          • COP visibility.
          • Lighting and climate control.
          • The ease of information flow.
          • User interface with communications systems.
          • The positioning of information displays for ease of use.
          • Displaying complementary information on maps and information systems.
          • Adequate workspace for the staff and commander.
          • The ease of displacement (setup, tear-down, and movement).

     1-56. The commander will continue to refine this design throughout the tables and upon notification of
     new mission requirements and by accounting for factors in the OE that influence the need for CP
     effectiveness and survivability. Refer to Individual Task 150-C2-5200, Conduct Command Post
     Operations, and 150-C2-5202, Develop Command Post Layout.

ASSESS SUBORDINATE LEADERS

     1-57. The battalion commander trains subordinate leaders throughout all the tables. In Table IV, the
     commander is afforded the opportunity to conduct a holistic assessment of their leaders working as a
     team towards a common goal. Unique to each commander, assessments are developed to measure
     performance levels and proficiency in craft. Of note, commanders throughout the Army are responsible
     for leaders/subordinate commanders two levels down. For direct assessment, the battalion commander
     is typically responsible to assess and certify company commanders and separate platoon leaders, and
     their primary staff leaders.

     1-58. In Table III, the commander assessed basic written and verbal assessments of key staff leaders.
     The commander now focuses on building and developing their skills to be successful in all operational
     aspects of C2. The commander should develop an assessment plan that includes an evaluation of written
     directives, verbal communication of appropriate staff/subordinate mission requirements (including
     MDMP), leader actions, and routine operations observed in a variety of conditions over time. The
     certification of subordinate leaders takes place in Table V. Refer to Individual Task, 158-LDR-8003,
     Conduct Initial Assessment for a Leadership Position.

REFINE TRAINING GUIDANCE

     1-59. Commanders provide pre-execution guidance to subordinate units early in the planning process.
     This ensures the staff meets the commander’s guidance throughout planning. This guidance helps keep
     subordinate leaders, planners, primary staff and special staff synchronized with the commander’s vision
     for the event (such as iterations, duration, and participation level). Before the event begins, the
     commander establishes the training objectives for the event through planning guidance. When
     developing training guidance for each event, the commander reviews previous evaluations of the
     organization, identifies the overall purpose of the training, describes the expected performance, dictates

14         TC 6-0.2  30 July 2025


---

                                        Command and Control Warfighting Function Training Tables – Battalions

     the conditions under which the tasks are performed, sets the standards used to measure performance, and
     reviews all applicable T&EOs for each MET to be trained (e.g., Collective Task 55-BN-4800, Conduct
     Expeditionary Deployment Operations at the Battalion Level, when conducting a deployment exercise).

     1-60. When writing objectives the commander uses clear, direct language not indirect or passive words
     (such as understand or familiarize) and links the objectives to the event. Training objectives are tied to
     unit METLs and achieving task proficiency. To avoid subordinate and staff confusion, commanders need
     to be specific about what constitutes the expected standard level of performance through simple, clear,
     and concisely written objectives. For example, the battalion commander directs through their planning
     guidance that the upcoming CPX will focus on their ‘movement to contact’ and ‘conduct a defense’
     METs from their METL. The commander further directs that the staff be proficient to the P level on
     performance measures outlined in the METs T&EO, that the training of all Tables III-IV be performed,
     and that the CP be set up in the battalion training area. The commander may refer to Individual Task
     150-LDR-5321, Establish Planning Guidance.

TABLE V: DRIVE THE OPERATIONS PROCESS

     1-61. Though Staff Table V is designed for the XO to conduct a comprehensive assessment prior to the
     certification of the staff, the commander monitors the process. The commander uses Individual Task
     150-LDR-5100, Lead the Operations Process, throughout Table V as a guide, as well as exercising
     appropriate degrees of command presence at key moments and decision points within the operations
     process to shape and prepare the staff for Table VI. While unable to devote all their time to planning,
     commanders follow the status of the planning effort, participate during critical events of the process, and
     make decisions based on the detailed work of the staff. This table is conducted in conjunction with Table
     V for each of the staff, CP, and digital crew. The ITSP 150-DIV-0006 / 2.0, The Division Commander
     Drives the Operations Process, is available to commanders in two formats (one Russian threat based,
     the other Chinese threat based) to enhance their understanding of their role in the operations process.
     Figure 1-5 on page 15 establishes the inputs, tasks to be accomplished during this table, and the auditable
     outputs for the table:

LEAD THE OPERATIONS PROCESS

    1-62. Commanders are the most important participants in the operations process. Commanders drive the
    operations process through the activities of understanding, visualizing, describing, directing, leading,
    and assessing operations. Throughout the operations process, commanders apply leadership to translate
    decisions into action. This is achieved through exercising appropriate levels of command presence by
    sharing technical and tactical knowledge gained through experience with subordinates throughout the
    event as the mission and situation dictate. The enduring effects of successful employment of command
    presence provide a background for subordinates to realize how and when to adapt to achieve the
    commander’s intent for mission accomplishment. In this table the commander certifies subordinate
    leaders and applicable crews on the overarching framework for exercising C2 through the operations
    process. The commander directs section/cell level AARs and that the staff retrain as needed before Table
    VI certification. Commanders can use the same event as outlined in Table IV or a different event based
    on time and resources. Refer to Individual Task 150-LDR-5100, Lead the Operations Process.

DETERMINE COMMANDER’S CRITICAL INFORMATION REQUIREMENTS

    1-63. Determining the commander’s critical information requirements (CCIR) is addressed in Individual
    Task 150-LDR-5019, Determine the Commander’s Critical Information Requirements. These CCIR are
    based on the unit’s OE, its mission, and the organization’s designed capabilities. The commander
    develops CCIR to cover gaps in their own analysis and the staff’s analysis or to identify critical
    information requirements to facilitate timely decisions. These CCIR allow the commander to better
    understand and visualize the OE, define or redefine the problem, and inform planning guidance to
    support time-sensitive decision-making requirements. However, CCIR are not focused solely on data or
    isolated information but rather provide relevant understanding to enable mission accomplishment. Well-
    developed CCIR, in combination with the commander’s intent, assist in accelerating allocation of
    resources, guide and prioritize employment of collection assets/analysis resources, and aid in
    information flow to create shared understanding. The two key elements are force information
    requirements (FFIR) and priority intelligence requirements (PIR). A CCIR is:

         • Specified by the commander for a specific operation.
         • Applicable only to the commander who specifies it.

16                                              TC 6-0.2                                          30 July 2025


---

                                        Command and Control Warfighting Function Training Tables – Battalions

          • Situation dependent.
          • Directly linked to a current or future mission.
          • Time-sensitive.

     1-64. CCIR will change and mature with mission priorities and the OE. Commanders dictate the
     notification criteria and reporting hierarchy with respect to prioritization and specified recipients. The
     commander also follows a process to periodically review and update the CCIR. Commanders widely
     distribute the CCIR and revise them as the situation changes. Information is continuously collected. It
     may be delivered on a routine schedule or as requested. An information-push system pushes information
     from the source to the user as it becomes available or according to a schedule (such as through routine
     reports). An information-pull system supplies information as requested. Commanders and staffs
     determine how to use and integrate both types of systems. In addition to CCIR, commanders develop a
     wake-up or notification criteria list/matrix/worksheet separate from CCIR. CCIR may be considered
     wake up criteria.

ASSESS TACTICAL SITUATION AND OPERATION

     1-65. As the unit begins operating within their designed capabilities against their assigned mission set,
     the commander establishes how they will assess the unit’s performance of assigned tasks and progress
     towards mission accomplishment. One technique is to establish measures of effectiveness (MOE) and
     measures of performance (MOP) during the Table VI training event. Commanders can use Collective
     Task 71-BN-5130, Assess the Tactical Situation and Operation.

ASSESS PROCESSES AND PROCEDURES

     1-66. Though Staff Table V is designed for the XO to conduct a comprehensive assessment prior to the
     certification of the staff, the commander monitors the process. The commander exercises appropriate
     degrees of command presence at key moments and decision points during the operations process to shape
     and prepare the staff for Table VI. While unable to devote all their time to planning, commanders follow
     the status of the planning effort, participate during critical events of the process, and make decisions
     based on the detailed work of the staff. The commander can refer to Individual Tasks 150-C2-5200,
     Conduct Command Post Operations, and 150-C2-5130, Assess the Tactical Situation, to ensure members
     of the staff are following established processes and procedures, and formally changing them when
     necessary. At the conclusion of Table V, the commander directs any necessary updates or changes to
     identified processes and procedures before the progression to Table VI

CERTIFY SUBORDINATE LEADERS AND CREWS

     1-67. In Table V, the battalion commander certifies leaders in the C2 system. Once the battalion
     commander certifies the XO, that commander may delegate subordinate leader certification to the XO
     for Table V. Battalion commanders use the T&EOs associated with each task in Training Tables I
     through V. Battalion commanders can also direct additional certification tasks for subordinate leaders
     based on uniqueness of their organizations or gaps identified in the TC. The commander is ultimately
     responsible for all certifications within their organization. The commander trains and empowers the XO
     and staff primaries to assist in certification duties. Of note, as leaders cycle in-and-out of leadership
     positions, the commander continuously assesses, trains, retrains, and certifies these leaders. Refer to
     Collective Task 71-BN-0050, Establish a Command Post.

TABLE VI: ASSESS AND CERTIFY THE HEADQUARTERS

     1-68. This table is the certifying event for the commander and the headquarters. This table is conducted
     as a collective unit event during which the staff, CPs, and digital crew execute their respective Table VI
     certifications while conducting MET training. The commander uses the tasks listed in each audience’s
     Table VI to certify them. The commander completes this table when the headquarters has successfully
     completed all Table VI requirements, and the brigade commander has certified the battalion commander.
     The end state of this table is reached when the commander certifies that the staff, CP, and digital crew
     are prepared to perform their respective roles and functions during the conduct of collective training. For
     example, the battalion commander uses a CPX to conduct one or more tasks from their METL and

CERTIFY SUBORDINATES ON PREPARING AN OPERATION ORDER

    1-70. The commander participates and assesses the staff and the XO as they develop the operation order
    (OPORD) using Individual Task 71-BN-5119, Prepare an Operation Order.

CERTIFY SUBORDINATES ON EMPLACING AND DISPLACING THE COMMAND POST

    1-71. Commanders assess the emplacement and displacement of the CP (for those CPs that must move
    during the conduct of LSCO). The commander evaluates the officer in charge of each CP on Individual
    Tasks 150-C2-5200, Conduct Command Post Operations, and 150-C2-5201, Displace the Command
    Post, and uses ATP 6-0.5 as needed. The commander and XO also evaluate the headquarters using
    collective tasks:

         • Collective Task 71-BN-5200, Conduct Command Post Operations.
         • Collective Task 71-BN-5201, Displace the Command Post.
         • Collective Task 71-BN-5202, Conduct Transfer Control of Operations.
         • Collective Task 71-BN-5250, Maintain Continuity of Command and Control.

CERTIFY SUBORDINATES ON ESTABLISHING THE COMMON OPERATIONAL PICTURE

    1-72. Commanders assess the establishment of the COP as the S-3 completes Individual Task 150-C2-
    5315, Establish the Common Operational Picture, and the headquarters using Collective Task 71-BN-
    5319, Establish the Common Operational Picture.

              Command and Control Warfighting Function Training Tables – Battalions

CERTIFY THE BATTLE RHYTHM

     1-73. Commanders evaluate the functionality of the battle rhythm throughout the event making changes
     or adjustments as needed. The commander assesses the XO on managing the battle rhythm using
     Individual Task 150-C2-5123, Establish a Battle Rhythm, and the staff on executing the battle rhythm
     using Collective Task 71-BN-5125, Implement the Battle Rhythm.

CONDUCT AFTER ACTION REVIEWS

     1-74. Commanders are responsible for the training of their unit. An AAR is the primary tool for
     providing feedback. Although leaders can conduct two types of AARs, formal and informal,
     commanders provide guidance to leaders on specific areas of interest, assessments, or feedback to gain
     insight of their organization. This guidance may include collaboration of staff sections, internal and
     external communications, reporting formats, synchronization, briefing techniques, use of the CPs, CP
     layout, and so on. While the T&EO and task proficiency standards provide objective criteria for
     determining task proficiency, assessments allow leaders to consider the subjective nature of training.
     Leaders’ assessments combine their personal observations with other information from the staff to
     develop an overall assessment of the unit’s ability to accomplish its mission. Commanders identify areas
     for retraining, as needed. Commanders use Individual Task 150-C2-5133, Conduct a Formal After
     Action Review.

CERTIFY THE COMMAND AND CONTROL SYSTEM

     1-75. The commander evaluates the unit’s C2 system on how well it enhances the commander’s ability
     to conduct operations using Individual Task 150-LDR-5251, Organize the Command and Control
     System. Commanders evaluate the four components of their C2 system: people, processes, networks, and
     CPs. Commanders evaluate these four components of the C2 system relating to how they:

          • Support the commander’s decision-making.
          • Collect, create, and maintain relevant information.
          • Prepare knowledge products to support the commander’s and leaders’ understanding and

                visualization.
          • Prepare and communicate directives.

     1-76. The outcome of C2 Tables I-VI is the commander has built a team, philosophy, and training plan
     based on the unit design, capabilities, and mission, and has certified that the headquarters is prepared to
     train collectively as one C2 system. As part of the entire training audience (commander, staff, CP, and
     digital crew) the commander transitions to tables VII-X. These tables are complex and stress the
     application of the C2 WFF. These tables provide a progressive framework for the commander in
     preparation for their unit’s culminating training event (CTE).

TABLE VII: DIRECT COMMAND AND CONTROL SYSTEM
INTEGRATION

     1-77. The battalion commander has the flexibility to organize the four components of the C2 system
     (people, processes, CPs, and network) to support their ability to make decisions and facilitate
     communication within the battalion headquarters and with higher, subordinate, adjacent, supported, and
     supporting commanders. The commander can move back to the previous tables to retrain or recertify at
     any time they feel it would benefit the unit.

     1-78. At the beginning of Table VII, the unit will receive a fragmentary order (FRAGORD) from higher
     headquarters directing a change of mission from the activity in Table VI. This order will drive training
     in Tables VII through X. It includes the task organization the battalion will fight with when conducting
     its external evaluation. It also provides the commander information on the unified action partners that
     will participate in the exercise. Figure 1-7 on page 19 establishes the inputs, tasks to be accomplished
     during this table, and the auditable outputs for the table.
