ORGANIZE PERSONNEL

     1-79. The commander directs the organization of all non-organic personnel (liaisons, attachments, etc.)
     based on capabilities, mission, and the higher commander’s intent. The commander’s designated
     representative will ensure all are assimilated into their positions in a posture that allows them to
     contribute effectively. The commander uses Individual Task 150-LDR-5013, Organize the Staff for
     Operations, to assist in this effort.

     1-80. New personnel must establish lines of communications and should physically meet with
     counterparts at lower, higher, adjacent, and supporting headquarters whenever possible. The transition
     or establishment and maintenance of these personal relationships are important to maintaining situational
     and shared understanding for the commander. Considerations for organizing include command and
     support relationships and performance of three essential functions:

          • Supporting the commander through their area of expertise.
          • Assisting subordinate units through active collaboration to solve problems.
          • Informing units and organizations outside the headquarters with relevant information.

     1-81. The battalion commander may delegate authority to manage the battalion staff to the XO; however,
     commander oversight is still encouraged to ensure personalities, leadership traits, subject matter
     expertise, and other requirements meet guidance for organizing personnel.

     1-82. The commander, through designated representatives, also directs appropriate foreign disclosure
     actions for information exchange with unified action partners as needed.

ENHANCE INFORMATION SYSTEM TRAINING GUIDANCE

     1-83. The battalion has C2IS systems that collect, process, store, display, and disseminate information.
     This includes a variety of hardware and software that enhance the commander’s ability to visualize the
     operation and assist in decision-making. The foundation of any information system is the continuous,
     uninterrupted flow and processing of information to support planning, preparation, execution, and
     continuous assessment. The battalion has information systems that are interoperable, flexible,
     responsive, mobile, survivable, and sustainable. Refer to Individual Task 150-C2-5251, Organize the
     Command and Control Network.

20                                                   TC 6-0.2                                          30 July 2025


---

                                        Command and Control Warfighting Function Training Tables – Battalions

     1-84. Because of the additional personnel/attachments and mission requirements, the commander
     reviews the established systems and makes changes to account for new interaction, collaboration, and
     synchronization demands, as well as the needs of attached or assigned units/individuals, and the higher
     commander’s guidance.

     1-85. To perform advanced, complex, interactive training, the commander ensures the digital crew is
     certified and that new shift personnel are incorporated through training event repetitions. The
     commander authorizes the XO and the knowledge management officer (KMO) to enhance the training
     of the Soldiers and supporting personnel operating the C2IS. Battle captains, KM representatives, COP
     managers, and unit C2 information integrators (formerly Mission Command Digital Master Gunners
     MCDMGs) can provide guidance and leadership to the crews and lead them through command directed
     scenarios, and report progress back to the commander. KMO may be an appointed additional duty –
     usually for the S-6 – at battalion level.

     1-86. The S-6/KMO and C2 information integrators, under the authority of the XO and the commander,
     can further tie into communication exercises (COMEXs) or digital exercises being led by the S-6 as a
     time to enhance training opportunities for the staff and digital crew. For example, the battalion XO
     directs the information system operators to participate in the upcoming COMEX for the battalion and
     subordinate units. The C2 information integrator uses training objectives with clear performance
     measures for assessing the performance of all digital operators. The XO reports the assessment to the
     commander.

SECURE AND MAINTAIN NETWORKS

     1-87. Networks are formed when terminal devices and transmission media are interconnected with
     switching equipment to ensure that information (voice, imagery, data, or message) is transported to
     appropriate locations. The commander, working with the S-6, ensures that the network is both secured
     and maintained in compliance with Department of Defense (DoD) and Department of the Army (DA)
     regulations, unit organization/manning requirements, environmental conditions, unit SOP and directives
     from the higher headquarters. Essential elements include identification of authorized/prioritized users,
     access control policies/strategies, and information security training as well as system/software upgrades
     that enable interoperability and compatibility. The battalion does not have all the expertise required to
     secure the network, so the S-6 must coordinate with higher or external signal assets to secure the network.
     In addition, developing a PACE communications plan will mitigate short term loss of connectivity and
     maintain situational and shared understanding. The commander refers to Individual Tasks 113-367-5001,
     Implement Network Protection Measures; and 113-473-7007, Implement Network Security Measures
     within a System Architecture, as needed.

REFINE PROCESSES AND PROCEDURES

     1-88. In this table the commander refines and updates processes and procedures to organize activities
     within and between the headquarters, and between flank, subordinate, supported, and supporting units.
     The battalion staff conducts the following processes: the operations process, MDMP, the targeting
     process, the intelligence process, intelligence preparation of the operational environment (IPOE), the
     information collection process, and the KM process. The commander refines processes and procedures
     based on running estimates, AARs, and the commander directed assessment plan, which may include
     measures of effectiveness (MOEs) and measures of performance (MOPs). As updates and changes are
     made, the commander ensures those actions are captured in SOPs and published to the force. Refer to
     Individual Task 150-LDR-5251, Organize the Command and Control System, and Collective Task 71-
     BN-5125, Implement the Battle Rhythm. Examples of typical battalion processes and procedures that C2
     information systems support include:

          • CP battle drills.
          • Shift-change briefings.
          • Operation update and assessment briefings.
          • Operations synchronization meetings.
          • Transferring control of the operation between CPs.
          • The battle rhythm.

Chapter 1

EVALUATE THE COMMAND POST SIGNATURE

     1-89. The commander, with S-2, S-3, S-4, and S-6 input, considers the digital systems and CPs required
     to support C2 based on the mission set and OE. They evaluate the unit’s electromagnetic signature based
     on enemy detection capabilities. The commander ensures that the unit is mitigating/minimizing its
     footprint while still maintaining C2. Mitigation measures might include limiting the amount of satellite
     time or reducing redundant information systems that create significant signatures. At the battalion level
     the S-6 sill have to coordinate with higher headquarters for the technical assets required for the battalion
     to analyze its signature.

     1-90. The commander ensures that cyber defense drills are being conducted and assessed throughout
     training events. These drills replicate a ‘near peer’ competitor in the OE environment as directed by the
     commander. Refer to Individual Task 113-25E-3003, Define the Electromagnetic Operational
     Environment (EMOE), and GTA 11-11-001, Emission Control Planning Aid.

ASSESS TACTICAL SITUATION AND OPERATIONS

     1-91. As the unit begins performing METs within its designed capabilities against the newly assigned
     mission set, the commander refines their assessment plan to measure mission accomplishment.
     Commanders can use Collective Task 71-BN-5130, Assess the Tactical Situation and Operation.

MAINTAIN CONTINUITY OF COMMAND AND CONTROL

     1-92. During this table commanders should begin exercising succession of responsibilities to seconds in
     charge of sections and cells, delegating responsibilities, and otherwise accounting for contingencies that
     stress the C2 system. Commanders can use events in the master scenario events list (MSEL) to trigger
     conditions that force the C2 system to react (e.g. an indirect fire attack temporarily renders a CP
     incapable of performing its primary functions). Commanders can use Individual Task 150-LDR-5029,
     Maintain Continuity of Command and Control, and Collective Task 71-BN-5250, Maintain Continuity
     of Command and Control.

TABLE VIII: APPLY THE MISSION COMMAND APPROACH TO
COMMAND AND CONTROL

     1-93. This table nests the graduate levels of C2 with the integration of additional personnel and
     equipment. Table VIII prepares the commander for the validation rehearsal in Table IX and the CTE for
     the headquarters in Table X. As with the other tables, this C2 training table aligns with events already
     being conducted by the unit. For example, the commander uses a CPX for training tables VIII-IX over a
     four day exercise. Figure 1-8 on page 22 establishes the inputs, tasks to be accomplished during this
     table, and the auditable outputs for the table.

LEAD THE OPERATIONS PROCESS

     1-94. The commander uses Collective Task 71-BN-5100, Conduct the Operations Process for Command
     and Control, and Individual Task 150-LDR-5100, Lead the Operations Process, to train and evaluate
     the headquarters’ execution of the operations process in support of a MET. Integral to the completion of
     these collective tasks is the commander driving the operations process. Commanders drive the operations
     process through their activities of understanding, visualizing, describing, directing, leading, and
     assessing (UVDDLA) operations. Battalion commanders also use mission variables in combination with
     operational variables to refine their understanding of the situation and how these variables might affect
     the mission. How the commander implements UVDDLA activities, identifies key variables, and conveys
     their intent to drive the operations process has a direct impact on the unit’s ability to establish unity of
     effort and exploit vulnerabilities. The operations process is the overarching framework for exercising
     C2.

     1-95. As battalion commanders drive the operations process to support the tactical fight, they provide
     guidance on how to exercise control. No single control option works best for all situations. The XO and
     S-3 are trained and able to drive the operations process in the absence of the commander. This does not
     negate the commander’s responsibility to be present for commander designated performance measures
     listed in the collective T&EOs. For example, the battalion commander delivers the commander’s intent
     at the CP as part of a leader’s performance measure within the Collective Task 71-BN-5100, Conduct
     the Operations Process for Command and Control.

          • Understand – Commanders actively collaborate with other commanders, the staff, and unified
                action partners, to create a shared understanding.

          • Visualize – Commanders visualize the operation’s end state and potential solutions to solve
                problems.

          • Describe – Commanders describe this visualization to their staffs and subordinates. This
                description facilitates shared understanding of the situation, mission, and intent.

          • Direct – Based on this understanding, commanders make decisions and direct action throughout
                the operations process.

          • Lead – Commanders use the operations process to lead Soldiers and forces by providing
                direction and guidance.

30 July 2025                        TC 6-0.2                                                                                        23


---

Chapter 1

          • Assess – Commanders assess operations continuously to better understand current conditions
                and determine how operations are progressing. Commanders incorporate the assessments of the
                staff, subordinate commanders, and unified action partners into their personal assessment of the
                situation. Based on their assessment, commanders modify plans and orders to better accomplish
                the mission. If their assessment reveals a significant variance from their original commander’s
                visualization, commanders reframe the problem and develop a new operational approach.

ESTABLISH THE MISSION COMMAND APPROACH TO COMMAND AND CONTROL

     1-96. In previous tables the commander performed different subtasks of the C2 WFF. Table VIII
     combines those previous efforts to implement the mission command approach to C2. The MC approach
     to C2 is the exercise of authority and direction by the commander using mission orders to enable
     disciplined initiative within the commander’s intent to empower agile and adaptive leaders in the conduct
     of LSCO. The commander completes, or refines if completed in previous tables, the listed Individual
     Tasks:

          • 150-LDR-5028, Establish the Mission Command Approach to Command and Control.
          • 150-LDR-5015, Lead the Unit.
          • 158-LDR-5001, Develop Cohesive Teams Through Mutual Trust.
          • 150-LDR-5007, Establish a Shared Understanding.
          • 150-LDR-5009, Issue Commander’s Guidance.
          • 150-LDR-5004, Communicate the Commander’s Intent.
          • 150-LDR-5036, Determine Appropriate Level of Control.
          • 150-LDR-5020, Establish Control Measures.
          • 150-LDR-5006, Establish Conditions for Subordinates to Exercise Initiative.
          • 150-LDR-5003, Use the Mission Order Technique.
          • 150-LDR-5040, Maintain the Commander’s Estimate.
          • 150-LDR-5043, Establish the Five Integrating Processes.

     1-97. As the commander leads the headquarters through the completion of collective training, the
     commander assesses synchronization, integration, and interoperability of all non-organic units to include
     unified action partners and liaisons. This assessment includes information systems, personnel or liaison
     exchanges, and CP elements. The commander adjusts the C2 system based on their observations.

TABLE IX: COMMAND AND CONTROL WFF VALIDATION
EXERCISE REHEARSAL

     1-98. The commander designates the training event and conditions for the conduct of Table IX. This
     event takes place in a field environment and is scenario driven using the same variables of political,
     military, economic, social, information, infrastructure, physical environment, and time (PMESII-PT)
     that will be used in the unit’s EXEVAL. The conduct of this table is tied to all four training audiences
     of the C2TT working as a unified C2 system towards the successful execution of the unit’s METL. Units
     can also tie this event to training on A-METL. During this table, each CP (such as main, TAC, combat
     trains, field trains) displace at least once in preparation for evaluating displacements during Table X.
     Figure 1-9 on page 24 establishes the inputs, tasks to be accomplished during this table, and the auditable
     outputs for the table.

DRIVE THE OPERATIONS PROCESS

     1-99. Commanders are the most important participants in the operations process. Refer to Individual
     Task 150-LDR-5100, Lead the Operations Process. While staffs, led by the XO perform essential
     functions that contribute to the effectiveness of operations, commanders drive the operations process
     through activities of understand, visualize, describe, direct, and assess in each major operations process
     activity of plan, prepare, execute, and assess.

     1-100. The commander performs these activities throughout the training event as the mission dictates.
     The commander applies an appropriate degree of command presence at the appropriate time and place
     within each major operations process activity while still allowing the headquarters to exercise disciplined
     initiative within their capabilities.

LEAD THE REHEARSAL

     1-101. A rehearsal is a session in which the commander, staff, and unit practices expected actions to
     improve performance during execution (ADP 5-0). Rehearsals are the commander’s tool to ensure that
     staffs and subordinates understand the commander’s intent and the concept of operations. They allow
     commanders and staffs to identify shortcomings in the plan that have not been previously recognized.
     Rehearsals also contribute to external and internal coordination, as the staff identifies additional
     coordinating requirements.

     1-102. The commander leads the unit’s combined arms rehearsal (CAR) and any of the support
     rehearsals (fires, reconnaissance and surveillance, sustainment, etc.) that the commander considers
     particularly important to executing the MET about to be performed. Commanders command the rehearsal
     just as they will command the operation. They maintain the focus and level of intensity, limiting the
     potential for subordinate confusion. An effective rehearsal is not a commander’s briefing to subordinates,
     nor a subordinate brief to the commander. The CAR should be similar to a "practice", where participants
     simultaneously rehearse their actions, reports, and control measures. An effective rehearsal validates
     synchronization—the what, when, and where—of tasks that subordinate units will perform to execute
     the operation, how they relate to each other, and achieve the commander’s intent. Individual Task 150-
     LDR-5039, Lead the Rehearsal, guides the commander for their role in executing the rehearsal.

30 July 2025                                     TC 6-0.2                                                                           25

Chapter 1

PREPARE FOR OPERATIONS

     1-103. Commanders drive preparation activities with a focus on leading and assessing. The functions of
     preparation include the following:

          • Improve situational understanding.
          • Develop a common understanding of the plan.
          • Train and become proficient on critical tasks.
          • Task-organize and integrate the force.
          • Ensure forces and resources are positioned,

     1-104. The commander employs subordinate leaders, such as the CSM to verify that subordinate units
     are conducting directed and SOP pre-combat inspections and provides guidance to the XO and operations
     SGM for doing the same with the staff, CPs, and digital crew. See Individual Task 150-LDR-5022,
     Conduct Pre-Combat Inspections, for greater detail.

COMMAND FORCES

     1-105. The commander leads forces in the execution of the unit METL, exercising authority and
     responsibility to effectively use available resources to accomplish the assigned mission. The commander
     combines the elements of command (authority, responsibility, decision making, and leadership) to
     employ all available combat power to create multiple dilemmas for enemy forces, gain positions of
     relative advantage, and exploiting opportunities to achieve results. Commanders evaluate their
     performance using Individual Task 150-LDR-5037, Command Forces.

CONDUCT THE RAPID DECISION-MAKING AND SYNCHRONIZATION PROCESS

     1-106. Commanders use rapid decision-making and synchronization process (RDSP) for situations when
     the current events do not match the predetermined plan and necessitates an adjustment decision without
     enough time for full MDMP. The commander leads the staff through the conduct of the RDSP. The
     commander completes Individual Task 150-LDR-5014, Lead the Rapid Decision-Making and
     Synchronization Process. This task is performed in conjunction with a collective task. For example, the
     battalion commander uses RDSP after receiving a change in mission from higher to conduct a security
     operation in conjunction with conducting an attack.

CONDUCT AFTER ACTION REVIEWS

     1-107. The commander leads an AAR of the operation conducted during Table IX training with a focus
     on identifying and correcting training deficiencies and/or unit procedures that must be corrected prior to
     the Table X external evaluation. Refer to Individual Task 150-C2-5133, Conduct a Formal After Action
     Review.

TABLE X: COMMAND AND CONTROL WARFIGHTING
FUNCTION VALIDATION EXERCISE

     1-108. This event is typically concurrent with a CTE such as the battalion EXEVAL or combat training
     center (CTC) rotation. This table is directly tied to all four training audiences for this TC (the
     commander, the staff, the CP, and the digital crew). This event is externally evaluated while the unit
     executes collective tasks on the unit’s METL or A-METL. Each CP (such as main, TAC, combat trains,
     or field trains) will be evaluated on its ability to displace during the validation exercise.

     1-109. The outcome of Commander Table X is that the evaluating commander validates that the battalion
     commander is prepared to lead the battalion using the mission command approach to C2 in the execution
     of METs during LSCO. Figure 1-10 on page 26 establishes the inputs, tasks to be accomplished during
     this table, and the auditable outputs for the table.

1-110. Commanders are the most important participants in the operations process. Refer to Individual

LEAD THE UNIT IN COMPLETION OF ASSIGNED TACTICAL TASKS

1-112. The commander leads the battalion and supporting forces to accomplish the tactical mission tasks
from the unit’s METL per the orders of the higher headquarters. See Individual Task 150-LDR-5037,

EVALUATE METL TASKS

1-113. The battalion commander considers the observations of the external evaluation team and

SECTION III – BATTALION STAFF TRAINING TABLES

     1-114. Section III discusses the command and control training tables for the battalion staff.

COMMAND AND CONTROL TRAINING TABLES FOR THE
BATTALION STAFF

     1-115. This section guides the commander and XO through the tables used to train, certify, and validate
     the staff. The principal staff officers consist of the officers that lead coordinating and special staff
     sections, and personal staff officers. The staff consists of the principal staff officers and the officers,
     NCOs, and Soldiers that work in their sections.

30 July 2025                          TC 6-0.2                                                                                        27


---

Chapter 1

    1-116. This section does not cover every aspect of training, nor pinpoint exactly how to train each
    collective or individual task. Commanders and leaders wanting more information on training the force
    will reference the T&EOs for each task, the available training support packages, and FM 7-0 for
    assistance.

    1-117. While the commander is responsible for certifying the staff, the XO is responsible for overall
    staff training. Principal staff officers are responsible for training their staff sections for certification, and
    for ensuring leaders of all staff members participating in a particular function under their purview (e.g.
    conducting a battle update brief, which falls under the S-3) prepare their Soldiers to fulfill their roles.
    Principal staff officers ensure their subordinate Soldiers and Leaders are trained to execute the following:

         • The tasks in the individual critical task list (ICTL) for their military occupational specialty
              (MOS) and skill level.

         • Tasks in the ICTL for additional skill identifiers (ASI) assigned to their soldiers in the unit’s
              table of organization and equipment (TO&E). For example, the C2 information integrator can
              perform all the tasks on the ICTL for ASI 5C.

         • Section level collective tasks, such as maintaining the section’s running estimate.
         • Individual and collective tasks associated with their role in functions outside the section such

              as:
               A section representative to the current operations cell.
               A member of the unit’s digital crew.
               A member of the CP crew.
               A representative of the section to a staff board, working group, or planning team.

    1-118. The important responsibility of principle staff leaders and their subordinate officers and NCOs to
    train their sections and cell personnel can be overlooked, particularly at battalion level where daily
    operational duties consume much of their time. Nevertheless, this responsibility is vital to successful
    operations of the staff in LSCO and should not be neglected. Battalion leaders must train and evaluate
    subordinate staff officers and NCOs to properly train them. They should give feedback to them on their
    performance of this function in performance and professional growth counseling per ATP 6-22.1.

    1-119. Training a battalion staff can be challenging. The commander, XO, and principal staff officers
    must balance daily operational requirements with training opportunities. The XO will leverage the
    principal staff officer’s section and cell NCOs in charge (NCOICs), and Headquarters and Headquarters
    Company (HHC) command team to conduct many aspects of the tables below. However, at a minimum
    tables V and VI must be performed as a unit to achieve the desired level of training proficiency. Once
    the staff is certified, they can proceed to unit training in Tables VII through X.

    1-120. Tables I through V are designed to train the battalion staff at the individual, section, and cell
    levels. Tables I-II occur on an on-going basis as Soldiers and officers join the unit. Table V is the
    rehearsal, as the headquarters elements come together to work as a collective unit. The commander
    certifies the staff on Table VI. There are no external attachments or liaisons required for the execution
    of Tables I through VI. The XO is the authority, as determined by the commander, to transition the staff
    through tables I through V. The commander certifies the staff using Table VI for transitioning to the
    more advanced Tables VII through X.

    1-121. During Tables VII – X, the staff in all the unit’s CPs function as one C2 system to conduct the
    operations process and support the commander’s decision making. To do this successfully, all staff
    members must understand the roles and functions of not just their section and CP, but of all sections and
    CPs in the C2 system.

    1-122. The tables are written so the commander can retrain the staff to overcome skill atrophy or
    personnel turnover before a future training event. For example, the commander can use an upcoming
    STAFFEX or STX to retrain the staff on Tables III through V to achieve a greater level of proficiency
    before a CPX is to be conducted in a few months. The tables are meant to be flexible for the commander
    to train the staff to a level of proficiency needed to conduct LSCO.

    1-123. Once the commander certifies the staff at Table VI, the staff receives an OPORD/FRAGORD for
    the battalion’s EXEVAL. This order is used throughout tables VII-X to drive training.

     1-124. Upon completion of Table X and the formal & informal AARs conducted by the EXEVAL team,
     leaders of staff sections and cells, CPs, and the digital crew should conduct internal sessions with their
     Soldiers to identify areas for retraining or other corrective actions such as making changes in SOPs.
     Commanders should include designated time for these activities in the training schedule.

TABLE I: ORGANIZE AND ACCLIMATE THE STAFF TO THE
ORGANIZATION

     1-125. This table is the introduction of the staff members to the unit. This table assumes that all officers,
     NCOs, and Soldiers will come to the unit with a basic proficiency in their individual MOS skills.
     Principal staff officers can use programmed training time to retrain job skill deficiencies. In Table I the
     staff section leaders further the training and develop initial staff training through basic understanding of
     duties, responsibilities, and the mission under the supervision of the principal staff officers and XO.
     Table I-II tasks are considered perishable skills and must be performed frequently to maintain basic
     proficiency. Table I-II can also be used to integrate new personnel upon arrival to the unit during the
     unit/commander orientation program. Figure 1-11 below establishes the inputs, tasks to be accomplished
     during this table, and the auditable outputs for the table.

UNDERSTAND UNIT DOCTRINE APPLICATIONS, STAFF DUTIES, RESPONSIBILITIES, AND
STANDARD OPERATING PROCEDURES

1-126. All members of the staff familiarize themselves on all command policies, procedures, and current
staff section and cell SOPs. battalion staff officers and NCOs familiarize themselves with the doctrine
relevant to the battalion conducting LSCO, including FM 3-0 and applicable FMs and ATPs for the unit
type. They also familiarize themselves with their specific branch/functional area responsibilities at the
battalion level. The staff section or cell leader develops a basic understanding of upcoming unit missions
and how those requirements translate into the basic skills section/cell members will need. Section leaders
further define the roles and responsibilities of each section member and determine current and future
expectations. Staff section leaders study and refine individual and section battle drills relating to their
duty position and the current organization. They train their Soldiers using individual and collective tasks
as defined by the appropriate proponent. For example, the S-2 conducts intelligence preparation of the
OE training for their section using Collective Task 34-SEC-3180, Perform Intelligence Preparation of

     the Operational Environment, provided by the Intelligence Center of Excellence. Refer to Individual
     Task 150-LDR-5007, Establish a Shared Understanding for building section and cell level teams.

ORGANIZE THE STAFF

     1-127. The XO and principal staff officers, after consultation and guidance from the commander, will
     direct the organization or reorganization of the battalion staff. The commander determines and the XO
     and principal staff officers clearly define extra duties. See Individual Task 150-C2-0000, Organize the
     Command and Control System as the Staff Officer. (See FM 6-0 for additional information.)

CONDUCT WARFIGHTING FUNCTION AND COMMAND POST ORGANIZATION

     1-128. The XO, with the assistance of the principal staff officers, organizes the staff according to
     commander’s guidance and using Individual Task 150-LDR-5013, Organize the Staff for Operations.
     Once the XO has established the staff structure, the principal staff officers, and section and cell NCOs
     ensure that all members of their staff elements understand their roles within the CP to which they are
     assigned, as well as how their section interacts with other entities of the entire staff. This applies to staff
     elements in all CPs, not just the main CP (TAC CP, combat trains CP, field trains, or any other alternate
     CP the commander establishes). Furthermore, each member of the staff must develop an understanding
     of the roles and responsibilities of other staff sections and cells and unit CPs. The officers and NCOs in
     charge of the battalion CPs consider manning, maturity, subject matter expertise or specialty of assigned
     personnel. They also consider CP requirements such as collaboration frequency, coordination
     requirements, facilitation for rapid exchange of information, inputs and outputs, and mission
     requirements for presentation to the XO with recommendations for staff organization.

BUILD STAFF RELATIONSHIPS

     1-129. The XO directs basic team building among the staff, and nests staff team building with the
     commander’s program and the higher headquarters. For example, the XO conducts teambuilding for the
     staff by conducting morning physical training together periodically.

     1-130. Typically, senior level staff members conduct team building events independently of most of the
     staff, but together with senior company level leaders. Additionally, senior level staff members build
     unity among staff sections by holding combined team building events.

     1-131. Battalion level team building events will have consistent employment of professionalism and
     interpersonal skills that foster effective open communication and builds unity within the command.
     Using a variety of venues and techniques which allow team members to contribute through observable
     means is essential to relationship building. Feedback mechanisms are established to promote interaction
     or prevent conflict within the staff. See Individual Task 158-LDR-5001, Develop Cohesive Teams
     Through Mutual Trust, and Collective Task 71-TM-5004, Develop a Cohesive Team Through Mutual
     Trust.

DEVELOP NONCOMMISSIONED OFFICERS AND SOLDIERS

     1-132. Section leaders will ensure that NCOs and Soldiers are developed to accomplish their mission
     and can provide their expertise and input to the operations process. NCO and Soldier development is
     grounded in the unit professional development program. The program must combine developmental
     expectations with the projected mission requirements the organization will experience. The section
     leader will use every opportunity to seek appropriate technical/skill qualification schools, standardized
     training with proficiency assessments, as well as any additional training opportunities that may benefit
     the NCO, each Soldier, and the organization. Refer to Individual Task 158-LDR-8003, Conduct Initial
     Assessment for a Leadership Position.

     1-133. The XO continues to supervise the principal staff officers as they refine the basic and intermediate
     skills of their section and its members. Principal staff officers have primary responsibility for this table,

DEVELOP RUNNING ESTIMATES

1-134. As part of each staff section understanding their role within the headquarters, the principal staff
officer and section leaders will train their sections on the purpose behind and the development of running
estimates: to record information, provide expert analysis/opinions and to provide recommendations that
will help the commander make decisions. Running estimates should include analyzed information that
pertains to the operation and is of value to the commander, not raw data. Running estimates always
include recommendations for anticipated decisions. Leaders use Collective Task 71-BN-5144, Develop
a Running Estimate, Individual Task 150-C2-5144, Develop a Running Estimate, and tasks from their
corresponding WFF proponent, such as 63-BN-4007, Develop Sustainment Running Estimate for the S-
4 section. Staff leaders will find Individual Tasks 150-C2-5129, Analyze the Mission Variables, and 150-
C2-5137, Analyze the Operational Variables, useful in this training as well.

CONDUCT THE MILITARY DECISION-MAKING PROCESS

1-135. Principal staff officers review with their section its roles and contributions to the MDMP. Leaders
run their sections through drills using the OE selected by the commander. Leaders ensure the training
enhances the section’s thoroughness, clarity, sound judgment, logic and professional knowledge to
understand situations, develop options to solve problems, and make recommendations.

1-136. Once the section gains basic proficiency in MDMP, the principal staff officer or section leader
will run abbreviated MDMP drills and scenarios based on a time constrained environment. The leader
will use the collective tasks listed here to ensure the section understands and fulfills their duties and
responsibilities for MDMP. It is not intended for the XO to integrate all the sections into a full MDMP

Chapter 1

     at this time. Staff officers use Collective Task 71-BN-5111, Conduct the Military Decision-Making
     Process to ensure they understand the section’s roles. Staff personnel participating in MDMP refer to
     Individual Tasks 150-C2-5110 through 150-C2-5121 for detailed guidance on the conduct of each step
     in the process. Individual staff sections also refer to their proponent training products for more tailored
     training.

     1-137. The outcome of this element of Table II is that staff section personnel know what products and
     inputs they contribute to the MDMP according to their unit SOP and are prepared to produce them once
     the staff begins collective MDMP.

CONDUCT THE RAPID DECISION-MAKING AND SYNCHRONIZATION PROCESS

     1-138. The rapid decision-making and synchronization process (RDSP) is a technique that commanders
     and staffs commonly use during execution when they recognize that reality does not match assumptions
     made during planning (see Individual Task 150-C2-5142, Identify Variances). While identified here with
     a specific name and method, the approach is not new; its use in the Army is well established.
     Commanders and staffs develop this capability through training and practice. The RDSP facilitates
     integration and synchronization of the WFFs to address ever-changing situations. The RDSP is based on
     an existing order and the commander’s priorities as expressed in the order. The most important control
     measures developed during RDSP are the draft proposed commander’s intent, concept of operations, and
     CCIRs. Refer to FM 5-0 and Collective Task 71-BN-5002, Conduct Rapid Decision-Making and
     Synchronization Process.

REVIEW COMMANDER’S DECISION SUPPORT TEMPLATE AND DECISION SUPPORT
MATRIX

     1-139. The decision support template (DST) and the decision support matrix (DSM) provide the
     projection of where and when a decision must be made based on expected events at specified locations
     with stated criteria and planned friendly actions in a narrative form. The battalion includes standard
     formats for decision support materials in the unit SOP, and tailor those products to the situation at hand.

     1-140. The DST graphically represents decision points and projected situations, and indicates when,
     where, and under what conditions a decision is most likely to be required to initiate a specific activity or
     event. It contains time phase lines, named areas of interest, target areas of interest, and decision points.

     1-141. A DSM is a written record of a war-gamed COA that describes decision points and associated
     actions at those decision points. It lists decision points, locations of decision points, criteria to be
     evaluated at decision points, actions that occur at decision points, and the units responsible to act on the
     decision points. It also lists the units responsible for observing and reporting information affecting the
     criteria for decisions. Refer to Collective Task 71-BN-5009, Develop a Decision Support Template, and
     Individual Tasks, 150-C2-5111, Conduct the Military Decision-Making Process, 150-C2-5138, Develop
     a Decision Support Template, and 150-C2-5139, Develop a Decision Support Matrix.

     1-142. Battalion leaders can use DST and DSM from earlier missions as training aids while conducting
     Table II. During this table, and as part of the MDMP instruction, the principal staff officers will:

          • Run their sections through internal wargaming drills.
          • Instruct how their wargaming inputs result in outputs to feed the DST and DSM in COA

                development.
          • Instruct how the DST and DSM inform the commander for COA selection.
          • Train procedures their sections take once a COA is selected including:

                 Refining DST graphics over space and time as the situation develops.
                 Refining the DSM written record, to include decision points, over space and time as the

                     situation develops.

     1-143. In addition to the DST and DSM, commanders and staffs use a wide array of tools (orders,
     overlays, templates, matrices, lists, tables, and charts) to assist them in synchronizing operations and
     making decisions. A common tool is the execution matrix, a visual and sequential representation of
     critical tasks and responsible organizations by time. An execution matrix could be for the entire force,

---

              Command and Control Warfighting Function Training Tables – Battalions

     such as an air assault execution matrix, or it may be specific to a WFF as in a fire support execution
     matrix. Each principal staff officer and section leader will train on and incorporate tools specific to their
     warfighting function to assist the commander in decision-making. For a listing of these tools, see ATP
     6-0.5, appendix D.

ASSESS THE CURRENT SITUATION AND OPERATION

     1-144. Each principal staff officer/section leader will use the proponent T&EOs for assessment tasks to
     manage and train their section on the use and application of these assessments. Refer to Individual Task
     150-C2-5130, Assess the Tactical Situation, to train on formal assessments.

CONDUCT SECTION REHEARSALS

     1-145. Table II rehearsals begin at the section level. Principal staff officers ensure that their section and
     cell members thoroughly understand their role in section/cell operations through rehearsals. The leader
     identified the tasks the section must be prepared to perform during Table I. In Table II they train and
     practice the section in their performance of them. The section/cell members should be trained to execute
     assigned tasks with the other shift members they will be assigned with during operations. As the shift
     teams gain proficiency leaders can increase the difficulty by taking out shift members or increase overall
     team competency by swapping members from one shift to the other during new repetitions. Refer to
     Collective Task 71-BN-5122, Perform a Rehearsal, and Individual Task 150-C2-5122, Facilitate a
     Rehearsal.

VERIFY SECTION OUTPUTS

     1-146. The XO ensures that all sections and cells account for respective, predetermined outputs per unit
     SOP. These outputs come in all types and serve various purposes, including those that provide direction
     to subordinate units; inputs to other staff processes/procedures within the battalion; and those that inform
     or drive processes for higher, adjacent, supporting, and supported headquarters. All outputs must lead to
     a better understanding of a situation and facilitate steps towards a decision. Examples of these can
     include a daily threat update from the S-2 that is an input to other staff sections and processes, and a
     sustainment forecast over space and time that includes friendly losses from the S-1 that informs the unit
     DSM and the battalion personnel replacement process. Refer to Individual Task 150-LDR-5011,
     Establish Boards and Working Groups.

DEVELOP SECTION TRAINING PLANS

     1-147. Staff leaders must have a comprehensive understanding of their respective section/cell
     requirements and inputs/outputs and have identified the training required for their section to be
     responsive to the commander’s decision-making process. The C2TT and the unit CATS should prove
     helpful to the staff leader in identifying tasks to train. The XO, in coordination with the S-3 and
     headquarters company leaders, provides guidance and direction for section/cell leaders to develop a
     training plan that focuses on the needs of their section, its designed capabilities, the mission, and the
     commander’s requirements. The S-3 captures these training plans in the overall battalion training
     schedule. Once all sections have formalized their internal training plans, the S-3 develops a unit training
     plan to present to the commander. The commander provides guidance, and the S-3 and staff leaders will
     adjust the plan until approval is received and the training plan is published. Refer to Individual Task
     150-COM-7170, Write the Unit Training Plan Operation Order.

TABLE III: ESTABLISH STAFF AND CELL PROCESSES AND
INTEGRATE WARFIGHTING FUNCTIONS

     1-148. Table III begins the effort of integrating the staff by the XO forming CP cells. This table also

     aspects of the MDMP. This table can be performed in a garrison environment. Figure 1-13 3 establishes
     the inputs, tasks to be accomplished during this table, and the auditable outputs for the table.

ORGANIZE THE STAFF

     1-149. The XO organized the various sections into functioning CP(s) in Table I and II. Table III begins
     with the commander and XO reviewing the results of Table II and deciding if any changes to earlier
     organizational decisions should be made. Of note, not all battalions will have the full range of
     warfighting functions nor a large enough staff to establish planning cells and may have inexperienced
     staff officers and NCOs working in their first assignment to a staff. The commander and XO establish
     CP cells based on these factors, the unit MTO&E, and its capabilities and mission. They refer to FM 6-
     0 and the doctrine for their unit on establishing these cells. The XO, principal staff officers, and section
     leaders use Individual Task 150-C2-0000, Organize the Command and Control System as the Staff
     Officer.

VALIDATE WARFIGHTING FUNCTIONS

     1-150. As the unit receives new members to the staff or the staff realigns for operational reasons, the XO
     will validate each of the unit’s WFFs. The individual tasks covering each WFF are listed in the next
     paragraph. The XO verifies adherence to SOPs, the commander’s intent, orders from higher
     headquarters, and operational requirements. This publication will not get into the finer details of what
     actions each staff section performs or how they are to perform them. For example, the commander wants
     the S-2 section to conduct more detailed intelligence integration training based on an influx of additional
     personnel. The S-2 consults the unit CATS and finds Task Set (TS) 52-TS-8131, Integrate Intelligence

34                                                              TC 6-0.2                                 30 July 2025


---

                                        Command and Control Warfighting Function Training Tables – Battalions

     Activities in Support of Operations. The commander sees that within that TS there are several tasks to
     train the intelligence section, to include:

          • 34-SEC-3171, Plan Integration into the Intelligence Architecture.
          • 34-SEC-3171, Plan Integration into the Intelligence Architecture.

     1-151. The CATS displays the recommended number of times and hours that the sections are to train on
     the task set to gain the desired proficiency (crawl/walk/run). The XO uses the following individual tasks
     for validating each WFF (for those functions represented on the unit staff):

          • 150-C2-0001, Validate the Movement and Maneuver Warfighting Function Staff.
          • 150-C2-0002, Validate the Intelligence Warfighting Function Staff.
          • 150-C2-0003, Validate the Fires Warfighting Function Staff.
          • 150-C2-0004, Validate the Sustainment Warfighting Function Staff.
          • 150-C2-0005, Validate the Command and Control Warfighting Function Staff.
          • 150-C2-0006, Validate the Protection Warfighting Function Staff.

CONDUCT THE MILITARY DECISION-MAKING PROCESS

     1-152. The MDMP helps leaders apply thoroughness, clarity, sound judgment, logic, and professional
     knowledge to understanding situations, developing options to solve problems, and reaching decisions.
     This process helps commanders, staffs, and others think critically and creatively while planning. In Table
     III, the staff begins collectively training the MDMP using the scenario and higher headquarters order
     that is used during certification in Table VI. Refer to FM 5-0, Collective Task 71-BN-5111, Conduct the
     Military Decision-Making Process, and Individual Tasks 150-C2-5110 through 150-C2-5121 for the
     steps and procedures used during MDMP.

     1-153. All the following steps of the MDMP should be conducted in accordance with the unit SOP.

CONDUCT RECEIPT OF MISSION

     1-154. The S-3 uses the OPORD issued for conducting Table VI (later) to lead the staff through the steps
     of receipt of mission including alerting the staff, gathering the tools, updating estimates, conducting an
     initial assessment, receiving the commander’s guidance, and issuing a warning order using Individual
     Tasks 150-C2-5110, Conduct Receipt of Mission, and 150-C2-5117, Prepare a Warning Order.

CONDUCT MISSION ANALYSIS AND INTELLIGENCE PREPARATION OF THE
OPERATIONAL ENVIRONMENT

     1-155. Commanders and their staffs (informed by subordinate and adjacent commanders and by other
     partners) gather, analyze, and synthesize information to orient themselves on the current conditions of
     the OE. The commander and staff conduct mission analysis to better understand the situation and
     problem, and identify what the command must accomplish, when and where it must be done, and most
     importantly why—the purpose of the operation. IPOE is the systematic process of analyzing the mission
     variables of mission, mission, enemy, terrain & weather, troops & support available, time available, civil
     considerations, and informational considerations (METT-TC(I)) in an area of interest to determine their
     effect on operations. The IPOE process identifies critical gaps in the commander’s knowledge of an OE.
     Training time spent conducting thorough mission analysis and IPOE at this stage of the training tables
     will pay great dividends later. Refer to ATP 2-01.3 and Collective Tasks 71-BN-5112, Conduct Mission
     Analysis, and 34-SEC-3180, Perform Intelligence Preparation of the Operational Environment, for
     detailed execution guidance. Each contributing staff officer refers to Individual Task 150-C2-2210,
     Provide Input for Intelligence Preparation of the Operational Environment, when preparing their input
     to the IPOE.

RECOMMEND COMMANDER’S CRITICAL INFORMATION REQUIREMENTS

     1-156. As the staff begins work as a cohesive unit and prepares for the staff rehearsal, the XO leads the
     battalion on recommending CCIR to the commander. CCIR are information requirements identified by
     the commander as being critical to facilitate timely decision-making. Each staff section identifies and
     recommends FFIR and PIR to the commander to designate as the CCIR.

30 July 2025  TC 6-0.2  35


---

Chapter 1

     1-157. CCIR are established as part of the MDMP and refined throughout the conduct of operations. The
     staff uses Individual Task 150-C2-5113, Recommend the Commander's Critical Information
     Requirements.

CONDUCT A RISK ASSESSMENT

     1-158. Staff leaders begin risk management by conducting risk assessment in conjunction with mission
     analysis. Throughout the operations process, commanders and their staffs, use risk assessment to
     establish a basis to manage risks associated with hazards that have the potential to injure or kill friendly
     and civilian personnel, damage or destroy equipment, or otherwise impact mission effectiveness. See
     Individual Tasks 150-C2-5145, Conduct Risk Management, and Collective Task 71-BN-5145, Integrate
     Risk Management into the Operations Process. See ATP 5-19 for further description of risk management
     FM 5-0, Appendix G for a discussion of integrating risk management into the operations process.

ENGAGE IN COURSE OF ACTION DEVELOPMENT

     1-159. The battalion XO or S-3 leads the staff through COA development drills, ensuring each functional
     cell contributes to planning efforts within its functional responsibilities. The XO also designates the
     COA development roles and responsibilities of the special staff. The commander’s planning guidance
     for this step is important in COA development. Commander’s guidance must reflect an appreciation of
     the unpredictable and uncertain nature of the OE, and how to cope with ambiguity. It should include
     parameters for enabling subordinate unit initiative while establishing adequate controls based on risk.
     The staff employs the Table II step above on the DST and DSM to build initial decision products that
     they will subsequently use and refine in the wargame. The XO and staff members use Individual Task
     150-C2-5114, Engage in Course of Action Development.

     1-160. The planning team establishes evaluation criteria. The commander and staff use these criteria to
     measure the relative effectiveness and efficiency of one COA relative to other COAs. Evaluation criteria
     address factors that affect success and those that can cause failure. Criteria change from mission to
     mission and must be clearly defined and understood by all staff members before starting the war game
     to test the proposed COAs. Normally, the XO establishes weights based on the assessment of each
     criterion’s relative importance and the commander’s guidance.

CONDUCT COURSE OF ACTION ANALYSIS

     1-161. COA analysis enables commanders and staffs to identify difficulties or coordination problems as
     well as probable consequences of planned actions for each COA being considered. It helps them think
     through the tentative plan. COA analysis not only appraises the quality of each COA, but it also uncovers
     potential execution problems, required decisions, and contingencies. Section leaders train their personnel
     to contribute analysis for their designated function during the wargaming process. For example, the
     personnel section will contribute analysis regarding projected casualties based on the tempo and type of
     operation for each COA. Refer to Individual Task 150-C2-5115, Conduct Course of Action Analysis
     (War-Gaming), and ITSP 150-C2-5115 / 1.0, Wargaming, to guide their participation in comparing
     COAs for detailed execution guidance.

CONDUCT COURSE OF ACTION COMPARISON

     1-162. The staff conducts COA comparison as an objective process to evaluate COAs independently and
     against set evaluation criteria approved by the commander or the commander’s designated
     representative. The goal is to identify the advantages and disadvantages of each COA, compare COAs
     to determine cost and benefit, which enables recommending and selecting a COA with the highest
     probability of success, and then further developing a COA in an OPLAN or OPORD. Staff members
     should use Individual Task 150-C2-5116, Conduct Course of Action Comparison.

CONDUCT A COURSE OF ACTION DECISION BRIEFING

     1-163. After completing its analysis and comparison, the staff identifies its preferred COA and makes a
     recommendation. The staff then delivers a decision briefing to the commander. The XO highlights any
     changes to each COA resulting from the war game. At the end of the briefing the commander (or the XO

36         TC 6-0.2  30 July 2025


---

                      Command and Control Warfighting Function Training Tables – Battalions

     acting as the commander for training purposes) selects a COA that will carry the staff through Table VI;
     approves the mission statement and CCIR; and provides commander’s intent. Refer to Individual Task
     150-C2-5009, Conduct a Course of Action Decision Briefing, to evaluate this event.

CONDUCT COURSE OF ACTION APPROVAL

     1-164. After the decision briefing, the commander selects the COA to best accomplish the mission. If
     the commander rejects all COAs, the staff starts COA development again. If the commander modifies a
     proposed COA or gives the staff an entirely different one, the staff develops and analyzes the new COA
     and presents the results to the commander with a recommendation. After the commander approves a
     COA, the staff records the commander’s final planning guidance, including refined intent and approved
     CCIR. The staff issues another warning order for subordinate units per FM 5-0. Use Individual Task
     150-C2-5121, Conduct Course of Action Approval.

PREPARE AN ORDER

     1-165. The battalion is required to publish an OPORD as part of the operations process. The staff must
     be proficient in conducting the MDMP, and the OPORD reveals that proficiency. The length and detail
     of the order can vary due to time and the mission. Once the order is approved by the commander or S-3,
     the staff prepares and executes an OPORD brief based on commander’s guidance. They also distribute
     the order to higher, adjacent, lower/subordinate, supporting, and supported units. Adhere to the basic
     standards, as written in Individual Task 150-C2-5119, Prepare an Operation Order, and Collective Task
     71-BN-5119, Prepare an Operation Order.

DEVELOP KNOWLEDGE MANAGEMENT PLAN

     1-166. The ability to efficiently manage information and knowledge is essential to effective mission
     accomplishment. The volume of available information at the battalion level makes it difficult to identify
     and use relevant information. KM provides the means to efficiently share knowledge, thus enabling
     shared understanding and learning. During Table III, the staff, led by the KMO, conduct the first three
     steps of the KM process (Assess, Design, and Develop) to draft the knowledge management plan (KMP).
     Later in the training tables, the KMO will lead the piloting and implementation of the KMP, and will
     lead the KM working group to assess and update the KMP leading to the Validation Exercise in Table
     X. The staff will use Individual Task 150-C2-7654, Perform Knowledge Management, and Collective
     Task 71-BN-5330, Conduct Knowledge Management.

TABLE IV: SYNCHRONIZE COMMAND POST OPERATIONS

     1-167. Table IV begins the process of fully integrating the functional and integrating cells within the
     headquarters. The XO begins the more complex steps of conducting the entire operations process as well
     as manning the CPs. Table IV can take place during a STAFFEX or a similar training event for one of
     the battalion’s mission essential tasks. Table IV is designed to be scenario driven in a complex and
     dynamic OE, and against a hybrid threat. Figure 1-14 on page 37 establishes the inputs, tasks to be
     accomplished during this table, and the auditable outputs for the table.

    Figure 1-14. Battalion Staff Table IV: Synchronize Command Post Operations

INTEGRATE STAFF CELLS FOR OPERATIONS

    1-168. The XO uses the training exercise in Table IV to fully integrate the WFFs and integrating cells
    in the headquarters to enable synchronization for decision-making. The XO can use the battle rhythm
    for this event to conduct this integration. For this step within the table, the XO ensures inputs and outputs
    by staff sections are integrated and the sections/cells are not acting as independent bodies. The cells
    complement and feed the proper cell or working group for rapid and informed decision-making. The XO
    will adjust the organization as needed or as directed by the commander. See Individual Task 150-C2-
    5126, Integrate Staff Cells for Operations.

SYNCHRONIZE STAFF PROCESSES

    1-169. The XO, with the assistance of the principal staff officers and cell NCOs, synchronizes staff
    working groups, planning teams, and processes. When the process or activity being synchronized leads
    to a command decision, a board may be the appropriate forum. These working groups, boards, and
    processes take place throughout the operations process and as the mission and commander require. The
    staff will also participate in and provide input to the boards, working groups, and processes of the higher
    headquarters. The XO allows adequate time in the battle rhythm for working groups, boards, and
    processes to run their course so outputs can achieve the desired effects. See Individual Task 150-LDR-
    5011, Establish Boards and Working Groups.

ESTABLISH A BATTLE RHYTHM

    1-170. The battle rhythm is a deliberate daily cycle of command, staff, and unit activities intended to
    synchronize current and future operations. A headquarters’ battle rhythm consists of a series of meetings,
    briefings, and other activities synchronized by time and purpose. The battle rhythm provides structure
    and synchronization for managing a headquarters’ most important internal resource – the time of the
    commander and staff. As part of this synchronization, the XO establishes a staff battle rhythm. The battle
    rhythm must include all unit CPs, and account for the battle rhythm requirements of higher, subordinate,
    and supported units. However, the battle rhythm is not static. An effective battle rhythm is modified as
    the situation evolves to include the types of meetings with expected staff representation, outcomes, and
    delegated decision authority for particular purposes or functions. Considerations for an effective battle
    rhythm include:

         • Facilitates commander-centric requirements for touch points.
         • Leads to timely decisions by the commander.
         • Addresses daily, weekly, and monthly requirements.
         • Balances time allotted for discussion, synchronization, and production.
         • Facilitates interaction between the commander, staff, and subordinate commanders.
         • Supports building and maintaining shared understanding throughout the headquarters.
         • Establishes a routine for staff interaction and coordination.

    1-171. To ensure meetings are organized well and achieve what is intended, staff leads develop
    instructions for each meeting (sometimes referred to as “7-Minute Drills”) to include the following:

38                                                        TC 6-0.2                                30 July 2025


---

                                        Command and Control Warfighting Function Training Tables – Battalions

          • Purpose.
          • Frequency, duration, and location.
          • Medium (face-to-face, video teleconference, and others).
          • Expected participants (staff lead, chairperson, and members).
          • Required inputs (such as updated collection plan).
          • Expected outputs (such as approved target nominations).
          • Agenda.

     1-172. Commanders and XOs allow white space in the battle rhythm and avoid inundating the staff with
     too many meetings. The XO uses Individual Task 150-C2-5123, Establish a Battle Rhythm. Refer to
     ATP 6-0.5 and ITSP 150-C2-5123 / 1.0, Establish the Battle Rhythm.

CONDUCT REHEARSALS

     1-173. Rehearsals are the commander’s tool to ensure staffs and subordinate units understand the
     commander’s intent and the concept of operations. The primary purpose for rehearsals is to generate
     shared understanding of the commander’s visualization of the upcoming operation. They allow
     commanders, their staffs, and subordinate unit commanders to identify shortcomings in the plan not
     previously recognized (though the purpose is not to conduct wargaming again). Rehearsals also
     contribute to external and internal coordination as the staff identifies additional coordinating
     requirements. During Table IV, the XO will lead the staff in rehearsing the planned operation to perform
     the mission associated with the MET selected for the training scenario. The XO should lead the staff to
     practice all rehearsals designated by the battalion SOP (combined arms, fires, sustainment, etc.).
     Following the rehearsal, the S-3 will publish the results and refinements to the plan in a FRAGORD.
     Refer to FM 6-0 and Collective Task 71-BN-5122, Perform a Rehearsal, and Individual Task 150-C2-
     5125, Prepare a Fragmentary Order. While executing rehearsals, the staff should refine decision support
     products initially developed during planning per Individual Tasks 150-C2-5138, Develop a Decision
     Support Template, and 150-C2-5139, Develop a Decision Support Matrix.

PRACTICE STAFF BATTLE DRILLS

     1-174. A battle drill is a collective action rapidly executed without applying a deliberate decision-
     making process. A battle drill or SOP rehearsal ensures that all participants understand a technique or a
     specific set of procedures. While the battalion establishes its own battle drills in the SOP, FM 6-0 has a
     list of suggested battle drills for CPs.

REFINE THE PLAN

     1-175. The commander and staff continuously refine the plan during all phases (plan, prepare, execute,
     assess) of the operation. The commander maintains a personal situational understanding of the OE (the
     commander’s estimate) through battlefield circulation, monitoring voice networks, and a variety of
     updates from the staff and subordinate leaders. The commander provides direction on key activities for
     the staff to monitor that may require additional refinement or adjustments to the plan. Refining the plan
     typically takes place when new information validates or invalidates assumptions made during planning,
     intelligence confirms or denies enemy actions, or the status of friendly forces changes as the situation
     develops. The XO leads the staff through a refinement of the plan and the mission objectives contained
     therein. The S-3 will publish approved refinements to the plan in FRAGORDs to the battalion. The XO
     will use Individual Task 150-C2-5124, Refine the Plan, and Collective Task 71-BN-5132, Perform
     Planned Actions, Sequels, and Branches

ESTABLISH THE COMMAND POST

     1-176. A CP is designed to help the commander exercise C2. Depending on the type of battalion, the
     unit will have a main CP and may have a TAC CP (or simply TAC), a combat trains CP, a field trains
     CP or may have other alternate CPs. The staff is organized into functional cells and staff sections in each
     of the battalion CPs to facilitate coordination and promote efficiency. These provide the battalion
     commander flexibility in arranging the battalion’s C2 nodes within the battalion’s assigned area.

30 July 2025  TC 6-0.2  39


---

Chapter 1

     1-177. The commander determines the sequence of deployment, timing of moves, initial locations and
     task organization for all the battalion’s CPs based on the mission variables of METT-TC(I) and the
     commander’s visualization. The commander task-organizes functional capabilities and personnel across
     the main and other CPs to match the commander’s concept for control of operations. The battalion TACs
     and main CPs deploy to separate locations or are consolidated based on the mission variables. Each CP
     performs functions implied by its organizational design and those tasks assigned by the commander. In
     the C2 paragraph (paragraph 5) of the battalion OPORD, the commander details any changes to the
     doctrinal authority, responsibilities, and task organization of the battalion CPs and any special
     instructions for them.

     1-178. Each battalion CP standardizes procedures associated with:
          • CP battle drills.
          • Shift-change briefings.
          • Operations update and assessment briefings.
          • Operations synchronization meetings.
          • Transferring control of the operation between CPs.

     1-179. The unit will use Collective Task 71-BN-0050, Establish a Command Post. Refer to ATP 6-0.5.

FORMALIZE SHIFT CREWS

     1-180. Once the CPs are established and manned, the XO will formalize and recommend shift crews to
     the commander. When organizing shifts, the XO and principal staff officers must consider the duties and
     responsibilities inherent to an area of expertise or WFF required for that specific CP in relation to time,
     space, and other battlefield conditions. The commander adjusts or confirms the shift crews early in the
     C2TT so crews can gain familiarity with one another and their shift leader. The XO will ensure crews
     understand the inputs, outputs, and battle rhythm to support the commander. The XO will ensure the
     formalized shifts have ample opportunity to exercise ‘reps and sets’ to build shift proficiency. Refer to
     Individual Task 150-C2-5205, Lead a Shift Change.

     1-181. Unit manning documents for most battalion-sized units are designed to provide two 12-hour shifts
     to meet the requirement for continuous operations (ATP 6-0.5). Units organize available personnel to
     provide effective continuous operations. Establishing shifts provides a sufficient quantity of personnel
     to operate the CP and the required expertise to make decisions on major issues. Units may organize shifts
     using the standard shift, heavy or light shift, and stagger shift techniques described in ATP 6-0.5.

     1-182. Regardless of the method used, several considerations apply. The commander and key leaders
     are not placed on a duty shift. Personnel who do not work permanently in the CP are not integral parts
     of a duty shift. This includes liaison officers and any attached special staff officers who are unit leaders
     or commanders. Additionally, members of the command group and the tactical CP are not included.
     These personnel integrate into the existing manning schedules when present at the main CP for an
     extended period.

VERIFY COMMAND POST STANDARD OPERATING PROCEDURES AND FUNCTIONALITY

     1-183. Once the shifts and CPs are established, and the staff runs through the event, the section leaders
     and the XO will ensure that all SOPs are continuously updated based on changes to the OE AARs;
     lessons learned; the evolving needs of the commander; higher, lower, and supporting units; and the
     mission set. Commanders ensure that comprehensive and detailed SOPs are developed, maintained,
     trained, and used for all aspects of operations. In this way, operations become a matter of routine that
     are successfully executed in periods of stress and great demand. Refer to Individual Task 150-C2-5200,
     Conduct Command Post Operations.

TABLE V: CONDUCT STAFF REHEARSAL

     1-184. By conducting Table V, the XO will verify the staff is prepared to achieve the commander’s
     certification in Table VI. This rehearsal incorporates all cells and processes into a single training
     exercise. This rehearsal aligns with an already scheduled collective training event in accordance with the
     unit CATS. For example, the battalion is performing their first CPX leading to an EXEVAL. As budget
