CONDUCT COMMAND AND CONTROL INFORMATION SYSTEM OPERATIONS

     1-381. Digital crew members will use their C2IS to battle track, manage the COP, execute battle drills,
     maintain running estimates, conduct briefings, and manage the commander's information requirements
     in accordance with Individual Task 150-DMG-2012, Communicate using Command and Control
     Information System. At a minimum, the C2 information integrator requires the digital crew to conduct
     the following using assigned C2IS:

          • Receive OPORD.
          • Develop running estimates.
          • Support mission analysis with associated WARNORD distribution.
          • Support COA development.
          • Support a COA approval brief.
          • Develop and distribute an OPORD.

     1-382. Tables VII through IX lead to digital crews that are integrated with their supported staff who
     operate as a cohesive unit. Simultaneously, the staff and commander are synchronized with external units
     and liaisons. Each staff section and cell member understands the commander’s intent and is capable of
     leveraging their digital crew and C2IS to seamlessly support the mission. At the conclusion of Table IX,
     the commander leads an AAR, and the digital crew makes any final changes to the COP and digital
     portions of the unit SOP. Training shortfalls identified during Table IX are corrected before beginning
     Table X.

     1-383. The outcome of Table X is that the digital crew and staff work as a cohesive unit, led by the
     commander, with external augmenters and liaisons. The digital crew and C2IS perform the operations
     process seamlessly. Successfully completing Table X indicates that the command is prepared at an
     advanced level to conduct operations. The commander consults with the external evaluators and the
     evaluating headquarters commander and validates the digital crew as prepared to support the commander
     and staff to conduct the operations process during the conduct of LSCO.

                                               Chapter 2

  Command and Control Warfighting Function Training
                            Tables – Brigades

    This chapter describes training tables for Army brigade combat teams, functional and
    multifunctional brigades, groups, and similar units commanded by colonels and that have
    staffs and one or more command posts (CP) (hereinafter referred to generically as
    ‘brigades’ or ‘the brigade’). The command and control training tables (C2TT) for brigades
    are designed to develop and test the proficiency of four training audiences – commander,
    staff, CPs, and digital crew–at the basic, intermediate, and advanced levels. The tasks in
    each table are intended to replicate training under realistic conditions against all threats in
    all domains (air, land, maritime, space, and cyberspace) in the operational environment
    (OE). Progression through all Tables will reinforce perishable skills and refine
    organizational processes and procedures. This chapter initially discusses considerations
    and tasks the commander uses to be an effective commander. This is followed by the staff
    training tables that reflect how the staff supports the commander. The chapter then covers
    the tasks involved with training the CPs, and how the CP personnel support the
    commander. The chapter concludes with training the digital crew and how the digital crew
    supports the commander and staff.

SECTION I – INTRODUCTION TO CHAPTER 2

     1-384. Section I discusses the overview and scope for chapter 2.

OVERVIEW

     1-385. Chapter 2 assists brigade commanders in verifying proficiency in collective and individual
     training through a certification and validation process. The unit progresses through a common, standard,
     and commander-driven certification of the unit commander, staff, CPs, and digital crew. Validation of
     the unit’s command and control (C2) system occurs through an externally evaluated culminating training
     event (CTE) (usually the brigade’s external evaluation (EXEVAL). Refer to Appendix D for a
     cumulative listing of tasks and drills that are identified within this publication.

SCOPE

     1-386. The tables in this chapter apply to Army brigade headquarters. Given the broad variety of brigade-
     sized units that this chapter covers, it is likely that some of those units will lack staff structure or
     equipment to execute selected tasks in some of these tables. For example, an Army field support brigade
     CP is not designed to be mobile and may not displace, or a theater signal brigade may not actually deploy
     its headquarters into a theater, so it wouldn’t necessarily need to secure its CP while conducting C2. In
     such cases, brigade commanders would discuss which tasks to perform during each table – and in
     particularly during their Table X Validation Exercise – with their higher headquarters commander.

     1-387. The C2TTs are closely aligned with the brigade level C2 functional Combined Arms Training
     Strategy (CATS). The exercises mentioned in the C2TTs are meant to nest with collective training the
     unit is already performing. A commander does not need to schedule a training event outside the scope
     of the unit CATS solely for purpose of completing the C2TT. However, as resources, time, and other
     restrictions affect training, commanders can extend or repeat any of the tables to enhance unit readiness,
     based on their assessment of the unit’s proficiency.

     1-388. Given the current Army training resources being focused on live training mostly at the company
     level and below, brigade commanders will have to seek opportunities to train their staff, CPs, and digital
     crew as they provide oversight of company level training, and in some cases battalion FTXs. In some
     cases, that brigade-level training may take the form of providing stimulation to and reacting to responses

30 July 2025  TC 6-0.2  83


---

Chapter 2

     from companies or battalions conducting company through squad/crew level training. Mission training
     complexes (MTC) can provide commanders with support to command post exercises (CPXs) and staff
     exercises (STAFFEXs).

     1-389. This chapter details all levels of C2 warfighting function (WFF) training and certifications in
     detail. Table 2-1 summarizes how the C2TTs support C2 training and shows the progressive objectives
     for the brigade C2 system. Tables I-V guide the unit through basic proficiency of the listed tasks at the
     cell and section level. The four training audiences will typically train Tables I-V independently. Table
     VI is the certification for each of the training audiences (commander, staff, CP crew, digital crew), Tables
     VII-VIII reflect advanced task mastery at the headquarters level. Tables IX and X provide the framework
     for validating the C2 WFF proficiency of the brigade headquarters. The four training audiences will train
     together to progress through Tables VI-X.

1-390. Tables I through X for each training audience present a training strategy that supports the
progressive development of C2 WFF proficiency. All subordinate steps list individual and collective task
training and evaluation outlines (T&EOs) for each table. The tables are designed to support the
commander’s training plan through reinforcing repetitions and sets to achieve and maintain proficiency.
The tables and associated individual and collective tasks are designed to be trained concurrent with
training events already on the unit’s CATS – the brigade should not have to schedule separate events
specifically for executing the C2TT. Some CATS will have the C2TT already integrated into the unit
CATS. In other cases, units may have to manually schedule the C2TT into training events listed on their
CATS. See Appendix A for a discussion of how C2TT are incorporated into unit CATS.

30 July 2025                                                       TC 6-0.2                                   85


---

Chapter 2

     1-391. Tables I through VI are designed to improve and enhance individual skills and to establish limited
     collective task proficiency while the unit progresses towards exercising C2 in the conduct of large-scale
     combat operations (LSCO). They are the ‘crawl’ of C2 training. The different audiences may enter
     training tables I through IV at different points based on different proficiency levels (e.g. the staff had
     more turnover than digital crew and starts with Table I whereas digital crew enter at Table III). Similarly,
     the different audiences may proceed through these tables at different paces as long as they all compete
     Table IV before beginning to work together in Table V.

     1-392. Tables I-II are designed to facilitate reoccurring training throughout the year to integrate and
     build proficiency in new personnel as they are assigned to the unit. Tables III-IV are designed to establish
     staff section-level and CP cell-level proficiency prior to their integration into the staff and unit CP(s).

     1-393. Tables V-VI are structured for the commander to evaluate, assess, and certify their organization
     while conducting mission essential tasks (METs) or assigned mission METs. Tables V and VI can be
     accomplished in the same CPX by designating two to three days for Table V, taking a break for after-
     action reviews (AAR) and adjusting, then completing the CPX with Table VI.

     1-394. Commanders or staff leaders can choose to run Tables III-IV in a single event or across several
     events. For example, the brigade commander may use a STAFFEX prior to a CPX to exercise the
     headquarters through the first five tables on the first two days of the STAFFEX and then retrain Table
     IV under tougher conditions on the third and fourth day.

     1-395. Table V should be conducted as a CPX under the same conditions the commander will certify the
     staff in Table VI. Leaders conduct section / cell level AARs to ensure their teams are ready for
     certification.

     1-396. Once staff leaders have certified personnel in all shifts of their respective sections, cells, CPs and
     digital crews on their respective Table V requirements, the unit conducts Table VI simultaneously. The
     commander certifies that the staff, CPs, and digital crew are proficient on Table VI collective tasks, and
     that the entire C2 system as a whole is prepared to conduct ‘walk’ level training in tables VII and VIII.
     Table VI should be conducted as a CPX with the C2 system functioning in 24-hour operations with
     multidomain threats.

     1-397. Tables VII through X build on unit competency and require inclusion of supporting and supported
     units, augmentees, unified action partners, or liaisons (as required by the mission or the OE). Tables VII-
     IX incorporate the full complement of echelon appropriate associated assets, a rigorous and dynamic
     OE, and the stresses typically experienced during CTEs or deployments. mission training complexes
     (MTCs) can assist the brigade with replicating higher, lower, supporting, or supported units or other
     friendly entities in the OE.

     1-398. The figures at the beginning of each training table discussion for each training audience
     summarize the key inputs, performance elements, and outputs for each training table. The remainder of
     this chapter lays out the training tables for the four training audiences in greater detail in the following
     order:

          • The commander training tables.
          • The staff training tables.
          • The CP training tables.
          • The digital crew training tables.

SECTION II – BRIGADE COMMANDER TRAINING TABLES

     1-399. Section II discusses the command and control training tables for the brigade commander.

COMMAND AND CONTROL TRAINING TABLES FOR THE
BRIGADE COMMANDER

     1-400. Commanders can use tables I through V for their own professional development and as a resource
     in counseling subordinate commanders. Many of the tasks in the early tables are designed to complement

86         TC 6-0.2  30 July 2025


---

                         Command and Control Warfighting Function Training Tables – Brigades

     the Center for Army Leadership Publication, Army Handbook for Leadership Transitions. Table VI is
     the commander certification training table. Brigade commanders certify themselves on Table VI and
     then, under the counseling and assessment of their evaluating commander, validate that certification in
     Table X. For example, the 82d Combat Aviation Brigade (CAB) commander performs Table X during
     a brigade FTX with external evaluators selected by the 82d Airborne Division commander. Based on the
     EXEVAL outcomes, discussion with the division commander, and AAR results, the 82 CAB commander
     validates the unit’s C2 system.

     1-401. Brigade commanders complete Tables I-VI at a pace consistent with the unit’s training plan,
     mission variables, and the evaluating commander’s guidance. Once commanders certify themselves and
     the other audiences in Table VI, the four training audiences transition into Tables VII-X together.

SOP        Standard Operating Procedures

   Figure 2-1. Brigade Commander Table I: Establish Organizational Business Rules and
                                              Develop Training Strategy

DEVELOP COHESIVE TEAMS THROUGH MUTUAL TRUST

     1-403. Teambuilding is an essential ingredient when assembling any organization. However, building
     the team is a commander centric responsibility driven by perceptions and perspectives shaped by their
     schooling, personal experiences, personality, and military maturity with consideration of the staff’s
     knowledge, skills, attributes, and capacity. Within time and resource constraints, the commander
     develops the entire team and encourages leadership within the command to conduct team building within
     their organizations, sections, cells, working groups, and warfighting functions. See Individual Task 158-
     LDR-5001, Develop Cohesive Teams Through Mutual Trust.

EMPLOY APPLIED CRITICAL THINKING

     1-404. Applied critical thinking is the deliberate process of applying tools and methodologies to critically
     review problems by asking better questions, deconstructing arguments, examining analogies,
     challenging assumptions, and exploring alternatives. During the first weeks of command, commanders

30 July 2025                                    TC 6-0.2                                                                              87


---

Chapter 2

     teach subordinates to understand their leadership style and how they best absorb information. The forms
     and methods in which they prefer information to be presented can change given the wide-ranging variety
     of responsibilities of the commander. Examples of this include military decision-making process
     (MDMP), decision briefings, town halls, budgets, and program management. How information is
     presented to the commander affects how they visualize concepts, understand data, and avoid
     predetermined outcomes. To assist their staff in establishing the preferred communication methods, the
     commander conducts a self-awareness assessment and reviews the applied critical thinking performance
     steps to ensure their subordinates understand their biases and problem-solving expectations to provide
     insight into their critical thinking methods. Commanders challenge their staff and subordinates to always
     ask themselves the “so what” of any given piece of information received or delivered. As the commander
     and staff relationship matures, the commander conducts a reassessment of their preferred communication
     methods to ensure the staff is meeting expectations aligned with organizational mission, goals and
     growth. Critical thinking skills are covered in Individual Task 158-MC-8012, Employ Applied Critical
     Thinking.

UNDERSTAND UNIT DOCTRINE APPLICATIONS

     1-405. Brigade commanders will already have a thorough understanding of doctrine and policy. To
     successfully transition from Table I to Table II, the commander demonstrates an application of that
     understanding in the context of their organization. Further, the commander reviews and re-approves,
     amends, deletes, or adds policies and procedures appropriate to the organization that meet their
     leadership style and adhere to the Army Ethic. Commanders also review FM 7-0, regarding how to train
     their headquarters and the force as a whole. Refer to Individual Task 158-LDR-8000, Transition to a
     New Leadership Position.

ESTABLISH A LEADER PROFESSIONAL DEVELOPMENT PROGRAM

     1-406. Commanders are responsible for a leader development program and a climate in which learning
     can take place to build stronger subordinates. Leader development is a continuous and purposeful process
     that can include assigned professional readings, a leader professional development program, staff rides,
     and so on. The brigade commander schedules leader development activities that incorporate the mission
     command approach to C2 and discussion of how they envision responsibilities when conducting the
     operations process. These activities focus on the seven principles of mission command as well as the
     tasks and systems that develop and integrate activities enabling the commander to balance the art of
     command and the science of control. The underlying theme for the overall program is to build
     competency in current and future leaders and to facilitate a deeper understanding and practice of the
     mission command approach to C2 WFF tasks, and the C2 system that enables the successful execution
     of the C2 tasks. Commanders use Individual Tasks 158-MC-8009, Analyze Army Character
     Development; and 158-MC-8005, Conduct Self Development; and FM 6-22 as the basis for their
     program.

ASSESS ORGANIZATIONAL EFFICIENCY AND EFFECTIVENESS

     1-407. Organizational redesign must address structure, processes, interrelationships and the integration
     of the work effort. This systems approach allows leaders to comprehend the multiple factors that create
     the organizational context. See Army Handbook for Leadership Transitions, Organizational Alignment
     section. These activities determine how commanders organize, tailor, or adapt their individual staffs to
     accomplish the mission (FM 6-0).

     1-408. Commanders review their mission essential task list (METL), anticipated mission sets, and the
     staff’s capabilities to address and meet impending mission requirements. Commanders also determine
     how the environment impacts the staff's effectiveness and efficiency across the C2 system. Assessing
     the staff's collaboration capability enables commanders to promote unity of effort. Commanders monitor
     each staff element’s function, assign associated duties and responsibilities, and make other adjustments
     as needed within the commander’s authority to change the doctrinal organization of their staff. Directing
     changes to the headquarters' physical organization to enhance collaboration and selecting personnel to
     fill key billets may be among the most effective techniques to develop a high functioning staff. For
     example, a maneuver enhancement brigade commander directs representation from all S-staff sections

88         TC 6-0.2  30 July 2025


---

                    Command and Control Warfighting Function Training Tables – Brigades

     to occupy a single large office space for garrison operations to develop the team and create a learning
     environment. The commander uses Individual Task 150-LDR-5013, Organize the Staff for Operations,
     and FM 6-0 to assist in this effort.

ASSESS CAPABILITIES AND ESTABLISH COMMAND AND CONTROL INFORMATION
SYSTEM REQUIREMENTS

     1-409. Upon assuming command, the new commander considers establishing their command and control
     information system (C2IS) requirements early in the process to give the staff maximum time to adjust.
     Commanders at brigade level typically understand the systems within their organization but may be
     unfamiliar with the integration capabilities or limitations between the information systems. Analyze the
     brigade’s C2IS to identify critical system requirements for the mission, current problem set, or the OE.
     Commanders must understand the electro-magnetic signature, power generation requirements, and
     information requirements of their CPs. Further, they must understand the relationship to higher, adjacent,
     supporting, supported, and subordinate CPs; and the bandwidth requirements for data which must be
     pushed or pulled between CPs and other headquarters. Understanding interoperability between these
     systems is key. Commanders consider which systems are appropriate for each echelon and grouping of
     echelons. Refer to Individual Task 150-C2-5251, Integrate the Command and Control Network.

TABLE II: ESTABLISH THE TRAINING ENVIRONMENT

     1-410. It is essential that the commander review the items in Table II closely as the decisions that the
     commander makes in this table directly impact the actions the staff takes in their Tables I through IV.
     The commander may tailor this table as need or as time allows. Figure 2-2 below establishes the inputs,
     tasks to be accomplished during this table, and the auditable outputs for the table.

COMPLETE UNIT STANDARD OPERATING PROCEDURES REVIEW AND UPDATES

     1-411. The commander conducts an internal review of all standard operating procedures (SOPs) and
     policies. The commander further directs an SOP review by all staff sections to ensure the latest doctrine
     and policies, and the results of recent unit AARs are incorporated and practiced in accordance with the
     current mission set and OE. Refer to Individual Task 158-LDR-8001, Prepare to Transition to a New
     Leadership Position.

30 July 2025        TC 6-0.2                                                                                                        89


---

Chapter 2

ESTABLISH A COMMON VISION

     1-412. As commanders begin to develop an understanding of the organization and the OE they start
     visualizing the operation’s end state – in this case achieving C2 proficiency – and potential solutions to
     solve problems. Commanders refine visualization as their understanding matures. Visualization is
     typically articulated to staffs and subordinate commanders using a concise description statement of one
     to three sentences. This description facilitates shared understanding of the situation, mission, and intent.
     Commanders refer to FM 6-22 and ADP 6-0 for additional information as well as Individual Task 150-
     LDR-5018, Establish a Common Vision.

ESTABLISH COMMAND / LEADERSHIP PHILOSOPHY

     1-413. Commanders establish their command (or leadership) philosophy. This philosophy establishes
     the commander’s method for achieving personal and unit goals nested with the unit table of organization
     and equipment (TO&E) design and other resources. At the brigade level, the command philosophy
     focusses on the commander’s vision, goals, objectives, and performance measures and either reinforces
     or alters the existing organizational values. The command philosophy sets the organizational compass
     to effectively manage and control the systems that drive operations and sets the foundation for how the
     organization will create shared understanding. Commanders review assessments of the unit from peers,
     higher headquarters, and subordinate leaders when crafting their command philosophy. The command
     philosophy is informed by the unit’s standard METL. Commanders describe how they intend to achieve
     the principles of the mission command approach to C2 with their personal command or leadership
     philosophy to provide clarity to all subordinate commanders, leaders, and Soldiers.

     1-414. Commanders deliver their command philosophy in a place or venue of their choosing. Many
     commanders post their command philosophy in their annual training guidance. Other commanders
     deliver their philosophy verbally before the start of an exercise or operation. Refer to Individual Task
     150-LDR-5028, Establish the Mission Command Approach to Command and Control.

ESTABLISH PLANNING / TRAINING GUIDANCE

     1-415. Working with the brigade S-3, the commander reviews the current planning and training
     guidance, and training objectives for each event taking place - including how those objectives nest with
     the unit mission as a whole. Refer to Individual Task 150-LDR-5321, Establish Planning Guidance. The
     commander tailors that guidance to fit the mission set and the needs of their own command philosophy,
     vision, and intent. The commander must provide this guidance for the staff to develop appropriate
     products that enable the commander to achieve their desired end state. The commander then provides
     the updated planning and training guidance to the staff and subordinate leaders. The commander’s
     training guidance (CTG) can contain a variety of items to include the following (See FM 7-0, Appendix
     A, for a more comprehensive discussion of training guidance):

          • Commander-to-commander dialogues.
          • The command’s current training plans and strategies.
          • Changes in mission-essential task required proficiency, weapons proficiency, or collective live

                fire proficiency.
          • Publication of senior headquarters annual training guidance (ATG).
          • Major changes to training resources availability.
          • Changes in the OE, threat conditions, or unit regional alignment.

DRIVE CELL CONSTRUCT

     1-416. Typically, a brigade already has fully functioning staff cells. However, the commander reviews
     each during this portion of the tables and provides guidance for functional and integrating cell
     framework. This guidance and initial framework will allow the staff to establish these cells. The
     commander outlines the purpose for each cell using the SOP and unit-specific Army training publications
     (ATP) as a guide and directs updates or changes to processes and procedures. Refer to Individual Task
     150-LDR-5251, Organize the Command and Control System.

90  TC 6-0.2  30 July 2025


---

                         Command and Control Warfighting Function Training Tables – Brigades

ESTABLISH INPUTS AND OUTPUTS FOR THE STAFF

     1-417. The commander ensures that all staff elements, processes, and procedures have established and
     approved inputs and outputs specific to their respective functions, enabling them to achieve commander
     expectations. Staff outputs generally involve a decision by the commander. For example, the brigade
     commander refines requirements for each staff element to contribute to the mission analysis led by the
     XO during MDMP. These inputs provide the staff planners information so they can create feasible,
     acceptable, suitable, complete, and discrete courses of action (COAs) to consider.

     1-418. Defining the inputs and outputs assists in product refinement, creates processes standard to the
     unit, and manages staff involvement which enables effectiveness and efficiency within the organization.
     Refer to Individual Task 150-LDR-5011, Establish Boards and Working Groups; staff inputs and
     outputs are synchronized through an effective battle rhythm. The commander or designated
     representative for each working group must periodically review approved inputs and outputs to ensure
     alignment with changing conditions and commander requirements to create shared understanding within
     and external to the organization. Commanders and the XO) reference ATP 6-0.5, FM 6-0, and Individual
     Training Support Package (ITSP) 150-C2-5123 / 1.0, Establish the Battle Rhythm to develop input and
     output guidance. Appendix C of this TC addresses battle rhythm training considerations in more detail.

TABLE III: ORGANIZE THE COMMAND AND CONTROL
SYSTEM

     1-419. Tables I-II are typically completed by the commander once in their command cycle. However,
     the commander revisits Table III-IV as the staff performs similar tables for retraining. An example of
     this is the commander runs Table I-VI in the first three months of command. However, the commander
     notices that the staff needs retraining on staff Tables III-IV. The commander uses an upcoming CPX to
     retrain these tables. The commander runs through Tables III-V a second time to better refine the products
     and outputs needed by the staff allowing them to build proficiency. Figure 2-3 below establishes the
     inputs, tasks to be accomplished during this table, and the auditable outputs for the table.

Chapter 2

COMPLETE COMMAND, CONTROL, COMMUNICATIONS, COMPUTERS, INTELLIGENCE
CONSTRUCT WITHIN THE HEADQUARTERS

     1-420. As the headquarters prepares for Table IV, Prepare the Headquarters for Operations, the
     commander must determine the purpose, interaction, organization, and construct of the command,
     control, communications, computers, and intelligence (C4I) systems within the CP. This is based on
     mission requirements, the commander’s intent, and the OE. Table I began the process of determining
     what information systems to use and the interoperability of these systems. In this table, the commander
     refines those decisions and allows the staff to establish the structure to support those decisions. The
     commander must weigh the mission, current SOPs, and their tailored command philosophy when
     refining guidance for the C4I within the CP. When designing the C4I, the commander must weigh the
     importance of mobility, operability, and survivability of the CP. Refer to Individual Task 150-LDR-
     5253, Manage Command and Control Information Systems.

ASSESS STAFF WRITTEN AND VERBAL COMMUNICATIONS SKILLS

     1-421. Commanders train subordinates to write and speak effectively through formal and informal
     opportunities. Commanders will still seek opportunities to assess the written and oral communications
     skills of the staff, but formal assessments can be delegated to principal staff officers. Commanders or
     designated representatives assesses staff and subordinates’ written and verbal communications using AR
     25-50 and Individual Task 158-LDR-5009, Communicate Effectively. Once assessments are complete,
     the commander and principal staff officers should help individual staff members to develop action plans
     to overcome weaknesses in their communications skills.

DEVELOP INITIAL COMMAND POST ARCHITECTURE

     1-422. Once the commander understands the C2IS requirements, they provide guidance to develop the
     architecture for each CP. The XO, operations SGM, knowledge management (KM) team, and S-6 section
     establish and ensure network connectivity for the main CP, the tactical CP (TAC) and any other CPs the
     headquarters employs. Establishing guidance for each CP and how it is employed is based on its specific
     functions. Common considerations include:

          • Administration operations.
          • Assessing operations.
          • Controlling operations.
          • Coordinating with internal and external organizations.
          • Building and maintaining situational understanding.

     1-423. In addition, the commander and staff give attention to the identified or projected threats in the
     electromagnetic spectrum (EMS). Given the maturity of the organization and the dynamic threat that is
     encountered, CP guidance will constantly evolve and is addressed when internal or external conditions
     change that will affect the purpose, function, survivability, or effectiveness of the designated CPs. The
     commander references FM 6-0 and the appropriate doctrinal reference for their unit and can assess this
     with Individual Task 150-C2-5202, Develop a Command Post Layout.

DELEGATE AUTHORITIES

     1-424. The unit will most likely have in place delegated authorities for legal, administrative, and other
     pertinent items. Each commander has a unique personal decision-making style and staff interaction
     preference. For example, one commander may prefer to personally make task organization decisions,
     where another prefers to delegate decision-making authority for task organization to the XO. The
     commander reviews these delegated authorities to ensure that they are in keeping with their intent and
     philosophy, the mission, and overall unit well-being. Commanders must thoughtfully consider the
     selection for delegation of decision-making authority for specific meetings and functions; commanders
     cannot attend everything and must allow their staffs to function and develop. Essential elements, at a
     minimum, in development of authority delegation include constraints, risks, and success criteria.

     1-425. When delegating authorities, a commander considers how to best employ the command sergeant
     major (CSM) during the conduct of operations. As the senior noncommissioned officer (NCO) of the

92         TC 6-0.2  30 July 2025


---

                    Command and Control Warfighting Function Training Tables – Brigades

     command, the CSM carries out policies and enforce standards for the performance of individual training
     and conduct of Soldiers. They give advice and initiate recommendations to commanders and staffs in
     matters pertaining to enlisted Soldiers. In operations, a commander employs the CSM throughout the
     area of operations (AO) to extend command influence, assess morale of the force, and assist during
     critical events. Refer to Individual Tasks 150-LDR-5320, Designate Succession of Command, and 150-
     LDR-5319, Delegate Authority.

TABLE IV: PREPARE THE HEADQUARTERS FOR OPERATIONS

     1-426. As commanders direct their staffs through the operations process, they provide guidance on the
     following topics:

          • Common operational picture (COP) requirements.
          • C2 system.
          • Battle rhythm.
          • CP organization.
          • Assessment of subordinates.

     1-427. Before any event, the commander establishes training objectives. This is a critical component in
     comprehensive preparation and establishing expectations prior to executing operations. Training
     objectives include identification of specified tasks with associated conditions and standards, as well as
     the expected proficiency rating the commander anticipates the unit to achieve by the conclusion of the
     training event. For example, the commander uses a STAFFEX to train the staff through Tables III-IV
     over four days in their third month of command. The training objectives for the event include: the unit
     achieve an overall Practiced (P) rating on two of the unit METs, all processes are validated, all retraining
     is identified by day three and completed by day four, and the headquarters is prepared to be certified
     through a CPX in three weeks. Assessments and training objective proficiency/deficiency is integrated
     into retraining or included in SOP adjustments prior to Table V.

     1-428. At this stage of training the commander should strive to achieve basic C2 system competency.
     As the unit becomes more proficient, later tables should be conducted under tougher conditions that
     force the commander to refine their guidance to account for them in making decisions on such things as
     CP dispersion, site selection, security considerations and others. Figure 2-4 below establishes the inputs,
     tasks to be accomplished during this table, and the auditable outputs for the table.

Chapter 2

PREPARE FOR DRIVING THE OPERATIONS PROCESS

     1-429. As the staff initially begins integrating and preparing for operations, the commander uses
     Collective Task 71-BDE-5100, Conduct the Operations Process for Command and Control, as a
     reference and training aid to direct and refine actions. Commanders are the most important participants
     in the operations process and use Individual Task 150-LDR-5100, Lead the Operations Process, as
     needed to guide them through their role. While staffs perform essential functions that amplify the
     effectiveness of operations, commanders drive the operations process through understanding,
     visualizing, describing, directing, leading, and assessing operations. To effectively prepare for driving
     the operations process commanders consider and relay to the staff how they envision building and
     maintaining situational understanding, apply critical and creative thinking, and encourage collaboration
     and dialogue. The commander understands the doctrinal roles and responsibilities of each staff section
     and command post, and develops a primary, alternate, contingency, and emergency (PACE) plan within
     and between CPs that will sustain the operations process throughout the exigencies of LSCO.

     1-430. The operations process is always conducted in conjunction with another task, normally drawn
     from the unit METL. To gain or maintain proficiency, commanders and senior staff incorporate
     supporting tasks that reinforce the major C2 activities performed during operations and allocate time for
     retraining as required. For example, the unit performs well in the majority of steps in MDMP but
     struggles in COA development. It is not required for the command to complete this task to the Trained
     (T) or even P proficiency level at this point in training (Table IV - step 5), but the staff must be poised
     to attain least basic task proficiency in the following table (Table V).

DIRECT COMMON OPERATIONAL PICTURE REQUIREMENTS

     1-431. The common operational picture (COP) is a display of relevant information through a shared
     visual workspace where commanders and staff can monitor operations, analyze data, share thoughts, and
     plan COAs. Once the operations process is initiated, the commander begins directing COP requirements
     to assist in making informed and effective decisions through a display of relevant information. These
     requirements are based on all elements of the C2 system, the capabilities of the unit, the mission, the C4I
     construct, mission variables, operational variables, and the commander’s personal leadership
     philosophy. In development of the COP, the commander must consider difficulties with C2 processes
     and procedures, digital and analog COP correlation, and network and information system integration.

     1-432. The COP is key to each step of the operations process: plan, prepare, execute and assess.
     Commander requirements for incorporation of a relevant and comprehensive COP into a CP can include:

          • How to display intent and planning guidance.
          • Description of posted relevant information.
          • Level and type of known and templated enemy threats, hazards, activities, and locations.
          • How the organization will collaborate with higher and subordinate organizations.
          • Use of planning and brief-back tools.
          • Types of control measures, graphics, and efforts used.
          • Level of battle tracking.
          • Integration of information requirements.
          • Display of significant activities SIGACT.
          • Display of key nodes and critical infrastructure.

     1-433. For specific guidance to the staff, commanders can review Individual Task 150-LDR-5315,
     Provide Guidance for the Common Operational Picture, and ATP 6-0.5.

ORGANIZE THE COMMAND AND CONTROL SYSTEM

     1-434. The C2 system enhances the commander’s ability to conduct operations. Individual Task 150-
     LDR-5251, Organize the Command and Control System, addresses the effort. This includes the
     arrangement of people, processes, networks, and CPs. A commander’s C2 system begins with people.
     Commanders base their C2 system on human characteristics (skills, knowledge, and abilities) more than
     equipment and procedures. The C2 system must be organized to:

94         TC 6-0.2  30 July 2025


---

                                         Command and Control Warfighting Function Training Tables – Brigades

          • Support the commander’s decision-making.
          • Collect, create, and maintain relevant information.
          • Prepare knowledge products to support the commander’s and other leaders’ understanding and

                visualization.
          • Prepare and communicate directives.

     1-435. The commander ensures that the headquarters establishes and rehearses all aspects of the C2
     system before the staff begins their final rehearsal for certification in Staff Table V.

PROVIDE BATTLE RHYTHM GUIDANCE

     1-436. While the battle rhythm is managed by the XO, it is commander-centric and must be directed to
     support the commander’s decision-making process. Either directly, or through the XO, the provides the
     criteria for development of a deliberate daily cycle of command, staff, and unit activities intended to
     synchronize current and future operations. This guidance includes the types or series of meetings with
     expected staff representation, outcomes, and delegated decision authority for particular purposes or
     functions. The battle rhythm enables:

          • A commander’s decision making.
          • A routine for staff interaction and coordination.
          • Interaction between the commander and staff.
          • Staff synchronization across time, space, and purpose.
          • Staff planning.

     1-437. The battle rhythm is a logical arrangement for the sequencing and timing of reports, meetings,
     and briefings based on the commander’s preference, higher headquarters requirements, and the type of
     operations (FM 6.0). The commander must describe or direct their required touch points to the staff (both
     internal and external to the organization) to further develop and refine the battle rhythm to save time and
     energy and prevent frustration. Once the commander gives the direction, they will continue to monitor
     and receive updates from the staff on the effectiveness of the battle rhythm. The battle rhythm is tailored,
     adjusted, and assessed throughout the C2TT and unit events to match the needs of the command, the
     mission, and the OE. The commander can use Individual Task 150-C2-5123, Establish a Battle Rhythm,
     and Individual Training Support Package (ITSP) 150-C2-5123 / 1.0, Establish the Battle Rhythm, for
     reference in forming the battle rhythm framework, as well as FM 6-0 and ATP 6-0.5 for a list of
     integration functions and inputs and outputs to consider.

REFINE COMMAND POST LAYOUT

     1-438. Throughout the event for Table IV, the commander will review, refine, and reassess the CPs’
     physical design to ensure that it meets the commander’s information requirements and provides for
     efficient CP operations. The physical layout of a CP has a significant impact on its functionality. A layout
     contributes to how efficiently information is passed from one staff element to another and how
     effectively sections communicate with one another. Commanders will analyze the mission to vet the
     layout or design of their CPs (to include positioning of personnel, information systems and displays,
     equipment, network cabling, and electricity requirements) to ensure it is appropriate for the OE. This
     arrangement may change over the course of operations as the situation changes to maximize efficiency
     and effectiveness of collaboration and coordination. In addition, refinement guidance criteria also
     include considerations for mobility, agility, and survivability for each CP. The following list will aid in
     designing the CP physical layout:

          • Staff integration and crosstalk.
          • Meeting and working group workspace.
          • Trafficability.
          • COP visibility.
          • Lighting and climate control.
          • The ease of information flow.
          • User interface with communications systems.
          • The positioning of information displays for ease of use.
          • Displaying complementary information on maps and information systems.

30 July 2025  TC 6-0.2  95


---

Chapter 2

          • Adequate workspace for the staff and commander.
          • The ease of displacement (setup, tear-down, and movement).

     1-439. The commander will continue to refine this design throughout the tables and upon notification of
     new mission requirements and by accounting for factors in the OE that influence the need for CP
     effectiveness and survivability. Refer to Individual Tasks 150-C2-5200, Conduct Command Post
     Operations, and 150-C2-5202, Develop Command Post Layout.

ASSESS SUBORDINATE LEADERS

     1-440. The brigade commander trains subordinate leaders throughout all the tables. In Table IV, the
     commander is afforded the opportunity to conduct a holistic assessment of their leaders working as a
     team towards a common goal. Unique to each commander, assessments are developed to measure
     performance levels and proficiency in craft. Of note, commanders throughout the Army are responsible
     for leaders/subordinate commanders two levels down. For direct assessment, the brigade commander is
     typically responsible to assess and certify battalion and separate company commanders, and their
     primary staff leaders.

     1-441. In Table III, the commander assesses basic written and verbal assessments of key staff leaders.
     The commander now focuses on building and developing their skills to be successful in all operational
     aspects of C2. The commander should develop an assessment plan that includes an evaluation of written
     directives, verbal communication of appropriate staff/subordinate mission requirements (including
     MDMP), leader actions, and routine operations observed in a variety of conditions over time. The
     certification of subordinate leaders takes place in Table V. Refer to Individual Task, 158-LDR-8003,
     Conduct Initial Assessment for a New Leadership Position.

REFINE TRAINING GUIDANCE

     1-442. Commanders provide pre-execution guidance to subordinate units early in the planning process.
     This ensures the staff meets the commander’s guidance throughout planning. This guidance helps keep
     subordinate leaders, planners, primary and special staff in synch with the commander’s vision for the
     event. Before the event begins, the commander establishes the desired training outcome, training
     objectives for the event (such as iterations, duration, and participation level) through planning guidance.
     When developing training guidance for each event, the commander reviews previous evaluations of the
     organization, identifies the overall purpose of the training, describes the expected performance, dictates
     the conditions under which the task(s) is performed, identifies the standards used to measure
     performance, and reviews all applicable T&EOs for each MET to be trained (for example, Collective
     Task 55-BDE-4800, Conduct Expeditionary Deployment Operations at the Brigade Level, when
     conducting a deployment exercise).

     1-443. When writing objectives use clear, direct language not indirect or passive words (like
     ‘understand’ or ‘familiarize’) and link the objectives to the event. Training objectives are tied to unit
     METL and achieving task proficiency. To avoid subordinate and staff confusion, commanders must be
     specific about what constitutes the expected/standard level of performance through simple, clear and
     concisely written objectives. For example, the brigade commander directs through their planning
     guidance that the upcoming CPX will focus on their ‘movement to contact’ and ‘conduct a defense’
     METs. The commander further directs that the staff be proficient to the P level on performance measures
     outlined in the MET T&EOs, that the training of all Tables III-IV be performed, and that the CP be set
     up in the brigade training area. The commander may refer to Individual Task 150-LDR-5321, Establish
     Planning Guidance.

TABLE V: DRIVE THE OPERATIONS PROCESS

     1-444. This table is conducted in conjunction with Table V for the staff, CP, and digital crew. Figure 2-
     5 on next page establishes the inputs, tasks to be accomplished during this table, and the auditable outputs
     for the table.

LEAD THE OPERATIONS PROCESS

1-445. Commanders are the most important participants in the operations process. Commanders drive
the operations process through the activities of understanding, visualizing, describing, directing, leading,
and assessing operations. Throughout the operations process, commanders apply leadership to translate
decisions into action. This is achieved through exercising appropriate levels of command presence by
sharing technical and tactical knowledge through experience with subordinates throughout the event as
the mission and situation dictate. The enduring effects of successful employment of command presence
provide a background for subordinates to realize how and when to adapt to achieve the commander’s
intent for mission accomplishment. In this table the commander certifies subordinate leaders and
applicable crews on the overarching framework for exercising C2 through the operations process. The
commander directs section/cell level AARs and that the staff conduct retraining as needed before Table
VI certification. Commanders can use the same event as outlined in Table IV or a different event based
on time and resources. Refer to Individual Task 150-LDR-5100, Lead the Operations Process.

DETERMINE COMMANDER’S CRITICAL INFORMATION REQUIREMENTS

1-446. Determining the Commander’s Critical Information Requirements (CCIR) is addressed in
Individual Task 150-LDR-5019, Determine the Commander’s Critical Information Requirements. These
CCIR are based on the unit’s OE, its mission, and the organization’s designed capabilities. The
commander develops CCIR to cover gaps in their own analysis and the staff’s analysis or to identify
critical information requirements to facilitate timely decisions. Thee CCIR allows the commander to
better understand and visualize the OE, define/redefine the problem, and inform planning guidance to
support time-sensitive decision-making requirements. However, CCIR are not focused solely on data or
isolated information but rather provide relevant understanding to enable mission accomplishment. Well-
developed CCIR, in combination with the commander’s intent, assist in accelerating allocation of
resources, guide and prioritize employment of collection assets/analysis resources, and aid in
information flow to create shared understanding. The two key elements are friendly force information
requirements (FFIR) and priority intelligence requirements (PIR). A CCIR is:

     • Specified by the commander for a specific operation.
     • Applicable only to the commander who specifies it.

30 July 2025                                    TC 6-0.2                                                                            97


---

Chapter 2

          • Situation dependent.
          • Directly linked to a current or future mission.
          • Time-sensitive.

     1-447. CCIR will change and mature with mission priorities and the OE. Commanders dictate the
     notification criteria and reporting hierarchy with respect to prioritization and specified recipients. The
     commander also follows a process to periodically review and update the CCIR. Commanders widely
     distribute the CCIR and revise them as the situation changes. Information is continuously collected. It
     may be delivered on a routine schedule or as requested. An information-push system pushes information
     from the source to the user as it becomes available or according to a schedule (such as through routine
     reports). An information-pull system supplies information as requested. Commanders and staffs
     determine how to use and integrate both types of systems. In addition to CCIR, commanders develop a
     wake-up or notification criteria list/matrix/worksheet separate from CCIR. CCIR may be considered
     wake up criteria.

ASSESS TACTICAL SITUATION AND OPERATION

     1-448. As the unit begins operating within its designed capabilities against its assigned mission set, the
     commander establishes how they will assess the unit’s performance of assigned tasks and progress
     towards mission accomplishment. One technique is to establish measures of effectiveness (MOE) and
     measures of performance (MOP) to measure progress towards mission accomplishment during the Table
     VI training event. Commanders can use Collective Task 71-BDE-5130, Assess Tactical Situation and
     Operation.

ASSESS PROCESSES AND PROCEDURES

     1-449. Though Staff Table V is designed for the XO to conduct a comprehensive assessment prior to the
     certification of the staff by the commander. The commander monitors this process and exercises
     appropriate degrees of command presence at key moments and decision points during the operations
     process to shape and prepare the staff for Table VI. While unable to devote all their time to planning,
     commanders follow the status of the planning effort, participate during critical events of the process, and
     make decisions based on the detailed work of the staff. The commander can also refer to Individual
     Tasks 150-C2-5200, Conduct Command Post Operations, and 150-C2-5130, Assess the Tactical
     Situation, to ensure members of the staff are following established processes and procedures, and
     formally changing them when necessary. At the conclusion of Table V, the commander directs any
     necessary updates or changes to identified processes and procedures before the progression to Table VI.

CERTIFY SUBORDINATE LEADERS AND CREWS

     1-450. The brigade commander trains subordinate leaders throughout all the tables, and in Table V, the
     brigade commander then certifies these leaders. Brigade commanders use the T&EOs associated with
     each task in Training Tables I through V. Brigade commanders can also direct additional certification
     tasks for subordinate leaders based on the uniqueness of their organizations. The commander is
     ultimately responsible for all certifications within their organization. The commander trains and
     empowers the XO and staff primaries to assist in certification duties. Of note, as leaders cycle in-and-
     out of leadership positions, the commander continuously assesses, trains, retrains, and certifies these
     leaders. Refer to Collective Task 71-BDE-0050, Establish a Command Post.

TABLE VI: ASSESS AND CERTIFY THE HEADQUARTERS

     1-451. This table is the certifying event for the commander and the headquarters. This table is conducted
     as a collective unit event during which the staff, CP, and digital crew execute their respective Table VI
     certifications while conducting MET training. The commander uses the tasks listed in each audience’s
     Table VI to certify them. The commander completes this table when the headquarters successfully
     completes all Table VI requirements. The end state of this table is realized when the commander certifies
     that they, the staff, CP, and digital crew are prepared to perform their respective roles and functions
     during the conduct of collective training. For example, the brigade commander uses a CPX to conduct

CERTIFY SUBORDINATES ON PREPARING AND OPERATION ORDER

1-453. The commander assesses the staff and the XO as they develop the Operation Order (OPORD)
using Collective Task 71-BDE-5119, Prepare an Operation Order.

CERTIFY SUBORDINATES ON EMPLACING AND DISPLACING THE COMMAND POST

1-454. Commanders assess the emplacement and displacement of the CP (for those CPs that must move
during the conduct of LSCO). The commander evaluates the headquarters using Collective Tasks:

     • 71-BDE-5200, Conduct Command Post Operations.
     • 71-BDE-5201, Displace the Command Post.
     • 71-BDE-5202, Conduct Transfer Control of Operations.
     • 71-BDE-5250, Maintain Continuity of Command and Control.

1-455. Commanders assess the establishment of the COP as the S-3 completes Individual Task 150-C2-

CERTIFY THE BATTLE RHYTHM

1-456. Commanders evaluate the functionality of the battle rhythm throughout the event making changes
or adjustments as needed. The commander assesses the XO using Individual Task 150-C2-5123,

Chapter 2

     Establish a Battle Rhythm, and the staff on executing the battle rhythm using 71-BDE-5125, Implement
     the Battle Rhythm.

CONDUCT AFTER ACTION REVIEWS

     1-457. Commanders are responsible for the training of their units. An AAR is the primary tool for
     providing feedback. Although leaders can conduct two types of AARs, formal and informal,
     commanders provide guidance to leaders on specific areas of interest, assessments, or feedback to gain
     insight of their organization. This guidance may include collaboration of staff sections, internal and
     external communications, reporting formats, synchronization, briefing techniques, use of the CPs, CP
     infrastructure, and so on. While the T&EO and task proficiency standards provide objective criteria for
     determining task proficiency, assessments allow leaders to consider the subjective nature of training.
     Leaders’ assessments combine their personal observations with other information from the staff to
     develop an overall assessment of the unit’s ability to accomplish its mission. Commanders uses
     Individual Task 150-C2-5133, Conduct a Formal After Action Review.

CERTIFY THE COMMAND POST

     1-458. The commander evaluates the unit’s C2 system on how well it enhances the commander’s ability
     to conduct operations using Individual Task 150-LDR-5251, Organize the Command and Control
     System. Commanders evaluate the four components of their C2 system: people, processes, networks, and
     CPs. Commanders evaluate these four components of the C2 system relating to:

          • Support the commander’s decision making.
          • Collect, create, and maintain relevant information.
          • Prepare knowledge products to support the commander’s and leaders’ understanding and

                visualization.
          • Prepare and communicate directives.

     1-459. The outcome of Tables I-VI is the commander has built a team, philosophy, and training plan
     based on the unit’s design, capabilities, and mission, and has certified that the headquarters is prepared
     to train collectively as one C2 system. As part of the entire training audience (commander, staff, CP,
     digital crew), the commander transitions to Tables VII-X. These tables are complex and stress the
     application of the C2 WFF. The headquarters integrates external elements such as liaisons and unified
     action partners. The tables provide a progressive framework for the commander in preparation for their
     unit’s CTE.

TABLE VII: DIRECT COMMAND AND CONTROL SYSTEM
INTEGRATION

     1-460. The brigade commander has the flexibility to organize the four components of the C2 system
     (people, processes, CPs, and network) to support their ability to make decisions and facilitate
     communication within the brigade headquarters and with higher, subordinate, adjacent, and supporting
     commanders. When training scenarios reflect a robust (versus an austere) OE, this table may include
     maintaining communications with other services, other governmental agencies, foreign forces, host
     nation agencies (if present), and international organizations as applicable. This table takes into
     consideration this interaction and information sharing requirements of internal systems as well as the
     collaboration and synchronization that is expected with all stakeholders. The commander can move back
     to the previous tables to retrain or recertify at any time they feel it would benefit the command.

     1-461. At the beginning of Table VII, the command will receive a FRAGORD from higher headquarters
     directing a change of mission from the activity in Table VI. This order will drive training in Tables VII
     through X. It includes the task organization the brigade will fight as when conducting its external
     evaluation. It also provides the commander information on the unified action partners who will
     participate in the exercise. The commander fully integrates multinational forces into the brigade and
     integrates other unified action partners appropriate to their role, function, and mission beginning in Table
     VII. Figure 2-7 on next page establishes the inputs, tasks to be accomplished during this table, and the
     auditable outputs for the table.

ORGANIZE PERSONNEL

1-462. The commander directs the organization of all non-organic personnel (liaisons, attachments,
augmentees, etc.) based on capabilities, mission, and the higher commander’s intent. The commander’s
designated representative will ensure all are assimilated into their positions in a posture that allows them
to contribute effectively. The commander uses Individual Task 150-LDR-5013, Organize the Staff for
Operations, to assist in this effort.

1-463. New personnel must establish lines of communications and physically meet with counterparts at
lower, higher, adjacent, supported, and supporting headquarters whenever possible. The transition or
establishment and maintenance of these personal relationships are important to maintaining situational
and shared understanding for the commander. Consideration for organizing includes the command and
support relationship and performance of three essential functions:

     • Supporting the commander through their area of expertise.
     • Assisting subordinate units through active collaboration to solve problems.
     • Informing units and organizations outside the headquarters with relevant information.

1-464. The brigade commander may delegate authority to manage the brigade staff to the XO; however,
commander oversight is still encouraged to ensure personalities, leadership traits, subject matter
expertise, and other requirements meet guidance for organizing personnel.

1-465. The commander, through designated representatives, also directs appropriate foreign disclosure
actions for information exchange with unified action partners as needed.

ENHANCE INFORMATION SYSTEM TRAINING GUIDANCE

1-466. The brigade has C2IS that collect, process, store, display, and disseminate information. This
includes a variety of hardware and software that enhance the commander’s ability to visualize the
operation and assist in decision-making. The foundation of any information system is the uninterrupted
flow and processing of information to support planning, preparation, execution, and continuous
assessment. The brigade has information systems that are interoperable, flexible, responsive, mobile,
survivable, and sustainable. Refer to Individual Task 150-C2-5251, Organize the Command and Control
Network.

1-467. Because of the additional personnel/attachments and mission requirements, the commander
reviews the established systems and makes changes to account for new interaction, collaboration, and

30 July 2025                                    TC 6-0.2                                                                      101


---

Chapter 2

     synchronization demands, as well as the needs of attached or assigned units/individuals, and the higher
     commander’s guidance.

     1-468. To perform advanced, complex, interactive training, the commander ensures the digital crew is
     certified and new shift personnel are incorporated through training event repetitions. The commander
     authorizes the XO and the knowledge management officer (KMO) to enhance the training of the Soldiers
     and supporting personnel operating the C2IS. Battle captains, KM representatives, common COP
     managers, and unit C2 information integrators (formerly Mission Command Digital Master Gunner
     (MCDMGs)) can provide guidance and leadership to the crews and lead them through command directed
     scenarios, and report progress back to the commander.

     1-469. The S-6/KMO and C2 information integrators, under the authority of the XO and the commander,
     can further tie into communication exercises (COMEX) or digital exercises being held by the S-6 as a
     time to enhance training opportunities for the staff and digital crew. For example, the brigade XO directs
     the information system operators to participate in the upcoming COMEX for the brigade and subordinate
     units. The digital crew and supporting elements leverage satellite time allocated for software updates to
     exercise their systems during the COMEX (such as practice pushing and pulling graphics to other units
     participating in the event). The C2 information integrator uses training objectives with clear performance
     measures for assessing the performance of all digital operators. The XO reports the assessment to the
     commander.

SECURE AND MAINTAIN NETWORKS

     1-470. Networks are formed when terminal devices and transmission media are interconnected with
     switching equipment to ensure that information (voice, imagery, data, or message) is transported to
     appropriate locations. The commander, working with the S-6, ensures that the network is both secured
     and maintained in compliance with Department of Defense (DoD) and Department of the Army (DA)
     regulations, unit organization/manning requirements, environmental conditions, unit SOP and directives
     from the higher headquarters. Essential elements include identification of authorized/prioritized users,
     access control policies/strategies, and information security training as well as system/software upgrades
     that enable interoperability and compatibility. In addition, developing a primary, alternate, contingency,
     and emergency (PACE) plan will mitigate short term loss of connectivity and maintain situational and
     shared understanding. The commander refers to Individual Tasks 113-367-5001, Implement Network
     Protection Measures; and 113-473-7007, Implement Network Security Measures within a System
     Architecture, as needed.

REFINE PROCESSES AND PROCEDURES

     1-471. In this table the commander refines and updates processes and procedures to organize activities
     within and between the headquarters, and flank, subordinate, supported, and supporting units. The
     brigade staff conducts the following seven processes: the operations process, the MDMP, the targeting
     process, the intelligence process, intelligence preparation of the operational environment (IPOE), the
     information collection process, and the KM process. The commander refines processes and procedures
     based on running estimates, AARs, and the commander directed assessment plan, which may include
     measures of effectiveness (MOEs) and measures of performance (MOPs). As updates and changes are
     made, the commander ensures those actions are captured in SOPs and published to the force. Refer to
     Individual Task 150-LDR-5251, Organize the Command and Control System, and Collective Task 71-
     BDE-5125, Implement the Battle Rhythm. Examples of typical brigade processes and procedures that C2
     information systems support include:

          • CP battle drills.
          • Shift-change briefings.
          • Operation update and assessment briefings.
          • Operations synchronization meetings.
          • Transferring control between CPs.
          • The battle rhythm.

                                         Command and Control Warfighting Function Training Tables – Brigades

EVALUATE COMMAND POST SIGNATURE

     1-472. The commander, with S-2, S-3, S-6, and protection cell input, considers the digital systems and
     CPs required to support C2 based on the mission set and the OE. They evaluate the unit’s electromagnetic
     signature based on enemy detection capabilities. The commander ensures that the unit is
     mitigating/minimizing its footprint while still maintaining C2. Mitigation measures might include
     limiting the amount of satellite time or reducing redundant information systems that create significant
     signatures.

     1-473. The commander ensures that cyber defense drills are being conducted and assessed throughout
     training events. These drills replicate a ‘near peer’ competitor in the OE as directed by the commander.
     Refer to Individual Task 113-25E-3003, Define the Electromagnetic Operational Environment (EMOE),
     and GTA 11-11-001, Emission Control Planning Aid.

ASSESS TACTICAL SITUATION AND OPERATIONS

     1-474. As the unit begins performing METs within designed capabilities against the newly assigned
     mission set, the commander refines their assessment plan to measure mission accomplishment.
     Commanders can use Collective Task 71-BDE-5130, Assess the Tactical Situation and Operation.

MAINTAIN CONTINUITY OF COMMAND AND CONTROL

     1-475. During this table commanders should begin exercising succession of responsibilities to seconds
     in charge of sections and cells, delegating responsibilities, and otherwise accounting for contingencies
     that stress the C2 system. Commanders can use events in the exercise’s Master Scenario Events List
     (MSEL) to trigger conditions that force the C2 system to react (e.g., an indirect fire attack temporarily
     renders a CP incapable of performing its primary functions). Commanders can use Individual Task 150-
     LDR-5029, Maintain Continuity of Command and Control, and Collective Task 71-BDE-5250, Maintain
     Continuity of Command and Control.

TABLE VIII: APPLY THE MISSION COMMAND APPROACH TO
COMMAND AND CONTROL

     1-476. This table nests the graduate levels of C2 with the integration of additional personnel and
     equipment. Table VIII prepares the commander for the validation rehearsal in Table IX and the CTE for
     the headquarters in Table X. As with the other tables, this table event aligns with events already being
     performed by the unit. For example, the commander uses a CPX for training tables VIII-IX over a five
     day exercise. Figure 2-8 on next page establishes the inputs, tasks to be accomplished during this table,
     and the auditable outputs for the table.

LEAD THE OPERATIONS PROCESS

     1-477. The commander uses Collective Task 71-BDE-5100, Conduct the Operations Process for
     Command and Control, and Individual Task 150-LDR-5100, Lead the Operations Process, to train and
     evaluate the headquarters’ execution of the operations process in support of a MET. Integral to the
     completion of these collective tasks is the commander driving the operations process. Commanders drive
     the operations process through their activities of understanding, visualizing, describing, directing,
     leading, and assessing operations. Brigade commanders also use mission variables and operational
     variables, to refine their understanding of the situation and how these variables might affect the mission.
     How the commander implements command activities, identifies key variables, and conveys their intent
     to drive the operations process has direct impact on the unit’s ability to establish unity of effort and
     exploit opportunities and vulnerabilities. The operations process is the overarching framework for
     exercising C2.

     1-478. As brigade commanders drive the operations process to support the tactical fight, they provide
     guidance on how to exercise control. No single control option works best for all situations. The XO and
     S-3 are trained and able to drive the operations process in the absence of the commander. This does not
     negate the commander’s responsibility to be present for commander designated performance measures
     listed in the collective T&EOs. For example, the brigade commander delivers the commander’s intent at
     the CP as part of a leader’s performance measure within the Collective Task 71-BDE-5100, Conduct the
     Operations Process for Command and Control. Commanders:

          • Understand – Commanders actively collaborate with other commanders, the staff, and unified
                action partners, to create a shared understanding.

          • Visualize – Commanders visualize the operation’s end state and potential solutions to solve
                problems.

          • Describe – Commanders describe this visualization to their staffs and subordinates. This
                description facilitates shared understanding of the situation, mission, and intent.

          • Direct – Based on this understanding, commanders make decisions and direct action throughout
                the operations process.

          • Lead – Commanders use the operations process to lead Soldiers and forces by providing
                direction and guidance.

104                                             TC 6-0.2                                          30 July 2025


---

                                         Command and Control Warfighting Function Training Tables – Brigades

          • Assess – Commanders assess operations continuously to better understand current conditions
                and determine how operations are progressing. Commanders incorporate the assessments of the
                staff, subordinate commanders, and unified action partners into their personal assessment of the
                situation. Based on their assessment, commanders modify plans and orders to better accomplish
                the mission. If their assessment reveals a significant variance from their original commander’s
                visualization, commanders reframe the problem and develop a new operational approach.

ESTABLISH THE MISSION COMMAND APPROACH TO COMMAND AND CONTROL

     1-479. In previous tables the commander performed different sub tasks of the C2 WFF. Table VIII
     combines those previous efforts to implement the mission command approach to C2. The mission
     command approach C2 is the exercise of authority and direction by the commander using mission orders
     to enable disciplined initiative within the commander’s intent to empower agile and adaptive leaders in
     the conduct of LSCO. The commander completes, or refines if completed in previous tables, the listed
     Individual Tasks:

          • 150-LDR-5028, Establish the Mission Command Approach to Command and Control.
          • 150-LDR-5015, Lead the Unit.
          • 158-LDR-5001, Develop Cohesive Teams Through Mutual Trust.
          • 150-LDR-5007, Establish a Shared Understanding.
          • 150-LDR-5009, Communicate Commander’s Guidance.
          • 150-LDR-5004, Communicate the Commander’s Intent.
          • 150-LDR-5036, Determine Appropriate Level of Control.
          • 150-LDR-5020, Establish Control Measures.
          • 150-LDR-5006, Establish Conditions for Subordinates to Exercise Initiative.
          • 150-LDR-5003, Use the Mission Order Technique.
          • 150-LDR-5040, Maintain the Commander’s Estimate.
          • 150-LDR-5043, Establish the Five Integrating Processes.

     1-480. As the commander leads the headquarters through the completion of collective training, the
     commander assesses synchronization, integration, and interoperability of all non-organic units to include
     unified action partners and liaisons. This assessment includes information systems, personnel or liaison
     exchanges, and CP elements. The commander adjusts the C2 system based on their observations.

     PT that will be used in the unit’s EXEVAL. The conduct of this table is tied to all four training audiences
     of the C2TT working as a unified C2 system towards the completion of the unit’s METL. Units can also
     tie this event to training on their assigned mission METL (A-METL) as determined by the commander.
     During this table, each CP (such as main, TAC, field trains CPs) displaces at least once in preparation
     for evaluating displacements during Table X. Figure 2-9 on next page establishes the inputs, tasks to be
     accomplished during this table, and the auditable outputs for the table.
