DRIVE THE OPERATIONS PROCESS

     1-482. Commanders are the most important participants in the operations process. Refer to Individual
     Task 150-LDR-5100, Lead the Operations Process. While staffs, led by the XO, perform essential
     functions that contribute to the effectiveness of operations, commanders drive the operations process
     through activities of understand, visualize, describe, direct, lead, and assess in each major operations
     process activity of plan, prepare, execute and assess.

     1-483. The commander performs these activities throughout the training event as the mission dictates.
     The commander applies an appropriate degree of command presence at the appropriate time and place
     within each major operations process activity while still allowing the headquarters to exercise disciplined
     initiative within their capabilities.

LEAD THE REHEARSAL

     1-484. A rehearsal is a session in which the commander and staff or unit practices expected actions to
     improve performance during execution (ADP 5-0). Rehearsals are the commander’s tool to ensure that
     staffs and subordinates understand the commander’s intent and the concept of operations. They allow
     commanders and staffs to identify shortcomings in the plan that have not been previously recognized.
     Rehearsals also contribute to external and internal coordination, as the staff identifies additional
     coordinating requirements

     1-485. The commander leads the unit’s combined arms rehearsal and any of the support rehearsals (fires,
     reconnaissance and surveillance, sustainment, etc.) that the commander considers particularly important
     to executing the MET about to be performed. Commanders command the rehearsal just as they will
     command the operation. They maintain the focus and level of intensity, limiting the potential for
     subordinate confusion. An effective rehearsal is not a commander’s briefing to subordinates, nor a
     subordinate brief to the commander. The CAR should be similar to a "practice", where participants
     simultaneously rehearse their actions, reports, and control measures. It validates synchronization—the
     what, when, and where—of tasks that subordinate units will perform to execute the operation, how they

106                                             TC 6-0.2                                 30 July 2025


---

                                         Command and Control Warfighting Function Training Tables – Brigades

     relate to each other, and how they should achieve the commander’s intent. Individual Task 150-LDR-
     5039, Lead the Rehearsal, guides the commander for their role in executing the rehearsal.

PREPARE FOR OPERATIONS

     1-486. Commanders drive preparation activities with a focus on leading and assessing. The functions of
     preparation include the following:

          • Improve situational understanding.
          • Develop a common understanding of the plan.
          • Train and become proficient on critical tasks.
          • Task-organize and integrate the force.
          • Ensure forces and resources are positioned.

     1-487. The commander employs subordinate leaders, such as the command sergeant major (CSM) to
     verify that subordinate units are conducting directed and SOP pre-combat inspections (PCI) and provides
     guidance to the XO and operations SGM for doing the same with the staff, CPs, and digital crew. See
     Individual Task 150-LDR-5022, Conduct Pre-Combat Inspections, for greater detail.

COMMAND FORCES

     1-488. The commander leads forces in the execution of the unit METL, exercising authority and
     responsibility to effectively use available resources to accomplish the assigned mission. The commander
     combines the elements of command (authority, responsibility, decision making, and leadership) to
     employ all available combat power to create multiple dilemmas for enemy forces, gain positions of
     relative advantage, and exploit opportunities to achieve results. Commanders evaluate their performance
     using Individual Task 150-LDR-5037, Command Forces.

CONDUCT THE RAPID DECISION-MAKING AND SYNCHRONIZATION PROCESS

     1-489. Commanders use the rapid decision-making and synchronization Process (RDSP) for situations
     when the current events do not match the predetermined plan and necessitates an adjustment decision
     without enough time for full MDMP. The commander leads the staff through the conduct of the RDSP.
     The commander completes Individual Task 150-LDR-5014, Lead the Rapid Decision-Making and
     Synchronization Process. This task is performed in conjunction with a collective task. For example, the
     brigade commander uses RDSP after receiving a change in mission from higher headquarters to conduct
     a security operation in conjunction with conducting an attack.

CONDUCT AFTER ACTION REVIEWS

     1-490. The brigade commander leads an AAR of the operation conducted during Table IX training with
     a focus on identifying and correcting training deficiencies and/or unit procedures that must be corrected
     prior to the Table X external evaluation. Refer to Individual Task 150-C2-5133, Conduct a Formal After
     Action Review.

TABLE X: COMMAND AND CONTROL WARFIGHTING
FUNCTION VALIDATION EXERCISE

     1-491. This event is typically concurrent with a CTE such as the brigade EXEVAL or combat training
     center (CTC) rotation. This table is directly tied to all four training audiences (the commander, the staff,
     the CP, and the digital crew). This event is externally evaluated while the unit executes tasks on the
     unit’s METL or A-METL. Each CP (such as main, TAC, and field trains) will be evaluated on its ability
     to displace during the validation exercise.

     1-492. The outcome of Table X is that the evaluating commander validates that the brigade commander
     is prepared to lead the brigade using the mission command approach to C2 in the execution of METL
     tasks during LSCO. Figure 2-10 on next page establishes the inputs, tasks to be accomplished during
     this table, and the auditable outputs for the table.

DRIVE THE OPERATIONS PROCESS

     1-493. Commanders are the most important participants in the operations process. Refer to Individual
     Task 150-LDR-5100, Lead the Operations Process. While staffs, led by the XO, perform essential
     functions that contribute to the effectiveness of operations, commanders drive the operations process
     through activities of understand, visualize, describe, direct, and assess in each major mission command
     activity of plan, prepare, execute and assess.

     1-494. The commander performs these activities throughout the training event as the mission dictates.
     The commander applies an appropriate degree of command presence within each major operations
     process activity while still allowing the headquarters to exercise disciplined initiative within their
     capabilities.

LEAD THE UNIT IN COMPLETION OF ASSIGNED TACTICAL TASKS

     1-495. The commander leads the brigade and supporting forces to accomplish the tactical mission tasks
     from the unit’s METL per the orders of the higher headquarters. See Individual Task 150-LDR-5037,
     Command Forces.

EVALUATE METL TASKS

     1-496. The brigade commander considers the observations of the external evaluation team and
     determines the T-level achieved by the brigade on the METs performed during the exercise for updating
     the unit status report (USR). See FM 7-0.

SECTION III – BRIGADE STAFF TRAINING TABLES

     1-497. Section III discusses the command and control training tables for the brigade staff.

COMMAND AND CONTROL TRAINING TABLES FOR THE
BRIGADE STAFF

     1-498. This section guides the brigade commander and the XO through the tables used to train, certify,
     and validate the staff. The principal staff officers consist of officers who lead coordinating and special
     staff sections, and personal staff officers. The staff consists of the principal staff officers and the officers,
     NCOs, and Soldiers that work in their sections.

108                                             TC 6-0.2                                30 July 2025


---

                                   Command and Control Warfighting Function Training Tables – Brigades

1-499. This section does not cover every aspect of training, nor pinpoint exactly how to train each
collective or individual task. Commanders and leaders wanting more information on training the force
will reference the T&EOs for each task, the available training support packages, and FM 7-0 for
assistance.

1-500. While the commander is responsible for certifying the staff, the XO is responsible for overall
staff training. Principal staff officers are responsible for training their staff sections and for ensuring
leaders of all staff members participating in a particular function under their purview (e.g. conducting a
battle update brief, which falls under the S-3) prepare their Soldiers to fulfill their roles. Principal staff
officers ensure their subordinate Soldiers and leaders are trained to execute the following:

     • The tasks in the individual critical task list (ICTL) for their MOS and skill level.
     • Tasks in the ICTL for additional skill identifiers (ASI) assigned to their soldiers in the unit’s

          TO&E. For example, the C2 information integrator can perform all the tasks on the ICTL for
          ASI 5C.
     • Section level collective tasks, such as maintaining the section’s running estimate.
     • Individual and collective tasks associated with their role in functions outside the section such
          as:
           A section representative to the current operations integration cell (COIC).
           A member of the unit’s digital crew.
           A member of the CP crew.
           A representative of the section to a staff board, working group, or planning team.

1-501. The important responsibility of principal staff leaders and their subordinate officers and NCOs to
train their section and cell personnel can be overlooked, particularly at brigade level where daily
operational duties consume much of their time and their subordinates’. Nevertheless, this responsibility
is vital to successful operations of the staff in LSCO and should not be neglected. Brigade leaders must
train and evaluate subordinate staff officers and NCOs to properly train their subordinates and should
include feedback to them on their performance of this function in performance and professional growth
counseling per ATP 6-22.1.

1-502. The complexities of a brigade staff and its daily operations can make training the staff
challenging. The commander, XO, and principal staff officers always try to balance daily operational
requirements with training opportunities. The XO will leverage the principal staff officers, section and
cell NCOs in charge (NCOICs), and Headquarters and Headquarters Company (HHC) command team
to conduct many aspects of the tables below. However, at a minimum tables V and VI must be performed
as a unit to achieve the desired level of training proficiency. Once the staff is certified, they can proceed
to unit training in Tables VII through X.

1-503. Tables I through IV are designed to train the staff at the individual, section, and cell levels. Tables
I-II occur on an on-going basis as Soldiers and officers join the unit or rotate through different duty
positions. Table V is the rehearsal, as the headquarters elements come together to work as a collective
unit. The commander certifies the staff on Table VI, certification. There are no external attachments or
liaisons required for the execution of Tables I through VI. The XO is the authority, as determined by the
commander, to transition the staff through tables I through V. The commander certifies the staff using
Table VI for transitioning to the more advanced Tables VII through X.

1-504. During Tables VII - X, the staff in all the unit’s CPs function as one C2 system to conduct the
operations process and support the commander’s decision making. To do this successfully, all staff
members must understand the roles and functions of not just their section and CP, but of all sections and
CPs in the C2 system.

1-505. The tables are written such that the commander can retrain the staff to overcome skill atrophy or
personnel turnover before a future training event. For example, the commander can use an upcoming
STAFFEX or STX to retrain the staff on Tables III through V to achieve a greater level of proficiency
before the CPX is to be conducted in a few months. The tables are meant to be flexible for the commander
to train the staff to a level of proficiency needed to conduct LSCO.

1-506. Once the commander certifies the staff at Table VI, the staff receives an OPORD/FRAGORD for
the brigade’s EXEVAL. This order is used throughout tables VII-X to drive training. Furthermore, the

30 July 2025  TC 6-0.2  109

     unit begins at Table VII to incorporate multinational partners and other unified action partners that will
     be a factor for Table X and the EXEVAL.

     1-507. Upon the completion of Table X and the formal and informal AARs conducted by the EXEVAL
     team, leaders of staff sections and cells, CPs, and the digital crew should conduct internal sessions with
     their Soldiers to identify areas for retraining or other corrective actions such as making changes in SOPs.
     Commanders should include designated time for these activities in the training schedule.

TABLE I: ORGANIZE AND ACCLIMATE STAFF TO THE
ORGANIZATION

     1-508. This table is the introduction of the staff members to the unit. This table assumes that all officers,
     NCOs, and Soldiers will come to the unit with basic proficiency in their individual MOS skills. Principal
     staff officers can use programmed training time to retrain job skill deficiencies. In Table I the staff
     section leaders further the training and develop initial staff training through a basic understanding of
     duties, responsibilities, and the mission under the supervision of the principal staff officers and the XO.
     Table I and II tasks are considered perishable skills and should be performed frequently to maintain basic
     staff proficiency. Tables I and II can also be used to integrate new personnel upon arrival to the unit
     during the unit/commander orientation program. Figure 2-11 below establishes the inputs, tasks to be
     accomplished during this table, and the auditable outputs for the table.

UNDERSTAND UNIT DOCTRINE APPLICATIONS, STAFF DUTIES, RESPONSIBILITIES, AND
STANDARD OPERATING PROCEDURES

     1-509. All members of the staff familiarize themselves on all command policies, procedures, and current
     staff section and cell SOPs. Brigade staff officers and NCOs familiarize themselves with the doctrine
     relevant to the brigade conducting LSCO, including FM 3-0 and applicable FMs and ATPs for the unit
     type. They also familiarize themselves with their specific branch/functional area responsibilities at the
     brigade level. The staff section leader develops a basic understanding of upcoming unit missions and
     how those requirements translate into the basic skills section members will need. Section leaders further
     define the roles and responsibilities of each section member and determine current and future
     expectations. Staff section leaders study and refine individual tasks and section battle drills relating to
     their duty position and the current organization; they train their Soldiers using individual and section
     collective tasks as defined by the appropriate proponent. For example, the S-2 conducts intelligence

110                                                          TC 6-0.2                              30 July 2025


---

                                         Command and Control Warfighting Function Training Tables – Brigades

     preparation of the OE training using Collective Task 34-SEC-3180, Perform Intelligence Preparation of
     the Operational Environment; provided by the Intelligence Center of Excellence. Refer to Individual
     Task 150-LDR-5007, Establish a Shared Understanding for building section and cell level teams.

ORGANIZE THE STAFF

     1-510. The XO and principal staff officers, after consultation and guidance from the commander, will
     direct the organization or reorganization of the brigade staff. The commander determines and the XO
     and principal staff officers clearly define all extra duties. See Individual Task 150-C2-0000, Organize
     the Command and Control System as the Staff Officer. (See ATP 6-0.5 for additional information.)

CONDUCT WARFIGHTING FUNCTION AND COMMAND POST ORGANIZATION

     1-511. The XO, with the assistance of the principal staff officers, organizes the staff according to
     commander’s guidance and using Individual Task 150-LDR-5013, Organize the Staff for Operations.
     Once the XO has established the staff structure, the principal staff officers, and section and cell NCOs
     ensure that all members of their staff element understand their role within the CP to which they are
     assigned, as well as how their section interacts with other entities of the entire staff. This applies to staff
     elements in all CPs, not just the main CP (TAC CP, field trains, or any other alternate CP the commander
     establishes). Furthermore, each member of the staff must develop an understanding of the roles and
     responsibilities of other staff cells and unit CPs. The layout for staff elements within a CP can greatly
     promote or inhibit staff integration. The officers and NCOs in charge of the brigade CPs consider
     manning, maturity, subject matter expertise or specialty, collaboration frequency, coordination
     requirements, facilitation for rapid exchange of information, inputs and outputs, and mission
     requirements for presentation to the XO with recommendations for CP organization.

BUILD STAFF RELATIONSHIPS

     1-512. The XO conducts basic team building among the staff and nests staff team building with the
     commander’s program and the higher headquarters. Because of the complexity and size of the brigade
     staff, the XO employs the principal staff officers in this effort. For example, the XO conducts
     teambuilding for the staff by conducting morning physical training together periodically.

     1-513. Typically, senior level staff members conduct team building events independently of most of the
     staff. Additionally, senior level staff members build unity among staff sections by holding combined
     relationship building events. For example, the brigade current operations cell challenges the sustainment
     section to a team obstacle course contest for an upcoming physical training session.

     1-514. Brigade level relationship building events require consistent employment of professionalism and
     interpersonal skills that foster effective open communication and builds unity within the command.
     Using a variety of venues and techniques which allow team members to contribute through observable
     means is essential to effective relationship building. Feedback mechanisms are established to promote
     interaction or prevent conflict within the staff. See Individual Task 158-LDR-5001, Develop Cohesive
     Teams Through Mutual Trust, and Collective Task 71-TM-5004, Develop a Cohesive Team Through
     Mutual Trust.

DEVELOP NONCOMMISSIONED OFFICERS AND SOLDIERS

     1-515. Section leaders will ensure that NCOs and Soldiers are professionally developed to accomplish
     their mission and can provide their expertise and input to the operations process. NCO and Soldier
     development is grounded in the unit professional development program. The program must combine
     developmental expectations with the projected mission requirements the organization will experience.
     The section leader will use every opportunity to seek appropriate technical/skill qualification schools,
     standardized training with proficiency assessments, as well as any additional training opportunities that
     may benefit the NCO, each Soldier, and the organization. Refer to Individual Task 158-LDR-8003,
     Conduct Initial Assessment for a New Leadership Position.

DEVELOP RUNNING ESTIMATES

     1-517. As part of each staff section understanding their role within the headquarters, the principal staff
     officer or section leader will train the section on the purpose behind and the development of running
     estimates: to record information, provide expert analysis/opinions and to provide recommendations that
     will help the commander make decisions. Running estimates need to include analyzed information that
     pertains to the operation and is of immediate value to the commander, not simply raw, unanalyzed data.
     Running estimates must always include recommendations for anticipated decisions. Leaders use
     Collective Tasks 71-BDE-5144, Develop a Running Estimate, 150-C2-5129, Analyze the Mission
     Variables, and 150-C2-5137, Analyze the Operational Variables. Primary staff officers will also identify
     tasks from their corresponding WFF proponent such as 63-BDE-4905, Develop a Sustainment Running
     Estimate for the sustainment cell.

CONDUCT THE MILITARY DECISION-MAKING PROCESS

     1-518. Principal staff officers review with their section its role and contribution to the MDMP. Leaders
     run their sections through drills using the OE selected by the commander. Leaders ensure the training

112                                             TC 6-0.2                                                    30 July 2025


---

                                         Command and Control Warfighting Function Training Tables – Brigades

     enhances the section’s thoroughness, clarity, sound judgment, logic and professional knowledge to
     understand situations, develop options to solve problems, and make recommendations.

     1-519. Once the section gains basic proficiency in MDMP, the principal staff officer or section leader
     will run abbreviated MDMP drills and scenarios based on a time constrained environment. The leader
     will use the collective tasks listed here to ensure the section understands and fulfills their duties and
     responsibilities for MDMP. It is not intended for the XO to integrate all the sections into a full MDMP
     at this time. Principal staff officers use Collective Task 71-BDE-5111, Conduct the Military Decision-
     Making Process to ensure they understand the section’s role. Staff personnel participating in MDMP
     refer to Individual Tasks 150-C2-5110 through 150-C2-5121 for detailed guidance on the conduct of
     each step in the process. Individual staff sections also refer to their proponent training products for more
     tailored training.

     1-520. The outcome of this element of Table II is that staff section personnel know what products and
     inputs they contribute to the MDMP according to their unit SOP and are prepared to produce them once
     the staff begins collective MDMP.

CONDUCT THE RAPID DECISION-MAKING AND SYNCHRONIZATION PROCESS

     1-521. The RDSP is a technique that commanders and staffs commonly use during execution when they
     recognize that reality does not match assumptions made during planning (see Individual Task 150-C2-
     5142, Identify Variances). While identified here with a specific name and method, the approach is not
     new; its use in the Army is well established. Commanders and staffs develop this capability through
     training and practice. The RDSP facilitates integration and synchronization of the warfighting functions
     to address ever-changing situations. The RDSP is based on an existing order and the commander’s
     priorities as expressed in the order. The most important of these control measures are the draft proposed
     commander’s intent, concept of operations, and CCIRs. Refer to FM 5-0 and Collective Task 71-BDE-
     5002, Conduct Rapid Decision-Making and Synchronization Process.

REVIEW COMMANDER’S DECISION SUPPORT TEMPLATE AND DECISION SUPPORT
MATRIX

     1-522. The DST and DSM provide the projection of where and when a decision must be made based on
     expected events at specified locations with stated criteria and planned friendly actions in a narrative
     form. The brigade includes standard formats for decision support materials in the unit SOP, and tailor
     those products to the situation at hand.

     1-523. The DST graphically represents decision points and projected situations, and indicates when,
     where, and under what conditions a decision is most likely to be required to initiate a specific activity or
     event. It contains time phase lines, named areas of interest, target areas of interest, and decision points.

     1-524. A DSM is a written record of a war-gamed COA that describes decision points and associated
     actions at those decision points. It lists decision points, locations of decision points, criteria to be
     evaluated at decision points, actions that occur at decision points, and the units responsible to act on the
     decision points. It also lists the units responsible for observing and reporting information affecting the
     criteria for decisions. Refer to Collective Tasks, 71-BDE-5009, Develop a Decision Support Template,
     and Individual Tasks, 150-C2-5111, Conduct the Military Decision-Making Process, 150-C2-5138,
     Develop a Decision Support Template (DST), and 150-C2-5139, Develop a Decision Support Matrix.

     1-525. Brigade leaders can use DST and DSM from earlier missions as training aids while conducting
     Table II. During this table, and as part of the MDMP instruction, the principal staff officers will:

          • Run their sections through internal wargaming drills
          • Instruct how their wargaming inputs result in outputs to feed the DST and DSM in COA

                development
          • Instruct how the DST and DSM inform the commander for COA selection
          • Train procedures their sections take once a COA is selected including:

                 Refining DST graphics over space and time as the operational situation develops
                 Refining the DSM written record, to include decision points, over space and time as the

                     situation develops

30 July 2025  TC 6-0.2  113


---

Chapter 2

     1-526. In addition to the DST and DSM, commanders and staffs use several tools (orders, overlays,
     templates, matrices, lists, tables, and charts) to assist them in synchronizing operations and making
     decisions. A common tool is the execution matrix, a visual and sequential representation of critical tasks
     and responsible organizations by time. An execution matrix could be for the entire force, such as an air
     assault execution matrix, or it may be specific to a warfighting function as in a fire support execution
     matrix. Each principal staff officer and section leader will train on and incorporate tools specific to their
     warfighting function to assist the commander in decision-making. For a listing of these tools, see ATP
     6-0.5, appendix D. For creating a combined arms execution matrix, see Collective Task 71-BDE-5008,
     Develop an Execution Matrix.

ASSESS THE CURRENT SITUATION AND OPERATION

     1-527. Each principal staff officer/section leader will use the proponent T&EOs for assessment tasks to
     manage and train their section on the use and application of these assessments. Refer to Individual Task
     150-C2-5130, Assess the Tactical Situation, to train on formal assessments. If the commander elects to
     establish a formal assessment plan, the S-3 (or plans officer/S-5 if the unit has one) leads the staff in
     preparing a formal collection plan that includes:

          • MOPs to assess task accomplishment.
          • MOEs to assess progress towards the desired end state.
          • Indicators that support the MOPs and MOEs.
          • Inputs to the information collection plan to ensure indicators are collected and disseminated.
          • Inputs to the knowledge management plan to ensure MOP, MOE, indicators, and analysis are

                available to appropriate staff elements.

     1-528. Each WFF has its own type of formal assessment. The principal staff officer/section leader will
     use the proponent T&EOs for assessment tasks to manage and train their section on the use and
     application of these assessments. Refer to Individual Task 150-C2-5130, Assess the Tactical Situation,
     to train on formal assessments.

CONDUCT SECTION REHEARSALS

     1-529. Table II rehearsals begin at the section level. Principal staff officers ensure that their section and
     cell members thoroughly understand their roles in section/cell operations through rehearsals. The leader
     identified the tasks the section must be prepared to perform during Table I. In table II, they train and
     practice the section in their performance of them. The section/cell members should be trained to execute
     assigned tasks with the other shift members they will be assigned with during operations. As the shift
     teams gain proficiency leaders can increase the difficulty by taking out shift members or increase overall
     team competency by swapping members from one shift to the other during new repetitions. Refer to
     Individual Task 150-C2-5122, Facilitate a Rehearsal.

VERIFY SECTION OUTPUTS

     1-530. The XO ensures that all sections account for respective, predetermined outputs per the unit SOP.
     These outputs come in all types and serve various purposes, including those that provide direction to
     subordinate units; are inputs to other staff processes/procedures within the brigade; and those that inform
     or drive processes for higher, adjacent, supporting, and supported headquarters. All outputs must lead to
     a better understanding of a situation and facilitate steps towards a decision. Examples of these can
     include a daily threat update from the S-2 that is an input to various staff elements and processes, a
     sustainment forecast over space and time that includes friendly losses from the sustainment cell that
     informs the unit DSM, or an incoming personnel listing from the S-1 that influences the unit DSM and
     the brigade personnel replacement process. Refer to Individual Task 150-LDR-5011, Establish Boards
     and Working Groups.

DEVELOP SECTION TRAINING PLANS

     1-531. Section leaders must have a comprehensive understanding of their respective section/cell
     requirements, inputs and outputs and have identified the training required for their section to be
     responsive to the commander’s decision-making process. The C2TTs and the unit CATS should prove

114        TC 6-0.2  30 July 2025

                       Command and Control Warfighting Function Training Tables – Brigades

     helpful to the staff leader in identifying tasks to train. The section leader, in coordination with the S-3
     and the headquarters company, develops a training plan that focuses on the needs of the section, their
     designed capabilities, the mission, and the commander’s requirements. The S-3 captures these training
     plans in the overall brigade training schedule. Once all sections have formalized their internal training
     plans, the S-3 develops a unit training plan to present to the commander. The commander provides
     guidance, and the S-3 will adjust the plan until the commander approves, and the long range training
     plan is published. Refer to Individual Task 150-COM-7170, Write the Unit Training Plan Operation
     Order.

TABLE III: ESTABLISH STAFF AND CELL PROCESSES AND
INTEGRATE WARFIGHTING FUNCTIONS

     1-532. Table III begins the effort of integrating the staff by the XO forming CP cells. This table also
     refines the section skills with the principal staff officer/section leader training their sections on more
     aspects of the MDMP. This table can be performed in a garrison environment. Figure 2-13 below
     establishes the inputs, tasks to be accomplished during this table, and the auditable outputs for the table.

ESTABLISH FUNCTIONAL AND INTEGRATING CELLS

1-533. The XO organized the various sections into functioning CPs in Table I and II. Table III begins
with the commander and XO reviewing the results of Table II and deciding if any changes to earlier

Chapter 2

     organizational decisions should be made. Of note, not all brigades will have the full range of WFFs nor
     a large enough staff to establish planning cells and may have inexperienced staff officers and NCOs
     working in their first assignment to a staff. The commander and XO establish CP cells based on these
     factors, unit modified TO&E (MTO&E), its capabilities, and mission. Refer to FM 6-0 and the doctrine
     for the unit type on establishing these cells. The XO, principal staff officers, and section leaders use
     Individual Task 150-C2-0000, Organize Command and Control System as the Staff Officer. See Figure
     8-3 from FM 6-0.

VALIDATE WARFIGHTING FUNCTIONS

     1-534. As the unit receives new members to the staff or the staff realigns for operational reasons, the XO
     will validate each of the unit’s WFFs. The individual tasks covering each are listed in the next paragraph.
     The XO verifies adherence to SOPs, the commander’s intent, orders from higher headquarters, and
     operational requirements. This publication will not get into the finer details of what actions each staff
     section performs or how they are to perform them. For example, the division artillery commander wants
     the S-2 section to conduct more detailed intelligence integration training based on an influx of additional
     personnel. The commander consults the respective unit CATS and finds task set 52-TS-8131, Integrate
     Intelligence Activities in Support of Operations. The commander sees that within that task set (TS) there
     are several tasks to train the intelligence section, to include:

          • 34-SEC-1102, Provide Intelligence Overwatch.
          • 34-SEC-1315, Provide Geospatial Intelligence Analytic Support to Operations.
          • 34-SEC-1317, Provide Geospatial Intelligence Support to Requirements Planning.
          • 34-SEC-1320, Conduct Signals Intelligence Section Analysis.
          • 34-SEC-3040, Provide Signals Intelligence Support to Planning.
          • 34-SEC-3041, Asses Signaux Intelligence Collection.
          • 34-SEC-3042, Develop Signals Intelligence Products.
          • 34-SEC-3171, Plan Integration into the Intelligence Architecture.

     1-535. The CATS displays the recommended number of times and hours that the sections should train
     on the task set to gain the desired proficiency (crawl/walk/run). The XO uses the following individual
     tasks for validating each WFF (for those functions represented on the unit staff):

          • Individual Task 150-C2-0001, Validate the Movement and Maneuver Warfighting Function
                Staff.

          • Individual Task 150-C2-0002, Validate the Intelligence Warfighting Function Staff.
          • Individual Task 150-C2-0003, Validate the Fires Warfighting Function Staff.
          • Individual Task 150-C2-0004, Validate the Sustainment Warfighting Function Staff.
          • Individual Task 150-C2-0005, Validate the Command and Control Warfighting Function Staff.
          • Individual Task 150-C2-0006, Validate the Protection Warfighting Function Staff.

CONDUCT MILITARY DECISION-MAKING PROCESS

     1-536. The MDMP helps leaders apply thoroughness, clarity, sound judgment, logic, and professional
     knowledge to understand situations, develop options to solve problems, and reach decisions. This
     process helps commanders, staffs, and others think critically and creatively during planning. In Table
     III, the staff begins collectively training the MDMP using the scenario and higher headquarters order
     that will be used during certification in Table VI. Refer to FM 5-0, Collective Task 71-BDE-5111,
     Conduct the Military Decision-Making Process, and Individual Tasks 150-C2-5110 through 150-C2-
     5121 for the steps and procedures used during MDMP.

CONDUCT RECEIPT OF MISSION

     1-537. The S-3 uses the OPORD issued for conducting Table VI (later) to lead the staff through the steps
     of receipt of mission including alerting the staff, gathering the tools, updating estimates, conducting an
     initial assessment, receiving the commander’s guidance, and issuing a warning order (WARNORD)
     using Individual Tasks 150-C2-5110, Conduct Receipt of Mission, and 150-C2-5117, Prepare a Warning
     Order.

116  TC 6-0.2  30 July 2025


---

                                         Command and Control Warfighting Function Training Tables – Brigades

CONDUCT MISSION ANALYSIS AND INTELLIGENCE PREPARATION OF THE
OPERATIONAL ENVIRONMENT

     1-538. Commanders and their staffs (informed by subordinate and adjacent commanders and by other
     partners) gather, analyze, and synthesize information to orient themselves on the current conditions of
     the OE. The commander and staff conduct mission analysis to better understand the situation and
     problem, and identify what the command must accomplish, when and where it must be done, and most
     importantly why—the purpose of the operation. IPOE is the systematic process of analyzing the mission
     variables of mission, enemy, terrain & weather, troops & support available, time available, civil
     considerations and information (METT-TC(I)) in an area of interest to determine their effect on
     operations. The IPOE process identifies critical gaps in the commander’s knowledge of an OE. Training
     time spent conducting thorough mission analysis and IPOE at this stage of the training tables will pay
     great dividends later. Refer to FM 5-0, ATP 2-01.3, and Collective Tasks 71-BDE-5112, Conduct
     Mission Analysis, and 34-SEC-3180, Conduct Intelligence Preparation of the Operational Environment,
     for detailed execution guidance. Each contributing staff officer refers to 150-C2-2210, Provide Input for
     Intelligence Preparation of the Operational Environment, when preparing their input to the IPOE.

RECOMMEND COMMANDER’S CRITICAL INFORMATION REQUIREMENTS

     1-539. As the staff begins work as a cohesive unit and prepares for the staff rehearsal, the XO leads the
     brigade on recommending CCIR to the commander. CCIR are an information requirement identified by
     the commander as being critical to facilitate timely decision-making. Each staff section identifies and
     recommends PIR and FFIR to the commander to designate as the CCIR.

     1-540. CCIR are established as part of the MDMP and refined throughout the conduct of operations. The
     XO uses Individual Task 150-C2-5113, Recommend the Commander's Critical Information
     Requirements.

CONDUCT RISK ASSESSMENT

     1-541. Staff leaders begin risk management by conducting risk assessment in conjunction with mission
     analysis. Throughout the operations process commanders and their staff use risk assessment to establish
     a basis to manage risks associated with all hazards that have the potential to injure or kill friendly and
     civilian personnel, damage or destroy equipment, or otherwise impact mission effectiveness. See
     Individual Tasks 150-C2-5145, Conduct Risk Management, and Collective Task 71-BDE-5145,
     Integrate Risk Management into Operations. See ATP 5-19 for further description of risk management
     and FM 5-0, Appendix G for a discussion of integrating risk management into the operations process.

ENGAGE IN COURSE OF ACTION DEVELOPMENT

     1-542. The XO or S-3 leads the staff through COA development drills, ensuring each functional cell
     contributes to planning efforts within its functional responsibilities. The XO also designates the COA
     development roles and responsibilities of the special staff. The commander’s planning guidance for this
     step is important in COA development. Commander’s guidance must reflect an appreciation of the
     unpredictable and uncertain nature of the OE, and how to cope with ambiguity. It should include
     parameters for enabling subordinate unit initiative while establishing adequate controls based on risk.
     The staff employs the Table II step above on the DST and DSM to build initial decision products that
     they will subsequently use and refine in the wargame. The XO and staff members use Individual Task
     150-C2-5114, Engage in Course of Action Development.

     1-543. The planning team establishes evaluation criteria. The commander and staff use these criteria to
     measure the relative effectiveness and efficiency of one COA relative to other COAs. Evaluation criteria
     address factors that affect success and those that can cause failure. Criteria change from mission to
     mission and must be clearly defined and understood by all staff members before starting the war game
     to test the proposed COAs. Normally, the XO establishes weights based on the assessment of each
     criterion’s relative importance and the commander’s guidance.

30 July 2025  TC 6-0.2  117


---

Chapter 2

CONDUCT COURSE OF ACTION ANALYSIS

     1-544. COA analysis enables commanders and staffs to identify difficulties or coordination problems as
     well as probable consequences of planned actions for each COA being considered. It helps them think
     through the tentative plan. COA analysis not only appraises the quality of each COA, but it also uncovers
     potential execution problems, decisions, and contingencies. Section leaders train their personnel to
     contribute analysis for their designated function during the wargaming process. For example, the
     personnel section will contribute analysis regarding projected casualties based on the tempo and type of
     operation for each COA. Refer to Individual Task 150-C2-5115, Conduct Course of Action Analysis
     (War-Gaming), and ITSP 150-C2-5115 / 1.0, Wargaming, to guide their participation in comparing
     COAs for detailed execution guidance for detailed execution guidance.

CONDUCT COURSE OF ACTION COMPARISON

     1-545. The staff conducts COA comparison as an objective process to evaluate COAs independently and
     against set evaluation criteria approved by the commander or the commander’s designated
     representative. The goal is to identify the advantages and disadvantages of each COA, compare COAs
     to determine cost and benefit, which enables recommending and selecting a COA with the highest
     probability of success, and then further developing a COA in an OPLAN or OPORD. Staff members
     should use Individual Task 150-C2-5116, Conduct Course of Action Comparison.

CONDUCT A COURSE OF ACTION DECISION BRIEFING

     1-546. After completing its analysis and comparison, the staff identifies its preferred COA and makes a
     recommendation. The staff then delivers a decision briefing to the commander. The XO highlights any
     changes to each COA resulting from the war game. At the end of the briefing the commander (or the XO
     acting as the commander for training purposes) selects a COA that will carry the staff through Table VI;
     approves the mission statement and CCIR; and provides commander’s intent. Refer to Individual Task
     150-C2-5009, Conduct a Course of Action Decision Briefing, to evaluate this event.

CONDUCT COURSE OF ACTION APPROVAL

     1-547. After the decision briefing, the commander selects the COA to best accomplish the mission. If
     the commander rejects all COAs, the staff starts COA development again. If the commander modifies a
     proposed COA or gives the staff an entirely different one, the staff develops and analyzes the new COA
     and presents the results to the commander with a recommendation. After the commander approves a
     COA, the staff records the commander’s final planning guidance, including refined intent and approved
     CCIR. The staff issues another warning order for subordinate units per FM 5-0. Use Individual Task
     150-C2-5121, Conduct Course of Action Approval.

PREPARE AN OPERATION ORDER

     1-548. The brigade is required to publish an OPORD as part of the operations process. The staff must be
     proficient in conducting the MDMP, and the OPORD reveals that proficiency. The length and detail of
     the order can vary due to time and the mission. Once the order is approved by the commander or S-3,
     the staff prepares and executes an OPORD brief based on the commander’s guidance. They also
     distribute the order to higher, adjacent, lower/subordinate, supporting, and supported units. Adhere to
     the basic standards, as written in Individual Task 150-C2-5119, Prepare an Operation Order, and
     Collective Task 71-BDE-5119, Prepare an Operation Order.

DEVELOP KNOWLEDGE MANAGEMENT PLAN

     1-549. The ability to efficiently manage information and knowledge is essential to effective mission
     accomplishment. The volume of available information at the brigade level makes it difficult to identify
     and use relevant information. Knowledge management (KM) provides the means to efficiently share
     knowledge, thus enabling shared understanding and learning. During Table III, the staff, led by the S-6/
     knowledge management officer (KMO), conduct the first 3 steps of the knowledge management process
     (Assess, Design, and Develop) to draft the knowledge management plan (KMP). Later in the training
     tables, the KMO will lead the piloting and implementation of the KMP, and will lead the KM working

118        TC 6-0.2  30 July 2025


---

                             Command and Control Warfighting Function Training Tables – Brigades

     group to continuously assess and update the KMP leading to the Validation Exercise in Table X. The
     staff will use Individual Task 150-C2-7654, Perform Knowledge Management, and Collective Task 71-
     BDE-5330, Conduct Knowledge Management.

TABLE IV: SYNCHRONIZE COMMAND POST OPERATIONS

     1-550. Table IV begins the process of fully integrating the functional and integrating cells within the
     headquarters. The XO begins the more complex steps of conducting the entire operations process as well
     as manning the CPs. Table IV can take place during a STAFFEX for one of the battalion's mission
     essential tasks. Table IV is designed to be scenario driven in a complex and dynamic operational
     environment, and against a hybrid threat. The commander designates a MET T&EO from the unit’s
     METL for the training event. For this table, CPs train in isolation to establish internal processes and
     procedures, with limited or simulated interactions between the unit’s CPs and those of higher, adjacent,
     lower, supporting, and supported units. Figure 2-14 below establishes the inputs, tasks to be
     accomplished during this table, and the auditable outputs for the table.

INTEGRATE STAFF CELLS FOR OPERATIONS

1-551. The XO uses the training exercise in Table IV to fully integrate the WFFs and integrating cells
in the headquarters to enable synchronization for decision-making. The XO can use the battle rhythm
for this event to conduct this integration. For this first step of the table, the XO ensures inputs and outputs
by cells are integrated and the sections/cells are not acting as independent bodies. The cells complement
and feed the proper cell or working group for rapid and informed decision-making. The XO will adjust
the organization as needed or as directed by the commander. See Individual Task 150-C2-5126, Integrate
Staff Cells for Operations.

SYNCHRONIZE STAFF PROCESSES

1-552. The XO, with the assistance of the principal staff officers and cell NCOs, synchronizes staff
working groups, planning teams, and processes. When the process or activity being synchronized leads
to a command decision, a board may be the appropriate forum. These working groups, boards, and

30 July 2025                                      TC 6-0.2                                                                    119


---

Chapter 2

     processes take place throughout the operations process and as the mission and commander require. The
     staff will also participate in and provide input to the boards, working groups, and processes of the higher
     headquarters. The XO allows adequate time in the battle rhythm for working groups, boards, and process
     to run their course so outputs can achieve the desired effects. See Individual Task 150-LDR-5011,
     Establish Boards and Working Groups.

ESTABLISH A BATTLE RHYTHM

     1-553. The battle rhythm is a deliberate daily cycle of command, staff, and unit activities intended to
     synchronize current and future operations. A headquarters’ battle rhythm consists of a series of meetings,
     briefings, and other activities synchronized by time and purpose. The battle rhythm provides structure
     and synchronization for managing a headquarters’ most important internal resource – the time of the
     commander and staff. As part of this synchronization, the XO establishes a staff battle rhythm. The battle
     rhythm must include all unit CPs, and account for the battle rhythm requirements of higher, subordinate,
     and supported units. However, the battle rhythm is not static. An effective battle rhythm is modified as
     the situation evolves to include the types or series of meetings with expected staff representation,
     outcomes, and delegated decision authority for particular purposes or functions. Considerations for an
     effective battle rhythm include:

          • Facilitates commander-centric requirements for touch points.
          • Leads to timely decisions by the commander.
          • Addresses daily, weekly, and monthly requirements.
          • Balances time allotted for discussion, synchronization, and production.
          • Facilitates interaction between the commander, staff, and subordinate commanders.
          • Supports building and maintaining shared understanding throughout the headquarters.
          • Establishes a routine for staff interaction and coordination.

     1-554. To ensure meetings are organized well and achieve what is intended, staff leads develop
     instructions for each meeting (sometimes referred to as “7-Minute Drills”) to include the following:

          • Purpose.
          • Frequency, duration, and location.
          • Medium (face-to-face, video teleconference, and others).
          • Expected participants (staff lead, chairperson, and members).
          • Required inputs (such as updated collection plan).
          • Expected outputs (such as approved target nominations).
          • Agenda.

     1-555. Commanders and XOs should allow sufficient white space in the battle rhythm and avoid
     inundating the staff with too many meetings. The brigade events help build the battle rhythm. The XO
     uses Individual Task 150-C2-5123, Establish a Battle Rhythm. Refer to ATP 6-0.5, and Individual
     Training Support Package 150-C2-5123 / 1.0, Establish the Battle Rhythm.

CONDUCT REHEARSALS

     1-556. Rehearsals are the commander’s tool to ensure staffs and subordinate units understand the
     commander’s intent and the concept of operations. The primary purpose for rehearsals is to generate
     shared understanding of the commander’s visualization of the upcoming operation. They allow
     commanders, their staffs, and subordinate unit commanders to identify shortcomings in the plan not
     previously recognized (though the purpose is not to conduct wargaming again). Rehearsals also
     contribute to external and internal coordination, as the staff identifies additional coordinating
     requirements. During Table IV, the XO will lead the staff in rehearsing the planned operation to perform
     the mission associated with the MET selected for the training scenario. The XO should lead the staff to
     practice all rehearsals designated by the brigade SOP (combined arms, fires, sustainment, etc.).
     Following the rehearsal, the S-3 will publish the results and refinements to the plan in a FRAGORD.
     Refer to FM 6-0 and Collective Task 71-BDE-5122, Perform a Rehearsal and Individual Task 150-C2-
     5125, Prepare a Fragmentary Order. While executing rehearsals, the staff should refine decision support

120  TC 6-0.2  30 July 2025


---

                                         Command and Control Warfighting Function Training Tables – Brigades

     products initially developed during planning per Individual Tasks 150-C2-5138, Develop a Decision
     Support Template, and 150-C2-5139, Develop a Decision Support Matrix.

PRACTICE STAFF BATTLE DRILLS

     1-557. A battle drill is a collective action rapidly executed without applying a deliberate decision-making
     process. A battle drill or SOP rehearsal ensures that all participants understand a technique or a specific
     set of procedures. While the brigade establishes its own battle drills in the SOP, FM 6-0 has a list of
     suggested battle drills for CPs.

REFINE THE PLAN

     1-558. The commander and staff continuously refine the plan during all phases (plan, prepare, execute,
     assess) of the operations process. The commander maintains a personal situational understanding of the
     OE (commander’s estimate) through battlefield circulation, monitoring voice networks, and a variety of
     updates from the staff and subordinate leaders. The commander provides direction on key activities for
     the staff to monitor that may require additional refinement or adjustments to the plan. Refining the plan
     typically takes place when new information validates or invalidates assumptions made during planning,
     intelligence confirms or denies enemy actions, or the status of friendly forces changes as the situation
     develops. The XO leads the staff through a refinement of the plan and the mission objectives contained
     therein. The S-3 will publish approved refinements to the plan in a FRAGORD to the brigade. The XO
     will use Individual Task 150-C2-5124, Refine the Plan, and Collective Task 71-BN-5132, Perform
     Planned Actions, Sequels, and Branches.

ESTABLISH THE COMMAND POST

     1-559. A CP is the organization designed to help the commander exercise C2. Depending on the type of
     brigade, the unit will have a main CP and may have a tactical CP (TAC CP, or simply TAC), or may
     have 473 other alternate CPs. The staff is organized into functional cells and staff sections in each of the
     brigade CPs to facilitate coordination and promote efficiency. These CPs provide the brigade commander
     flexibility in arranging the brigade’s control nodes within the brigade’s assigned area.

     1-560. The commander determines the sequence of deployment, timing of moves, initial locations and
     task organization for all the brigade’s CPs based on the mission variables of METT-TC(I) and the
     commander’s visualization. The commander task-organizes functional capabilities and personnel across
     the main and other CPs to match the commander’s concept for control of operations. The brigade TAC
     and main CPs deploy to separate locations or are consolidated based on the mission variables. Each CPs
     performs functions implied by its organizational design and those tasks assigned by the commander. In
     the C2 paragraph (paragraph 5) of the brigade OPORD, the commander details any changes to the
     doctrinal authority, responsibilities, and task organization of the brigade command posts and any special
     instructions for them.

     1-561. Each brigade CP standardizes procedures associated with:
          • CP battle drills.
          • Shift-change briefings.
          • Operations update and assessment briefings.
          • Operations synchronization meetings.
          • Transferring control between CPs.

     1-562. The unit will use Collective Tasks 71-BDE-0050, Establish a Command Post. Refer to ATP 6-
     0.5.

FORMALIZE SHIFT CREWS

     1-563. Once the CPs are established and manned, the XO will formalize and recommend shift crews to
     the commander. When organizing shifts, the XO and principal staff officers must consider the duties and
     responsibilities inherent to an area of expertise or WFF required for that specific CP in relation to time,
     space, and other battlefield conditions. The commander adjusts or confirms the shift crews early in the
     C2TT so crews can gain familiarity with one another and their shift leader. The XO will ensure crews
     understand the inputs, outputs, and battle rhythm to support the commander. The XO will ensure the

30 July 2025  TC 6-0.2  121


---

Chapter 2

     formalized shifts have ample opportunity to exercise ‘reps and sets’ to build shift proficiency. Refer to
     Individual Task 150-C2-5205, Lead a Shift Change.

     1-564. Unit manning documents for most brigade-sized units are designed to provide two 12-hour shifts
     to meet the requirement for continuous operations (ATP 6-0.5). Units organize available personnel to
     provide effective continuous operations. Establishing shifts provides a sufficient quantity of personnel
     to operate the CP and the required expertise to make decisions on major issues. Units may organize shifts
     using the standard shift, heavy or light shift, and stagger shift techniques.

     1-565. Regardless of the method used, several considerations apply. The commander and key leaders
     are not placed on a duty shift. Personnel who do not work permanently in the CP are not integral parts
     of a duty shift. This includes liaison officers and any attached special staff officers who are unit leaders
     or commanders. Additionally, members of the command group and the TAC CP are not included. These
     personnel integrate into the existing manning schedules when present at the main CP for an extended
     period.

VERIFY COMMAND POST STANDARD OPERATING PROCEDURES AND FUNCTIONALITY

     1-566. Once the shifts and CPs are established, and the staff runs through the event, the section leaders
     and the XO will ensure that all SOPs are updated based on changes to the OE; AARs; lessons learned;
     the evolving needs of the commander; higher, lower, and supporting units; and the mission set.
     Commanders ensure that comprehensive and detailed SOPs are developed, maintained, trained, and used
     for all aspects of operations. In this way, operations become a matter of routine that are successfully
     executed in periods of stress and great demand. Refer to Individual Task 150-C2-5200, Conduct
     Command Post Operations, and Collective Task 71-BDE-5200, Conduct Command Post Operations.

TABLE V: CONDUCT STAFF REHEARSALS

     1-567. By conducting Table V, the XO will verify staff is prepared to achieve the commander’s
     certification in Table VI. This rehearsal incorporates all cells and processes into a single training
     exercise. This rehearsal aligns with an already scheduled collective training event in accordance with the
     unit CATS. For example, the brigade is performing their first CPX leading to an EXEVAL later in the
     year. As budget and training days allow, this table is conducted in a field or simulated field environment
     with 24-hour operations whereby each shift has an opportunity to train appropriate drills, battle rhythm
     events, and procedures. Commanders choose which collective tasks to perform. For example, the
     commander establishes that the brigade will use MET / Collective Task 55-BDE-4800, Conduct
     Expeditionary Deployment Operations at the Brigade Level, as the primary task for a deployment
     exercise (DEPEX). The commander might also select 71-BDE-5100, Conduct the Operations Process
     for Command and Control, and 55-BDE-4802 Conduct Home Station Mobilization Activities at the
     Brigade Level, as supporting collective tasks. Figure 2-15 on next paage establishes the inputs, tasks to
     be accomplished during this table, and the auditable outputs for the table.

REVIEW STAFF ORGANIZATION

     1-568. The XO and commander organized the staff in Table I-IV. In consultation with the commander,
     the XO uses this table as a last look for staff organization and operations. The rehearsal will give the XO
     time to confirm or reorganize the staff as needed based on manning and mission requirements. If changes
     are required, the XO makes those changes before the Table VI certification. The principal staff officers
     go through the same review process and make recommendations to the XO. During this table, the
     commander identifies, and the XO defines all additional duties yet to be assigned. See Individual Tasks
     150-LDR-5013, Organize the Staff for Operations; 150-C2-0000, Organize the Command and Control
     System as the Staff Officer; and Collective Task 71-BDE-5123, Task Organize for Operations.

ESTABLISH THE COMMON OPERATIONAL PICTURE

     1-569. This task is performed in unison with establishing the CP. The S-3 designates a battle captain and
     a COP manager prior to or during this event. The COP manager works for the battle captain. The COP
     manager is a person selected and designated to serve in this position due to their high degree of attention
     to detail, C2IS experience, computer skills, knowledge of FM 6-0, intelligence expertise, and experience
     working in a. The COP manager’s duties include:

          • Displaying all information required for the COP and decision-making.
          • Updating unit locations and events through the digital component of the COP.
          • Updating unit locations and events on the analog COP.
          • Coordinating with other staff sections for COP manipulation during briefings.
          • Receiving guidance from the command group on specific views or manipulations of the COP

                for situational understanding purposes.
          • Coordinating COP requirements with higher headquarters and subordinate units.

30 July 2025                                      TC 6-0.2                                                                    123


---

Chapter 2

     1-570. Both the COP manager and the battle captain work under the authority of the S-3 and are also
     part of the KM team. The C2 information integrator assists in the establishment of the COP as well. C2
     information integrators are the commander's subject matter experts regarding operation, maintenance,
     integration, and operator training for unit C2IS. The C2 information integrator duties include:

          • Integrating CP C2 network.
          • Troubleshooting C2 information systems architecture.
          • Assisting the COP manager in developing the COP.
          • Implementing a unit individual digital training program.

     1-571. While the C2 information integrator and the S-6 establish the framework and systems for the
     COP, the population of that framework and integration into the exercise is the responsibility of operators
     within the staff, under the guidance of the COP manager. The XO ensures that the staff establishes the
     COP to meet the requirements of the mission, in accordance with unit SOP and the commander’s intent.

     1-572. COP displays within a CP are prominent, recognizable, and accessible to those attempting to gain
     and maintain situational awareness. Adequate COP visibility sets the condition for effective battle
     tracking and decision making. CP personnel prioritize and emplace COP displays—both digital and
     analog—to facilitate situational awareness. See Collective Task 71-BDE-5319, Establish the Common
     Operational Picture, and Individual Task 150-C2-5315, Establish the Common Operational Picture.

CONDUCT THE OPERATIONS PROCESS

     1-573. The XO trains the staff to achieve a go in all performance measures on the T&EO for Collective
     Task 71-BDE-5100, Conduct the Operations Process for Command and Control. The staff will conduct
     the operations process fully supporting the commander and the mission. Each section will maintain
     running estimates and recommend any plan variations and changes in a timely manner to the commander.
     The staff will conduct the operations process while conducting tasks from the brigade’s standard METL,
     A- METL, or other tasks assigned by the higher headquarters or the commander.

PREPARE AN ORDER

     1-574. The brigade is required to publish an OPORD as part of the operations process. The staff must be
     proficient in conducting the MDMP, and the OPORD reveals that proficiency. The length and detail of
     the order can vary due to time and the mission. They also distribute the order to higher, adjacent,
     lower/subordinate, supporting, and supported units. The XO evaluates the OPORD against the basic
     standards established in Individual Task 150-C2-5119, Prepare an Operation Order, and Collective
     Task 71-BDE-5119, Prepare an Operation Order. Functional and multifunctional brigades may instead
     refer to T&EOs specific to their proponent when the form of their orders is doctrinally different from the
     standard OPORD format, such as 06-BDE-6057, Prepare the Field Artillery Operation Order (OPORD)
     for brigade echelon artillery units.

REACT TO A FRAGMENTARY ORDER

     1-575. At some point during the operation, the commander (or XO acting in their behalf for training
     purposes) initiates either a verbal or a written FRAGORD. This FRAGORD can be for any reason as
     determined by the commander and following the training scenario. For example, the brigade commander
     has the staff react to a FRAGORD from higher directing a transition of the force temporarily into a hasty
     defensive posture. The staff will conduct RDSP to ascertain necessary changes to the current plan and
     recommend changes to the commander within the designated timeline. See Collective Tasks 71-BDE-
     5203, Process a Fragmentary Order (FRAGORD), and 71-BDE-5002, Conduct Rapid Decision-Making
     and Synchronization Process.

FINALIZE THE BATTLE RHYTHM

     1-576. The XO and principal staff officers will use the commander’s guidance and the staff rehearsal to
     finalize the battle rhythm in the unit SOP. The XO will ensure that the battle rhythm reserves adequate
     time for the commander to participate (exercising command presence) during the operations process,
     and ample time for battlefield circulation and key leader engagements. This battle rhythm, at a minimum,
     will ensure all inputs and outputs are based on resources, the DSM/DST, mission, training objectives,

124        TC 6-0.2  30 July 2025


---

                                         Command and Control Warfighting Function Training Tables – Brigades

     delegated authorities, and the commander’s intent directly in support of decision-making and resource
     allocation. Refer to Collective Task 71-BDE-5125, Implement the Battle Rhythm.

FINALIZE DECISION SUPPORT PRODUCTS

     1-577. The DSM is an integral part of staff priorities. Here the staff refines the DSM and DST as well
     as the processes for employing and updating them. The DSM is tied to the operations process and is a
     product of the MDMP. The DSM and DST portray key decisions and potential actions that are likely to
     arise during the execution of each COA (FM 5-0, Ch. 5). The DSM is tied to resources, events, and
     decisions that the commander needs to make affecting outcomes. During the event, the staff will brief
     the commander on any changes to the DSM and DST. Delegation authorities are stipulated within the
     DSM and DST. Delegation authorities are stipulated within the DSM and DST for decisions that must
     be made in the commander’s absence. Refer to Individual Tasks 150-C2-5138, Develop a Decision
     Support Template, and 150-C2-5139, Develop a Decision Support Matrix.

EMPLOY THE COMMAND AND CONTROL SYSTEM

     1-578. The XO refines the C2 system, including:
          • Personnel – Ensures the staff is organized and all personnel are positioned appropriately
                according to job skill and the needs of the command.
          • Processes– Ensures all lessons learned to this point in the tables are captured in the unit SOP.
                All inputs and outputs from the various cells, boards, working groups, and planning teams are
                updated.
          • Networks – Ensures the servers, wires, networks and C2IS are updated, operating, and secure.
          • CPs – Ensures all equipment is maintained, reserved, and ground cleared. All command
                equipment (including vehicles) is maintained and prepared to execute the mission.

     1-579. Changes identified for retraining or adjusting during the Table V rehearsal are made prior to the
     Table VI certification. Refer to Individual Task 150-LDR-5251, Organize the Command and Control
     System.

CONDUCT COMMAND POST OPERATIONS

     1-580. The staff personnel conduct CP operations to support the commander in developing a shared
     understanding and making decisions. They execute several routine and recurring tasks to establish,
     operate, and move the CP. With the assistance of the staff, commanders develop processes and
     procedures for the execution of these tasks and capture them in the CP SOP. See ATP 6-0.5 and
     Collective Task 71-BDE-5200, Conduct Command Post Operations.

CONTROL OPERATIONS

     1-581. While executing one of the brigade METs, the staff assists the commander in controlling unit
     operations by arranging forces in time, space, and purpose to produce maximum relative combat power
     at the decisive point. During preparation phase of the operation, the staff monitors the progress of
     subordinate units’ conduct of PCIs, mission-specific training, implementation of risk controls,
     preparations to sustain themselves, and protecting themselves. Once the operation begins, the staff
     sections:

          • Advise and inform their commander.
          • Maintain running estimates.
          • Provide recommendations.
          • Prepare plans, orders, and other staff writing.
          • Assess the progress of operations against what was planned.
          • Manage information within their area of expertise.
          • Identify and analyze problems.
          • Perform risk management.
          • Exercise staff supervision over their area of expertise.

     1-582. The XO should use Collective Task 71-BDE-5103, Control Operations, to evaluate the staff.

30 July 2025  TC 6-0.2  125


---

Chapter 2

CONDUCT SENSITIVE COMPARTMENTALIZED INFORMATION FACILITY OPERATIONS
[OPTIONAL]

     1-583. Not all brigades have the personnel or equipment to establish a sensitive compartmentalized
     information facility (SCIF), but for those that do, the Special Security Office (SSO) or S-2 establishes
     controls for SCIF and tactical sensitive compartmented information facility (T-SCIF) and manages,
     ensures appropriate accreditation for, and reviews and manages emergency plans for SCIFs and T-SCIFs.
     The SSO coordinates access to the SCIF or T-SCIF and maintains records of access rosters, personnel
     clearances, and classified document registers and manages special access program (SAP) access. Refer
     to Collective Task 34-TM-3090, Manage Special Security Office Functions.

CONDUCT SECTION/CELL AFTER ACTION REVIEWS

     1-584. Staff section and cell leaders conduct internal AARs to identify adjustments to make to the SOP
     and retraining required prior to conducting certification in Table VI. Refer to 150-C2-5133, Conduct a
     Formal After Action Review for additional information.

TABLE VI: CONDUCT STAFF CERTIFICATION

     1-585. This table is conducted during a CPX or FTX (see the unit CATS). As budget and training days
     allow, this table is ideally conducted with the CPs in a field environment with 24 hour operations.
     Certification is an internally evaluated event. The externally evaluated event is Table X.

     1-586. Commanders choose which tactical tasks are to be performed. The commander certifies that the
     staff have met all the training criteria for Table VI by achieving at least a P rating in each of the tasks
     listed here. Once the staff is certified, they can proceed to unit training in Tables VII through X.

     1-587. This table is a culmination of all previous tables, and the commander can use any of the collective
     tasks from previous tables for training as necessary. Any retraining required from poor performance of
     tasks in the earlier tables should occur prior to Table VI.

     1-588. Commanders organize their C2 system to support decision making and facilitate communications.
     The XO manages the C2 system—the arrangement of people, processes, networks, and command posts
     that enable commanders to conduct operations.

     1-589. The outcome of Tables I through VI is that the staff work as a cohesive unit, led by the
     commander. Each staff section/cell and CP understands and operates within their designed capabilities
     to accomplish the mission. Certification indicates individual and section/cell competency meets Army
     standards and the commander’s intent. The staff performs the operations process seamlessly without
     outside augmentation. Figure 2-16 below establishes the inputs, tasks to be accomplished during this
     table, and the auditable outputs for the table.
