     while the remaining staff focus on establishing the CP. The command will refer to Collective Task 71-
     BDE-0050, Establish a Command Post.

PROCESS RELEVANT INFORMATION

     1-761. The XO and S-3 provide guidance on how the staff organizes to support the commander’s
     exercise of C2. The staff provides relevant information to the commander to answer information
     requirements. The digital crew will support the staff in processing relevant information in accordance
     with the standard established in Collective Task 71-BDE-5315, Process Relevant Information.

DISPLAY THE COMMON OPERATIONAL PICTURE

     1-762. The staff, led by the XO, directs the execution of the KMP and distribution to subordinate CPs to
     create and maintain shared understanding. The battle captain and COP manager update the COP to ensure
     the most relevant information is displayed while the S-3 directs the COP manager and C2 information
     integrator to post and disseminate the COP across the C2 network. The COP manager and C2 information
     integrator ensure the COP is accurately disseminated to lower echelons across the network. The
     command will reference Collective Task 71-BDE-5319, Establish the Common Operational Picture.

DISSEMINATE THE COMMON OPERATIONAL PICTURE AND EXECUTION INFORMATION

     1-763. The staff, led by the XO, directs the execution of the KMP and distribution to subordinate CPs to
     create and maintain shared understanding. The battle captain and COP manager update the COP to ensure
     the most relevant information is displayed while the S-3 directs the COP manager and C2 information
     integrator to post and disseminate the COP across the C2 network. The COP manager and C2 information
     integrator ensure the COP is accurately disseminated to lower echelons across the network. The
     command will reference Collective Task 71-BDE-5319, Establish the Common Operational Picture.

IMPLEMENT THE KNOWLEDGE MANAGEMENT PLAN

     1-764. The C2 information integrator supervises the digital crew in establishing the COP – a key
     component of the KMP. The C2 information integrator participates in refining the KMP and digital
     portions of the unit SOP. The C2 information integrator will oversee the sections and C2IS operators
     configuring their systems and equipment to conform to the information requirements established in the
     KMP. Refer to Collective Tasks 71-BDE-5330, Conduct Knowledge Management, and 71-BDE-5310,
     Manage Information and Data.

     with higher, subordinate and supporting units, and any unified action partners information requirements,
     across the WFFs, to achieve shared understanding. This table is scenario driven to achieve tactical
     proficiency. Upon completion of Table VIII, digital crews are integrated into the staff and prepared for
     a validation rehearsal exercise. Figure 2-38 on next page establishes the inputs, tasks to be accomplished
     during this table, and the auditable outputs for the table.

DEVELOP RUNNING ESTIMATES

     1-766. The command references Collective Task 71-BDE-5144, Develop a Running Estimate. The staff,

CONDUCT RAPID DECISION-MAKING AND SYNCHRONIZATION PROCESS

     1-767. The digital crew employs C2IS to conduct automated coordination with higher, adjacent,
     subordinate units, and any unified action partners to implement changes to the plan. When approved by
     the commander, they then implement these changes in the digital products. For example, the sustainment
     cell digital system operators will use their systems to coordinate changes to logistics priorities with
     supporting sustainment units. The command references Collective Task 71-BN-5002, Conduct Rapid
     Decision-Making and Synchronization Process.

CONDUCT AN OPERATIONS SYNCHRONIZATION MEETING

     1-768. The command conducts an operations synchronization meeting electronically, including all CPs
     and relevant subordinate units using Collective Task 71-BDE-5135, Conduct an Operations
     Synchronization Meeting, as a guide. Synchronization of staff efforts and priorities ensures productivity
     and greater unity of effort. The S-3, or designated representative, guides the staff through managing
     products to sustain the unity of effort. The staff provides updates to unit leaders through battle rhythm
     events such as daily briefing and staff updates to synchronize current and future events.

     of their ability to integrate the C2IS. This table simulates the conditions similar to the projected Table X
     environment. These conditions can be tailored towards a future CTC rotation or other OEs. Figure 2-38

     for the table.

ESTABLISH A COMMAND POST

     1-770. The digital crew establishes the C2 network in support of the staff and the crew setting up the CP,
     then uses it to support CP operations per unit SOP and Collective Tasks 71-BDE-0050, Establish a
     Command Post, and 71-BDE-5250, Conduct Command Post Operations.

VALIDATE THE DIGITAL STANDARD OPERATING PROCEDURE

     1-771. Under the authority of the commander, the C2 information integrator will validate the digital
     systems portion of the unit SOP using Collective Task 71-BDE-5330, Conduct Knowledge Management,
     ensuring that the critical SOP requirements are met:

          • Produce and disseminate mission orders through the C2IS.
          • Conduct battle hand-over: each CP to an alternate CP and back.
          • Execute update briefs.
          • Display statuses.
          • Track and report operations and sustainment.

ASSIST THE COMMANDER IN EXECUTING TACTICAL OPERATIONS

     1-772. The headquarters conducts the operations processes by integrating, analyzing, and synthesizing
     data to determine relevant information for action. This commander and staff process occurs internally
     and externally to the headquarters and simultaneously across the C2IS to achieve shared understanding
     at echelon and with adjacent units. The digital crew maintains a consistent and accurate COP to enable
     this process. The digital crew will complete Individual Task 150-C2-5131, Assist the Commander in
     Executing Operations, and Collective Task 71-BDE-5103, Control Operations.

SUPPORT THE COMMAND POST IN DISPLACING THE COMMAND POST

     1-773. The digital crew supports the CP during displacements by conducting battle handover for COP
     responsibilities to an alternate CP, preparing C2IS for transportation, moving to the new CP location,
     reestablishing the network, and reassuming COP management responsibilities. The digital crew follows
     the timeline established by the battle captain. Refer to Collective Task 71-BDE-5201, Displace the
     Command Post, and 71-BDE-5202, Conduct Transfer Control of Operations.

                     Command and Control Warfighting Function Training Tables – Brigades

     1-774. At the end of the training event, the C2 information integrator will conduct an informal AAR with
     the digital crew to capture final refinements to the COP and SOP and conduct any required retraining
     prior to the Validation Exercise.

TABLE X: COMMAND AND CONTROL WARFIGHTING
FUNCTION VALIDATION EXERCISE

     1-775. Commanders use observations and lessons learned from Table IX to refine planning and
     execution of Table X that serves as the brigade’s CTE. Table X execution is synchronized with other
     unit CTEs. During the training event, the digital crew and staff completes Collective Tasks 71-BDE-
     0050, Establish a Command Post, and 71-BDE-5200, Conduct Command Post Operations.

     1-776. Tables VII through IX lead to digital crews that are integrated with their supported staff who
     operate as a cohesive unit. Simultaneously, the staff and commander are synchronized with external units
     and liaisons. Each staff section and cell member understands the commander’s intent and is capable of
     leveraging their digital crew and C2IS to seamlessly support the mission. At the conclusion of Table IX,
     the commander leads an AAR, and the digital crew makes any final changes to the COP and digital
     portions of the unit SOP. Training shortfalls identified during Table IX are corrected before beginning

     1-779. Digital crew members will use their C2IS to battle track, manage the COP, execute battle drills,

                                              Appendix A

        Command and Control Training Management

    Appendix A describes the use of the operations training management process at battalion
    and brigade echelons to prioritize, plan, prepare, execute, and assess training, which is
    called Training Management. It describes how the command and control training tables
    (C2TTs) are integrated into the Long Range training Plan (LRTP) using the Combined
    Arms Training Strategies (CATS), and how trainers use the CATS to plan and prepare for
    command and control (C2) training. This appendix is derived from Chapter 3 of TC 6-0,
    which covers training management for C2 in greater detail.

  SECTION I – PLANNING C2 TRAINING

     A-1. The Army uses the operations training management process of plan, prepare, execute, and assess
     as its training framework, as described in FM 7-0. Using this framework, the unit commander remains
     central to the training management process in the same way the commander is central to the operations
     process. Informed by the Army’s Regionally Aligned Readiness and Modernization Model (known as
     ReARMM), the process begins with the Army Synchronization and Resourcing Conference (known as
     ASRC). This conference synchronizes Army activities across four lines of effort (operational
     requirements, training and exercises, modernization, and other Army service requirements). Each unit
     commander begins the training cycle with top-down training guidance from the higher commander.
     Commanders should nest C2 training tables within the context and events within this guidance using
     every opportunity to train using multi-echelon techniques to maximize time and resource efficiency.

     A-2. Senior leaders develop and communicate training guidance. Over a set period, they provide clear
     and concise guidance on what is trained, when it is trained, who is trained, why, and to what level of
     proficiency—task and purpose. Senior leaders provide a clear vision of their training expectations giving
     the unit direction, purpose, and motivation necessary to train effectively.

LONG-RANGE PLANNING AND THE UNIT TRAINING PLAN

     A-3. Long-range planning and preparation determine the training required to progress from the unit’s
     current state of training proficiency to the desired proficiency level. The unit commander conducts long-
     range planning to sequence training events with resources over time to determine who, what, when, and
     where to train. Long-range preparation ensures coordination and actions needed to secure long-lead time
     resources are accomplished prior to training. The products of the long-range planning process are a unit
     long-range training plan and a long-range training calendar.

     A-4. For Regular Army battalions, the long-range planning cycle is 12 months. For Reserve Component
     it is 2-3 years.

     A-5. In developing the unit’s long-range training plan, commanders and planners consider several key
     planning considerations. These include:

          • Higher headquarters training guidance/training requirements. Ideally, senior leaders’ guidance
                will include instruction for conducting C2 certification (Table VI) and validation (Table X) in
                conjunction with MET proficiency and collective live fire events.

          • Multi-echelon training. C2 training beyond Table V is improved when different echelons
                interact.

          • Training Events. The C2 functional CATS assist commanders in overlaying their C2TT
                objectives on scheduled training events. For some units, their unit CATS have incorporated the
                C2TT into training events to provide the commander recommendations for when to conduct
                each training table.

          • Allocate the necessary training time and resources for section, cell, and command post (CP) to
                train their elements to proficiency on C2TT I-IV separately.

30 July 2025  TC 6-0.2  167


---

Appendix A

          • Training Environments. There are three training environments—live, virtual, and constructive
                (LVC). C2 training at battalion and brigade echelons typically happens in a live or constructive
                environment.

     A-6. Staff training planners use commander’s guidance for implementing the C2TT when developing
     courses of action (COAs) for the unit training plan to overlay when each of the training audiences will
     conduct each table during proposed training. For example, training objectives for the event at which the
     unit will conduct Table VI certification should include achieving a fully trained (T) proficiency level on
     each of the tasks in every cohort’s Table VI.

     A-7. For some units, the C2TT have been built into their unit CATS with recommendations for which
     events include specific C2TT (see next paragraph for an example). Training planners for other units may
     have to use the echelon-appropriate functional CATS to make that determination themselves.

     A-8. Table A-1 below shows how the C2TT have been built into the battalion level C2 Functional
     CATS to correspond to specific events already in the battalion CATS. It shows the battalion CATS Task
     Set (TS) numbers (bold text) that correspond to each C2TT (Tables I through V for the commander have
     no associated TS). Since all four training cohorts train tables VII through X together, those tables are
     combined into one task set each in the CATS. On the bottom row it describes which training event in the
     CATS each training table is programmed against.

A-9. One important consideration for incorporating C2TT into events is the higher headquarters’ multi-
echelon training events in which the unit must participate. These events provide great opportunity for

30 July 2025                        TC 6-0.2                                            169


---

Appendix A

     C2 interactions between commanders and staffs at different echelons that meet the conditions for many
     C2 T&EO training objectives. For example, when a battalion is tasked to perform as a response cell for
     a brigade CPX, there are still opportunities to improve C2 training proficiency as parts of the staff, CP,
     and digital crew operate from inside the mission training complex

     A-10. A unit’s regional alignment or the higher commander’s annual training guidance (ACTG)
     identifies the operational environment (OE) to replicate in training. The Army’s Training and Doctrine
     Command (TRADOC) has made a number of OE and opposing forces (OPFOR) resources available to
     training planners to assist them in analyzing decisive action training environments and determining
     resources to replicate a decisive action training environment (DATE) OE on the TRADOC G-2 Exercise
     Support Application website, here: https://oe.tradoc.army.mil/esa-exercise-support-application/
     (Requires common access card (CAC)).

MID-RANGE PLANNING

     A-11. The mid-range planning horizon focuses on pre-execution planning for each training event
     identified in the LRTP. All training events are planned and executed following the Army operations
     process. During the mid-range planning horizon, training meetings and quarterly training briefings
     provide venues for the commander and unit leaders to ensure training event planning, resourcing, and
     coordination stay on track.

     A-12. For Regular Army battalions, the mid-range planning cycle is 3-4 months. For Reserve Component
     it is 12 months.

     A-13. Commanders and their training planners must understand the planning timelines for training
     resources such as ammunition, ranges, and mission training complex (MTC) support and ensure that
     requests for those resources are made an appropriate time in advance of each training event. Local SOPs
     will determine which resources must be requested during mid-range planning versus those requested
     during short-range planning.

SHORT-RANGE PLANNING

     A-14. As the date of training execution approaches, planners and leaders continue to monitor and
     coordinate actions and activities to ensure success of each training event. These final preparations ensure
     that proper coordination and resourcing has been conducted and ensures that units are prepared to
     conduct training as planned.

     A-15. For Regular Army battalions, the short-range planning cycle is 6 weeks. For Reserve Component
     it is 6 months.

     A-16. As a key part of final training event preparations, commanders aggressively protect approved and
     scheduled training from subsequent changes and unprogrammed training distracters.

     A-17. For C2 training, a key resource to coordinate during short-range planning is the higher
     headquarters operation order that sets the stage for the unit to conduct the tactical event, usually one or
     more mission essential tasks (METs) that is the primary focus of the training event. Depending on the
     echelon and complexity of the training, this order will usually be issued in advance of the training event
     so that the unit and its subordinates may conduct planning and issue their own orders before or at the
     start of the event. Even though this C2 activity may begin before the event, the commander should ensure
     the planning process and order produced are assessed or evaluated as well.

  SECTION II – PREPARING C2 TRAINING

     A-18. Preparing to train the C2 WFF uses the same basic principles as other training: train and validate
     leaders who will conduct the training and setting conditions for successful training.

170  TC 6-0.2  30 July 2025


---

                                                                                Command and Control Training Management

TRAINING AND VALIDATING LEADERS

     A-19. The Army develops leaders as part of every training event and ensures all training is led by trained
     and certified leaders. Commanders ensure they have developed and trained their subordinate leaders to
     enter the training events they will lead at higher levels of proficiency than the training audience.

     A-20. For C2 training, this principle applies typically to principal staff officers and their senior NCOs,
     battle captains, operations (SGM), COP managers, and information integrators. It may also involve other
     leaders listed in the “Leaders” statement of the standards paragraph of the training and evaluation
     outlines (T&EOs) to be trained.

SETTING CONDITIONS FOR SUCCESSFUL TRAINING

     A-21. Preparing for training involves those activities performed prior to training to improve the unit’s
     abilities to train effectively and consist primarily of pre-execution checks and rehearsals.

PRE-EXECUTION CHECKS

     A-22. Preparing for training also includes pre-execution checks, which are the informal coordination
     measures conducted prior to conducting training events and must not be confused with precombat
     checks. Some informal coordination measures for C2 training might include:

          • Reviewing training objective T&EO conditions statements to ensure the conditions are set to
                achieve the desired training outcome.

          • Verifying the Glossary (OPFOR), whether live or simulated in a command post exercise (CPX)
                by the MTC. is prepared to execute the proper OPFOR tasks as developed by TRADOC G-2 in
                accordance with the ATP 7-100 series of opposing forces tactics manuals.

          • Ensuring the MTC is prepared (physical, manning, and network aspects) to properly replicate
                simulated mission partners (any mix of subordinate, supporting, supported, higher, or flank
                units, or other partners).

          • Training areas for CPs to displace are coordinated with range control.
          • Training aids, devices, simulators, and simulations (TADSS) are deployed and operational.
          • Review the master scenario events list (MSEL) for the training event for completeness and that

                responsible parties know which events they are responsible for initiating.
          • External Evaluators are proficient in the tasks they are expected to evaluate during a unit

                EXEVAL.
          • Verify the ‘White Cell’ that will oversee the exercise is fully manned and scheduled to cover

                the event through its entirety.

TRAINING EVENT REHEARSALS

     A-23. Rehearsals are critical to the execution of any plan whether for operations or training. Preparing
     for training includes having units rehearse anticipated actions during the training event. All those
     involved in the training event conduct rehearsals to ensure understanding, synchronization, and
     preparation of tactical actions. Leaders supervise rehearsals to ensure that those responsible for the
     training are prepared to conduct efficient, organized, and effective performance-oriented training.

     A-24. For live or constructive training events with C2 training objectives, training rehearsals should
     include rehearsal of the means by which the exercise control group will provide stimulation for MSEL
     events. Such means could include the use of role player interaction with the commander or staff,
     simulations stimulating C2 information systems or replicated subordinate units reporting via tactical
     communications.

     A-25. The unit conducts a communications rehearsal for those trainers controlling the event, separate
     from the communications rehearsal for those participating in it. The communications rehearsal is
     important to ensure the scenario events are executed per the event timeline, and also for safety purposes.

30 July 2025  TC 6-0.2  171


---

Appendix A

  SECTION III – EXECUTING C2 TRAINING

     A-26. Effective C2 training occurs following detailed and coordinated planning and preparation. During
     each training event, a tactical scenario replicating the actual or anticipated OE and opposing force
     OPFOR elements drives the actions of the unit. Observers, leaders, and commanders conduct after action
     reviews during training events to provide participants valuable feedback to improve or sustain
     performance rather than letting a Soldier, section, cell, or CP keep making the same mistakes.

     A-27. Tasks and drills are rarely performed to standard on the first attempt. During a training event, units
     perform multiple iterations of tasks to ensure that they can reliably perform the task to the proficiency
     rating required by the end of the event. Leaders allocate time and other resources for retraining. Ideally
     units should complete retraining at the earliest opportunity if not immediately after they first attempt the
     task. In some cases, units may need to restart an event before moving to the next training event.

     A-28. Performing multiple iterations of a task already performed to standard can be made more difficult
     and challenging by changing the task’s conditions and leadership (e.g., having the second in command
     execute the task as the primary leader). A unit that can perform a task under increasingly complex
     conditions and in more iterations comes closer to attaining task mastery. For this reason, C2 training
     planners develop MSEL injects to confront commanders and their battle staff with increasingly
     complicated variations of task execution.

  SECTION IV – ASSESSING C2 TRAINING

     A-29. Assessing C2 training consists of evaluating task proficiency, conducting after action reviews, and
     planning retraining.

EVALUATING TASK PROFICIENCY

     A-30. Commanders determine training readiness using evaluations and assessments. An assessment is
     determination of the progress toward accomplishing a task, creating a condition, or achieving an
     objective (JP 3-0). Evaluations are based on the performance of tasks measured against an established
     standard under a given set of conditions. Evaluations are recorded using T&EOs for collective and
     individual tasks and drills. Users access T&EOs on The Army Training Network (ATN), via their CATS
     on the Army Training Management System (ATMS), and the Central Army Registry (CAR). These web
     sites are the Army’s only sources that provide the prescribed training tasks, conditions, and standards
     for all Army individual and collective tasks, and drills.

     A-31. When training Soldiers to perform individual tasks, trainers use the “Performance Steps” section
     of the T&EO to provide the soldier detailed step-by-step instruction and practice. When evaluating
     performance of individual tasks, leaders use the “Performance Measures” section of the T&EO to
     determine whether the Soldier performed those steps essential to successful task execution.

     A-32. Commanders, principal staff officers, and other leaders assess C2TT collective task proficiency.
     When assessing training proficiency to determine and report unit readiness, external evaluators use the
     standard statement, performance measures, and the objective training evaluation criteria matrix of
     T&EOs to record and report their observations of unit training. This external evaluation of C2TT task
     performance forms the basis for the higher commander’s validation of C2 system proficiency.

AFTER ACTION REVIEWS

     A-33. An after action review (AAR) is a guided analysis of an organization's performance, conducted at
     appropriate times during and at the conclusion of a training event or operation with the objective of
     improving future performance. It includes a facilitator, event participants, and other observers (FM
     7-0). The AAR is a professional discussion that requires the active participation of those being trained

     A-34. Evaluators, leaders, and commanders conduct AARs during and after training events to provide
     participants valuable feedback to improve or sustain performance. The unit collectively investigates and

172         TC 6-0.2  30 July 2025


---

                                                                                Command and Control Training Management

     records what went wrong to develop an improvement plan; it also discusses what went right to establish
     a proficiency sustainment plan.

     A-35. Leaders and evaluators integrate AARs into the conduct of the C2TTs at every opportunity. When
     conducting the individual skills development in the earliest tables, supervisors review performance with
     Soldiers as they complete individual tasks. Later, section, cell, and CP leaders conduct AARs after
     completing tasks, drills, or entire tables. For example, a battle captain in a brigade TAC may lead an
     AAR with the Soldiers in the COIC after they conduct an iteration of the drill React to Indirect Fire on
     the Command Post (07-BDE-D5148).

     A-36. Commanders conduct AARs at the conclusion of the Table VI event for the staff, CPs, and digital
     crew as part of the process for certifying those training audiences as being prepared to move on to
     collective C2 system training in Table VII. Likewise, the senior external evaluator facilitates an AAR at
     the conclusion of the Table X event to validate that the C2 system is prepared to support the commander
     in LSCO.

ASSESSING THE TRAINING EVENT

     A-37. Just as the training unit has an AAR, the commander of the unit running the training event conducts
     an AAR with an eye towards bettering the execution of the training the next time. Some things to consider
     are:

          • Were the trainers/evaluators prepared?
          • Were the right TADSS available when and where they were needed?
          • Was the training area/facility adequate to the training?
          • Were MSEL events effective in prompting the desired response from the unit?
          • Did the OPFOR conduct themselves in accordance with threat doctrine?

     A-38. Following the AAR the unit sponsoring the training should update their own SOP for planning
     and preparing for training.

30 July 2025  TC 6-0.2  173

                                           Appendix B

   Large-Scale Combat Operations and Multidomain
  Considerations for Command and Control Training

  This appendix addresses the threats to the battalion and brigade command and control (C2)
  systems posed on the modern battlefield during large-scale combat operations (LSCO) in
  a multidomain operational environment (OE) and their training implications.

SECTION I – INTRODUCTION

  B-1. The proliferation of new unmanned sensors combined with the increased persistent battlefield
  surveillance capability of electronic warfare, space, and cyber sensors combined with increased threat
  intelligence systems’ proficiency at cross-queueing sensors makes it very difficult to remain undetected
  on today’s battlefield. When combined with first person video (FPV) attack drones; modernized rocket,
  artillery, and air delivery systems and munitions gives potential enemies many options to attack the
  targets they find.

  B-2. In short, if a high value target can be found, it can be killed, and it is getting easier to find them.
  That is particularly true of command posts.

  B-3. Legacy Army command posts (CPs) as designed during the Global War on Terrorism (GWOT)
  are large, slow, or stationary, and have large visual, infrared, electronic, and noise signatures. During
  LSCO, these command posts can be easily seen by an ever-expanding array of sensors and just as easily
  struck by complementary effects throughout the depth and breadth of the battlefield. Near peer
  competitors have made attacking them a stated objective. Army CPs can and must become more flexible,
  agile, and resilient while not sacrificing effectiveness, and one of the keys to doing so is training
  command post personnel to operate in the operational environment.

                                            Command Post Survivability

          To increase survivability, commanders sought to protect their command posts by
          reducing their size, hardening them, splitting them up, camouflaging them, increasing
          their mobility, and actively defending them against all manner of threats including
          air, cyber, and electronic attack. Advancing technology has been on both sides of this
          dilemma. It has provided communications, automation, and information technology to
          compress the structure of command posts and make them more productive. However,
          technology also created a gateway into further functionality and capability that added
          size and structure counterproductive to survivability. Generally, this ebb and flow of
          effectiveness and survivability has been incremental, with actions and reactions
          reflected in doctrine, material, and design more evolutionary than revolutionary.

                LTG Milford “Beags” Beagle, BG Jason Slider, LTC Matthew Arrol, The Graveyard of
                Command Posts What Chornobaivka Should Teach Us about Command and Control

                                           in Large-Scale Combat Operations, Military Review, March 2023

SECTION II - THREATS

  B-4. This section will describe some of the threats CPs in modern operations.

  B-5. Internet-based open sources such as social media sites can provide a rapid means for civilians or
  threat actors to publish whereabouts of friendly military capabilities near them where threat intelligence
  personnel can read it. They can then cue intelligence systems to verify and refine target location to pass
  to lethal or nonlethal means to attack those targets.

  B-6. Unmanned aircraft systems (UAS), including one-way drones guided by either first person video
  or programmed coordinates with explosive warheads are rapidly proliferating in quantity and quality an

30 July 2025  TC 6-0.2  175


---

Appendix B

  include multi-spectrum censors such as visual light spectrum, infrared, and electromagnetic spectrum.
  Further, these 6434 drones can be constructed cheaply in mass using commonly available off-the-shelf
  parts.

  B-7. Our adversaries will use electromagnetic warfare (EW) resources in varying quantity, quality, or
  technical capability to conduct electromagnetic warfare support (EWS) to find friendly assets and
  electromagnetic attack (EA) to disable or disrupt them. They will also use EW to target friendly assets
  with fires. Friendly capabilities potentially affected include communications and our own EW and
  intelligence systems such as target acquisition radars

  B-8. Conventional artillery and missile systems are increasing in range, precision, munitions with on-
  board guidance or target recognition capabilities, and lethality. With the dissolution of the Intermediate
  Range Nuclear Forces Treaty, both friendly and adversary militaries are increasing the range of missiles
  and rockets and developing hypersonic munitions that are more difficult to defend.

  B-9. Both sides in the Russo-Ukraine war are using a tactic of ‘swarming’ unmanned aircraft systems,
  missiles, aerial delivery, and artillery to attempt to overwhelm opposing air defenses to successfully
  attack high payoff targets.

  B-10. The air defense threat (ground, air, and sea-based) has largely prevented both sides fighting in
  Ukraine from gaining even limited air superiority at a specified time, place, and duration. Even so both
  sides are able to execute airborne delivery of munitions against high payoff ground targets such as CPs.

  B-11. Attacking C2 nodes is a high priority for our near-peer adversaries. Doing so in recent ongoing
  conflicts has resulted in the deaths of a number of high ranking officers leaving large formations
  rudderless until those key leaders are replaced and integrated. It has also resulted in casualties among
  the soldiers that run CPs, reducing the effectiveness of those nodes.

  B-12. The proliferation of communications in the electromagnetic spectrum has rendered the use of
  misinformation, disinformation, and mal information eminently more likely and effective. These
  practices can greatly reduce a command’s understanding of their situation and hinder decision making.

  B-13. The proliferation of sensors in virtually every spectrum makes deception efforts very difficult and
  resource intensive. Establishing a decoy CP, for example, would require establishing the fake CP’s
  signature in the radio (including radar), visual, and infrared spectra, and sound frequencies.

  B-14. Bypassed forces and special operations forces operating behind the forces in the close fight have
  been a threat since the dawn of organized combat and continue to be so. They can act as sensors for
  enemy indirect and aerial delivered fires, and/or take direct action against CPs.

SECTION III – TRAINING IMPLICATIONS OF LSCO FOR C2

                                            Command Post Survivability

  Even without further modernization, the number of possible survivability approaches available to
  Commanders are significant. Selecting the appropriate methods may vary depending upon the unit’s
  modernization state, mission needs, and command preferences. U.S. Army commanders should consider
  employing the following approaches in their CP survivability training and preparations.

                       Command Post Survivability: What’s to Be Done? Mission Command Battle Lab
                                                                              (MCBL) Notice 23-713, February 2023

  B-15. The MCBL notice quoted above recommends employing the following CP survivability measures
  to balance effectiveness with survivability of CPs when training for LSCO:

        • Dispersion.
        • Mobility.
        • Redundancy.
        • Hardening.
        • Positioning.
        • Security and Protection.

176         TC 6-0.2  30 July 2025


---

Large-Scale Combat Operations and Multidomain Considerations for Command and Control Training

• Signature Management.
• Cover and Concealment.
• Staff Preparation for Attack.

B-16. Dispersion. Break the C2 system up into smaller, more dispersed that are more mobile and harder
to detect. Use cable for communications between dispersed nodes. Move personnel and equipment
unnecessary to conducting CP operations away from the CP.

B-17. Mobility. Increase frequency of CP moves. Unload equipment from vehicles only when it is
required for CP operations and return it when it is no longer necessary to decrease displacement time.
Rehearse emplacement and displacement as battle drills for the staff and CP personnel.

B-18. Redundancy. Have the means to perform essential CP functions in more than one CP so they can
be executed if the primary CP for a given function is attacked. Redundancy reduces the risk to mission
of a C2 node being attacked.

B-19. Hardening. Use existing facilities that provide protection from attack. Reinforce them when time
and the means allow.

B-20. Positioning. Conduct terrain analysis to determine positions that provide screening from visual,
infrared, radio frequency, or audio observation. Position CPs away from other potential enemy high
payoff targets such as friendly artillery, supply points, or sensors.

B-21. Security and protection. Securing and protecting the CP should focus on preventing detection of
it and countering the most likely means of attack. The various methods for doing so should be trained
any time CPs are training. Some protection measures CP personnel conduct or coordinate with others to
provide include:

     • Operations security (OPSEC) and camouflage.
     • Controlling entry to CP areas.
     • Patrolling and observation posts.
     • Managing contact with civilians to reduce exposure.
     • Counter-unmanned aircraft systems (C-UAS) and air defense.
     • Counter rockets artillery and mortars (C-RAM).
     • Placing field artillery critical friendly zone (CFZ) around the CP.
     • Counter mobility.

B-22.   Signature management (electromagnetic, visual, heat, sound, vehicle traffic signatures).
     •     GTA 11.11.001, Emission Control (EMCON) Planning Aid, provides a list of measures to be
     •     taken to reduce a CP’s electromagnetic signature. It also describes a technique developed at the
     •     National Training Center to establish EMCON status levels and measures of protection for each.
     •     Camouflage, particularly with radar-scattering camouflage netting, helps mitigate the visual
     •     and heat signatures.
           Minimizing the use of generators and placing sandbags around them mitigate noise.
     •     Having vehicles not connected to the CP (e.g., visiting commander vehicle) park away from the
     •     CP will mitigate the ability of drones or moving target sensors from to identify the CP.
           Most Army installations with a division headquarters assigned have been augmented with home
           station EW training capabilities that include contracted subject matter experts and home station
           training kits that include a spectrum analyzer. These capabilities are also available in Europe,
           Alaska, and Hawaii through the Foundry program. Seek out these experts and equipment
           through your installation’s training support system and use them during training to help with
           electromagnetic spectrum signature reduction.
           Schedule moves immediately following an event that will cause a large signature. For example,
           schedule a CP displacement to immediately follow broadcasting large files associated with a
           digital Operation Order (OPORD).
           Disperse the hosting of battle rhythm events so no single CP node can be identified as the center
           of operations.

B-23. Preparations for attack. Staffs must prepare their headquarters to remain effective during LSCO
and MDO. Preparations for attack units should develop through training include:

30 July 2025                     TC 6-0.2  177


---

Appendix B

          • Training communications personnel and command and control information system (C2IS)
                operators to recognize and report jamming and cyber security breaches.

          • Practicing transfer of control to other CPs.
          • Executing succession of command, key leader positions, and CPs.
          • Practicing reorganization.
          • Planning for technical continuity of operations.
          • Communications discipline.
          • Practicing conducting rapid decision-making and synchronization process (RDSP) at all CPs to

                mitigate plan degeneration.
          • Integrating risk management into the unit’s military decision-making process (MDMP).
          • Practicing CP displacement and emplacement as battle drills.
          • Rehearsing actions on contact during movement.

     B-24. One of the most important things that must be practiced during training, not just C2 training, is to
     mature the unit’s mission command approach to C2. Use every opportunity to build trust between the
     commander and subordinate units, commander and staff, personnel assigned to each CP, staff and
     subordinate units, and leaders and subordinates. Employ mission orders that allow staff and subordinate
     units to exercise disciplined initiative within the commander’s intent should the C2 system become
     degraded. Once units achieve proficiency at tasks, they should conduct additional iterations with key
     leaders removed, giving their seconds in charge the opportunity to lead the unit in their absence.

     B-25. The Mission Command Center of Excellence has developed the following training products to
     assist units in establishing and training a battle rhythm. They can be downloaded from the MCCOE DOT
     Products and Support page from MS Teams using Teams code 2wqy4u7.

          • Graphic Training Aid GTA 11-11-001, Emission Control Planning Aid.
          • Warfighter Training Support Package, WTSP-71-TS-BDE201 (Caspian Sea), Displace the

                Command Post for Brigade Combat Teams.
          • WTSP-71-TS-BDE201 (Europe), Displace the Command Post for BCTs.

     B-26. The TRADOC G-2 Exercise Support Application (ESA) is a hub for U.S. Army, Joint, and
     Coalition Partner Training exercises. The repository allows trainers to view, download, and share
     exercise materials. They can research exercise specifics and access entire collections or individual files
     in their original format. A simple search feature ensures swift navigation through the extensive catalogue.
     The application offers:

          • Comprehensive threat databases for Russian and Chinese threats based the ATP 7-100 series
                opposing force tactics manuals.

          • decisive action training environment (DATE) page that offers geographically accurate
                environments tailored to key regions, providing soldiers with the opportunity to refine decision-
                making abilities and tactical skills in an unclassified environment.

          • OE Applications - tools for training scenario developers, including:
                 The Information Operations Network (ION) for replicating World Wide Web training
                     stimulation.
                 OE Data Integration Network (ODIN) – a comprehensive and authoritative collection of
                     threat and operational environment data, curated for effective use in training, education,
                     and leader development.
                 Scenario development tools for events and exercise.
                 Opposing forces (OPFOR) training resources.

                                              Appendix C

 Battle Rhythm Training Considerations for Large-Scale
                          Combat Operations

    This appendix discusses considerations for training the unit battle rhythm at battalion and
    brigade echelons.

BATTLE RHYTHM TRAINING

     C-1. A battle rhythm is a cycle of command, staff, and unit activities intended to synchronize current
     and future operations (FM 6-0). The battle rhythm consists of a series of meetings, briefings, report
     requirements, and other activities. Typically, the battle rhythm for battalion and brigade sized formations
     is managed by the executive officer (XO) with help from the assigned or appointed knowledge
     management officer (KMO). In developing the unit’s battle rhythm, the XO considers:

          • Higher headquarters’ battle rhythm and report requirements.
          • Subordinate headquarters’ battle rhythm requirements.
          • The duration and intensity of the operation.
          • Integrating cells’ planning requirements.

     C-2. FM 6-0 Chapter 4, Battle Rhythm and Meetings, and Individual Training Support Package (ITSP)
     150-C2-5123 / 1.0, Establish the Battle Rhythm, go into great detail how to develop a battle rhythm and
     establish standards for the events that are part of it. Use Collective Task 71-BN (or BDE)-5125,
     Implement the Battle Rhythm, and Individual Task 150-C2-5123 Establish a Battle Rhythm to evaluate
     this process.

     C-3. For regular meetings, the unit standard operating procedure (SOP) should already assign primary
     responsibility for:

          • Who is the decision maker (for boards).
          • Who chairs the meeting.
          • Staff lead.
          • Participants.
          • Inputs (what information participants should bring or send before the meeting).
          • Outputs (what responsibilities do the participants have after the meeting).
          • Designated keeper of the minutes.

     C-4. These meeting instructions (FM 6-0) entries for battle rhythm events are often referred to as “7-
     Minute Drills” in unit SOPs. See Figure C-1 portraying sample meeting instructions for a battle update
     briefing from FM 6-0.

30 July 2025  TC 6-0.2  179


---

Appendix C

             Figure C-1. Sample Battle Update Brief Meeting Instructions from FM 6-0

     C-5. Unit SOPs should specify what reports are due from subordinate units and staff, when they’re due
     (situational or on a specific schedule), what format is required, etc.

     C-6. The battle rhythm changes during execution as operations progress. For example, early in the
     operation a commander may require a daily planning update briefing. As the situation changes, the
     commander may only require that update less frequently. As the unit gains expertise at meeting battle
     rhythm requirements during a given operation, it may be able to adjust the battle rhythm to reflect that
     proficiency by shortening meetings, reducing the number of participants, or conducting virtually instead
     of face-to-face.

     C-7. Training to execute the battle rhythm is essential to ensure the staff and command post personnel
     who execute it are familiar with the SOP prior to having to execute the battle rhythm in the field. Some
     considerations for training the battle rhythm are:

          • Conduct meetings in garrison the same way the unit SOP calls for them to be conducted during
               operations.

          • Practice battle rhythm events in garrison before exercises or deployments so the first meeting
               during the operation produces the desired outputs and isn’t used to train participants about how
               to conduct the meeting.

          • Enforce the SOP during meetings. If meetings are not achieving desired objectives, change the
               SOP so they do.

180         TC 6-0.2  30 July 2025


---

                                     Battle Rhythm Training Considerations for Large-Scale Combat Operations

          • Prepare for meetings:
                 Enforce SOP for meeting inputs.
                 Produce and distribute read-ahead package when time allows.
                 Follow-up meetings by distributing outputs and filing minutes per the knowledge
                     management plan (KMP).

          • Conduct after action reviews of battle rhythm events to improve their efficiency and
                effectiveness, or eliminate them if they become unnecessary, when time allows.

          • Sections designated responsibility for conducting events should establish criteria for
                determining if the events are achieving the desired outcomes.
                 Establish measures of performance such as are participants submitting inputs on time? Are
                     required participants missing meetings? Are outputs being produced in time for the next
                     meeting or higher headquarters reporting event they are designed to feed?
                 Establish measures of effectiveness - are outputs accomplishing what they are intended to?

BATTLE RHYTHM PACE PLAN

     C-8. Large-scale combat operations (LSCO) challenge the C2 system in a number of ways.
          • High operating tempo.
          • Enemy targeting of C2 nodes may require frequent displacements of command posts.
          • Contested and congested electromagnetic spectrum may disrupt communications.
          • Bypassed enemy forces and/or irregular forces may threaten C2 nodes.
          • Periods of radio silence.

     C-9. When faced with the challenges discussed above, the XO will be hard pressed to keep even a
     relatively simple battalion battle rhythm on track. If the main CP is forced to displace during the time
     the targeting meeting is supposed to occur, what does the unit do to make sure target nominations get
     turned in on time to not miss access to joint assets? If the administration and logistics operation center
     (ALOC) is being jammed when logistics status (LOGSTAT) reports are due, what does the unit do to
     make sure food, fuel, and ammunition get to the Soldiers and units that require it?

     C-10. Fortunately, the Army’s communicators use the primary, alternate, contingency, and emergency
     (PACE) plan as a tool that XOs can also use to ease the friction of battle rhythm interruptions. If XOs
     develop PACE plans for each item on the battle rhythm - meetings, updates, and reports – they can keep
     it on track with less frequent intervention.

     C-11. To add PACE plan elements of the SOP, XOs or the staff elements responsible for each battle
     rhythm item can add the alternate, contingency, and emergency means for executing the event/report.
     For each of the non-primary means, units should designate who is responsible for running the event,
     receiving the report, filing the report, and when necessary, the means for doing so.

     C-12. As an example, the PACE plan for a brigade targeting meeting might look like Figure C-1.

     C-13. As an example of a reporting PACE plan, the PACE plan for filing the brigade logistics status
     report (LOGSTAT) might look like figure C-2.

       Table C-2. Brigade Logistics Status Report Primary, Alternate, Contingency, and
                                              Emergency Plan Example

     C-14. For a PACE plan for the battle rhythm to work the alternate, contingency, and emergency means
     of accomplishing battle rhythm goals must be trained. Training planners can set conditions for practicing
     the battle rhythm pace plan by scheduling CP displacements during a time block that will drive
     implementing the PACE plan for a particular battle rhythm activity. They can also force unscheduled
     moves by injecting triggers in the scenario from the master scenario events list (MSEL), such as a report
     of a civilian taking and uploading pictures of a CP on a cellular telephone. They can also use simulated
     or actual disruptions of communications that render the normal means of executing a battle rhythm event
     useless at the appropriate time.

BATTLE RHYTHM TRAINING RESOURCES

     C-15. The Mission Command Center of Excellence has developed the following training products to
     assist units in establishing and training a battle rhythm.

          • Individual Task 150-C2-5205, Lead a Shift Change.
          • Collective Task 71-BN-5125, Implement the Battle Rhythm.
          • Collective Task 71-BDE-5125, Implement the Battle Rhythm.

proponent publication in parentheses after the definition.

                                                                                                                                    Glossary

  SECTION II – TERMS

*Mission Command
          (Army) The Army’s approach to command and control that empowers subordinate decision
          making and decentralized execution appropriate to the situation (ADP 6-0).

*Command and Control Training Tables
          A series of task based tables for training a unit's commander, staff, all command posts, and digital
          crews on individual and collective task training objectives with clear inputs and outputs (TC 6-0).

Training and Evaluation Outline
          A summary document that provides information on individual or collective task training
          objectives, resource requirements, and evaluation procedures (FM 7-0).
